## THÈSE POUR OBTENIR LE GRADE DE DOCTEUR DE L’INSTITUT AGRO MONTPELLIER ET DE L’UNIVERSITE DE MONTPELLIER

En Génie des procédés [SOURCE: Page_1]

École doctorale GAIA – Biodiversité, Agriculture, Alimentation, Environnement, Terre, Eau [SOURCE: Page_1]

Portée par l’UMR ITAP – Technologies et méthodes pour les agricultures de demain [SOURCE: Page_1]

ASSESSING T HE ENVIRONMENTAL PERFORMANCE OF LAND PLANNING SCENARIOS I N A CONTEXT OF CLIMATE CHANGE – APPLICATION T O IRRIGATED PERIMETERS [SOURCE: Page_1]

Présentée par Nicolas Rogy Le 14 décembre 2023 [SOURCE: Page_1]

Sous la direction d’Arnaud HELIAS Encadré par Eléonore LOISEAU [SOURCE: Page_1]

Devant le jury composé de Cécile BULLE, Professeure, Université du Québec à Montréal                 Rapporteure Wouter ACHTEN, Maître de conférence, Université libre de Bruxelles         Rapporteur Olivier THEROND, Ingénieur de recherche, INRAE                             Examinateur Aurélie METAY, Professeure, L’Institut agro Montpellier                    Examinatrice et présidente du jury Agata SFERRATORE, Experte sur l'environnement, SCP                         Invitée Arnaud HELIAS, Directeur de recherche, INRAE                               Directeur de thèse Eléonore LOISEAU, Ingénieure des Ponts, des Eaux et des Forêts, INRAE      Encadrante de la thèse [SOURCE: Page_1]

I [SOURCE: Page_2]

Financial support [SOURCE: Page_3]

This thesis was financed by the Occitanie Region, and the industrial partners (BRL, SCP, SUEZ, GRDF, Bonduelle and Ecofilae) of the Industrial Chair for Environmental and Social Sustainability Assessment “ELSA-PACT”. [SOURCE: Page_3]

II [SOURCE: Page_3]

III [SOURCE: Page_4]

Remerciements La thèse c’est bien plus qu’un apprentissage pédagogique, c’est aussi un apprentissage sur soi. Même si cette phrase sonne comme une phrase bateau qui aurait pu être dite par un vieux loup de mer, l’expérience est réelle. A l’heure où j’écris ces mots de remerciements, je me remémore la chance que j’ai eue de pouvoir faire cette thèse sur ce sujet passionnant que sont les évaluations environnementales de territoires. Un de mes plus gros apprentissages durant cette aventure est qu’il faut toujours avoir une question de recherche à laquelle répondre. C’est un phare qui permet de ne pas se perdre et ainsi de savoir quand on arrive à destination. Une fois arrivé à destination, un autre phare s’allume toujours et un autre voyage passionnant recommence. Le processus n’a pas de fin, ce qui fait que le plaisir est toujours là. Je tiens donc à remercier en premier lieu les personnes qui m’ont permis de rentrer dans le navire de la recherche. Tout d’abord, merci à Arnaud Hélias, mon directeur de thèse que j’ai pu contacter par le billet de ma chargée de cours d’Analyse de Cycle de Vie de Polytechnique Montréal. Merci d’avoir été intéressé par mon profil dès mes premiers pas en recherche méthodologique pour l’ACV lors de mon stage de fin d’études d’ingénieur. Les trois années de thèse ont aiguisé mon sens de l’ouïe, si bien que j’arrive à reconnaître à l’oreille quelles chaussures tu portes rien qu’au son de tes pas dans le couloir quand tu te diriges vers le bureau des doctorants. Ensuite, bien entendu, je remercie Eléonore Loiseau mon encadrante, que j’appelle ma 2ème directrice de thèse par abus de langage tellement on a travaillé ensemble. Je me souviens de mes premières diapositives qui ne t’avaient pas trop plus en raison de mon goût prononcé pour les couleurs crues. Grâce à toi, mes diapositives sont très belles maintenant. Si je reprends la métaphore du bateau, premièrement je dirai que tu as été une très bonne capitaine et que tu m’as bien appris à naviguer. Grâce à ton compas, j’ai toujours réussi à trouver la bonne voie à suivre. Certaines fois même, surtout dans la dernière ligne droite, tu as même dû la jouer sauveteuse en mer quand je tombais à l’eau. Pour tout cela, je te suis infiniment reconnaissant. Je voudrais remercier également les membres de mon jury : Cécile Bulle, Wouter Achten, Olivier Therond, Aurélie Metay et Agata Sferratore. J’ai énormément apprécié pouvoir discuter avec vous lors de ma soutenance de thèse, c’était vraiment un très bon moment pour moi. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Ensuite je voudrai remercier d’autres membres de l’équipage de l’équipe ELSA. Philippe Roux pour les discussions mouvementées autour des performances du MHR, qui je crois, n’est pas une équipe à suivre « sans regret » ... Merci à Charlotte Pradinaud qui a co-organisé la meilleure course d’orientation lors de l’école chercheure. Grâce à toi, j’ai appris que les courbes de niveau sur les cartes sont importantes à prendre en compte, et que ce qui a l’air d’un raccourci ne l’est pas toujours. Merci également à Thibault Salou qui m’a appris qu’il ne [SOURCE: Page_5]

IV [SOURCE: Page_5]

faut jamais s’en aller d’un bar sur une jambe. Merci à Amandine Pastor qui j’espère un jour courra le marathon de Montpellier sous la bannière des ACVites. Merci à Mélissa Cornelus pour ta bonne humeur et pour avoir bien voulu me remplacer au bureau Elsa. Merci à Baptiste de Masse et Dorian Issaly, les compères du 2ème étage avec qui il est toujours sympa de discuter et rigoler. Merci à Suzy Durand et Paola Guzman pour avoir bien voulu courir le marathon de Montpellier sous la bannière des Speedy Gonzalès après mes nombreuses sollicitations. Merci à Karine Duffosse pour ta pédagogie, ta grande sympathie et ton aide pour planifier mon voyage en Nouvelle-Zélande. Merci aux partenaires industriels de la chaire ELSA-PACT en particulier Agata Sferratore, Nicolas Géhéniau et Cécile Lovichi pour toute l’aide apportée durant ma thèse. Merci également aux chercheurs du groupe ELSA, Sandra Payen, Yannick Biard, Catherine Macombe, Romy Lynn Chaib, Angel Avadí et Anthony Benoist pour avoir pu être présent aux animations scientifiques que j’ai co-organisées. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Je n’oublie pas non plus les moussaillons comme moi. Les compagnons de voyages avec qui j’ai partagé les mêmes aventures. Merci Chloé Stanford-Clark pour toutes les bonnes rigolades et les supers moments passés ensemble, ils seraient trop longs à lister. On se les remémorera autour d’une bonne pinte à l’occasion. Grâce à toi, j’ai appris que l’humour anglais n’était pas toujours marrant pour les français mais que l’humour français était toujours marrant pour les anglais. Merci à Lazare Deteix pour les bonnes discussions et pour les bonnes crêpes. Merci à Clémence Huck pour m’avoir appris que le mont d’or n’était pas seulement un fromage, mais aussi le lieu où tu as grandi. Merci à Sibylle Duval-Dachary pour les bons moments passés à Dublin. Merci Adrien Cotil, le meilleur binôme de belote et de coinche : on gagnera un jour c’est sûr. On n’est pas nuls, c’est juste que les cartes sont toujours très mal mélangées. Merci à Paul Vandôme, Christina Orieschnig et Arturo Ramos avec qui j’ai eu la chance de partager un bureau, de bons repas, de bonnes discussions et de bonnes bières. Merci Maxime Dumont, Daniel Pasquel, Baptiste Oger, Yulin Zhang, Eva Fornieles et Bayu Suseno pour avoir été les meilleurs voisins de bureau et partenaires de course. C’était toujours agréable de parler de sujets de recherche en dehors de ma spécialité. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Merci également aux autres collègues du 1er étage du bâtiment 21. Merci Franck Dupré et Cédric Guillemin pour le courage dont vous avez fait preuve pour participer au relais du marathon de Montpellier. Merci Kevin Daudin et Gilles Belaud pour les discussions passionnantes sur l’hydrologie et pour tout l’intérêt que vous avez porté à mon sujet de thèse. Merci Bruno Tisseyre, Simon Moinard, Victoria Ruiz, Arnaud Ducanchez, Aurélien Richa, Romain Girardot, Léo Pichon, Guilhem Brunel, Jean-Philippe Gras, Basile Ploteau, Sarah Djafour, Thomas Crestey et Yoann Valoo pour avoir été toujours là pour discuter de sujets très variés. Merci Laure Haon et Laeticia De Volder pour votre bonne humeur tous les matins. [SOURCE: Page_6]

V [SOURCE: Page_6]

J’ai également des pensées très affectueuses pour mes amis de Chimie ParisTech, de Polytechnique Montréal et de Malakoff qui étaient toujours intéressés pour discuter avec moi. Je tiens aussi et surtout à remercier ma famille qui a été d’un soutien sans faille depuis le 6 décembre 1996 et sans qui il aurait été très difficile d’arriver au bout de cette aventure. Merci Alexandre et Pierre mes osties de frères adorés. Merci papa et maman pour avoir toujours cru en moi. Merci papi et mamie Schaeffer et papi et mamie Rogy pour votre grand soutien et votre grand intérêt pour ma thèse. Merci Emmanuel et Geneviève de vous être déplacés pour ma soutenance et d’avoir pris des notes. Merci aussi à Martine, Andréas, Elise, Loucas, Nicolas et Corinne pour avoir bien voulu m’accepter dans votre famille. Et bien sûr, un grand merci à Caroline, ma meilleure amie et fiancée, pour tous le soutien pendant cette thèse. Je suis très content de pouvoir partir en Nouvelle-Zélande avec toi et de pouvoir partager avec toi toutes les aventures qui nous attendent dans l’avenir. [SOURCE: Page_7]

12 [SOURCE: Page_20]

Abstract Territorial Life Cycle Assessment (T-LCA) is an adaptation of the conventional LCA framework, allowing to assess the environmental performance of a territory and an associated land planning scenario while considering its multifunctionality. For that purpose, eco-efficiency metrics, i.e. ratios of the services provided by the territory divided by its environmental impacts, are computed. To date, this concept remains theoretical, with no practical applications. In addition, T-LCA is a static approach, i.e. the scenario eco-efficiencies are assessed for a given year without considering potential disruptions. However, environmental changes such as climate change can affect the environmental performance of a system over time. The question of this PhD thesis is therefore: To what extent does climate change affect the environmental performance of long-term land planning scenarios for agricultural territories? At first, the adequacy of the T-LCA framework and the concept of eco-efficiency for comparing the environmental performance of land planning scenarios is analyzed through a case study comparing irrigated and rainfed scenarios. Second, methodological developments are proposed to consider the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios. The core of the methodological approach is based on a coupling between biophysical models, i.e. a crop model and a hydrological model, and T-LCA. The methodological developments are applied on agricultural territories to assess the environmental performance of land planning scenarios with or without irrigation in contrasted territorial contexts. Finally, a methodology based on the design of experiment was built to explore how the environmental performance of a territory can be maintained over time in a climate change context, according to the crops cultivated and the presence or absence of irrigation. The main results highlight the importance of considering climate change when assessing the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios. At the end, the interest and applicability of the developments are particularly important to identify “no- regrets” land planning scenarios over the long term. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Keywords: Territorial Life Cycle Assessment, adaptation, climate change, biophysical model, prospective, agriculture, irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_21]

13 [SOURCE: Page_21]

Résumé L'analyse du cycle de vie territoriale (ACV-T) est une adaptation du cadre conventionnel de l'ACV, qui permet d'évaluer la performance environnementale d'un territoire et d'un scénario d'aménagement associé, tout en tenant compte de sa multifonctionnalité. Dans ce but, des métriques d'éco-efficience, i.e. ratios des services fournis par le territoire avec ses impacts environnementaux, sont calculés. À ce jour, ce concept reste théorique et n'a pas d'applications pratiques. En outre, l’ACV-T est une approche statique, c'est-à-dire que les éco- efficiences des scénarios sont évaluées pour une année donnée sans tenir compte des perturbations potentielles. Cependant, les changements environnementaux tels que le changement climatique peuvent affecter la performance environnementale d'un système au fil du temps. La principale question de recherche de cette thèse est donc la suivante : Dans quelle mesure le changement climatique affecte-t-il la performance environnementale des scénarios d'aménagement à long terme pour les territoires agricoles ? Dans un premier temps, l'adéquation du cadre de l’ACV-T et du concept d'éco-efficience pour comparer la performance environnementale des scénarios d'aménagement est analysée à travers une étude de cas comparant des scénarios irrigués et pluviaux. Deuxièmement, des développements méthodologiques sont proposés pour prendre en compte les effets du changement climatique sur la performance environnementale des scénarios d'aménagement. Le cœur de l'approche méthodologique est basé sur un couplage entre des modèles biophysiques, c'est-à-dire un modèle de culture et un modèle hydrologique, et l’ACV-T. Les développements méthodologiques sont appliqués à des territoires agricoles pour évaluer la performance environnementale de scénarios d'aménagement avec ou sans irrigation dans des contextes territoriaux contrastés. Enfin, une méthodologie basée sur le plan d'expérience a été construite pour explorer comment la performance environnementale d'un territoire peut être maintenue dans le temps dans un contexte de changement climatique, en fonction des cultures et de la présence ou de l'absence d'irrigation. Les principaux résultats soulignent l'importance de la prise en compte du changement climatique dans l'évaluation de la performance environnementale des scénarios d'aménagement des territoires agricoles. Enfin, l'intérêt et l'applicabilité des développements sont particulièrement importants pour identifier des scénarios d'aménagement "sans regret" sur le long terme. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Mots-clés: Analyse du cycle de vie territoriale, adaptation, changement climatique, modèle biophysique, prospectif, agriculture, irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_22]

### 1. Agricultural land planning in the face of climate change

The challenges of agricultural systems [SOURCE: Page_26]

Agriculture is essential to meet one of humanity's essential needs, food provision. There will be 9.7 billion of people to feed in 2050, and up to 223 million hectares of cultivated land on top of the 1.5 billion hectares of 2010 would be needed to satisfy this rising demand (Regnier and Bozino, 2023). While facing increased demand for agricultural products, production systems must reduce their impacts on the environment (de Lattre-Gasquet et al., 2016). Agriculture, including forestry and other land uses (e.g. grasslands or wetlands as well as the conversion of both forest lands and grasslands to cropland or pasture) is the third largest emitting sector globally in terms of greenhouse gases (GHG) emissions, behind the industry, and the electricity and heat sectors, with 22% of the total burden (Dhakal et al., 2023). In addition to their significant contribution to climate change, agricultural systems are major sources of pollution in aquatic and terrestrial ecosystems, due to the large amount of nitrogen and phosphorus they release into the environment (Rockström et al., 2020). The agricultural sector also accounts for 70% of the world's water withdrawals (FAO, 2011). The expansion of agricultural land areas to the detriment of natural ecosystems with the intensification of farming practices, are major drivers of biodiversity loss (IPBES, 2019). The impacts of agricultural systems are projected to rise in order to feed the growing population, e.g. around a 50% increase in the amount of nitrogen and phosphorus applied and a 87% increase of GHG emissions between 2010 and 2050, if no mitigation measures are adopted (Springmann et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_26]

On top of being at the source of these environmental changes, agricultural systems are also highly vulnerable to them (Rockström et al., 2020). More specifically, cultivated areas are particularly threatened by climate change. Depending on the future anthropogenic GHG emissions, global warming at the end of the 21st century, i.e. the increase of global temperature with respect to the preindustrial level, will be between 1.4 and 4.4 °C (IPCC, 2021a). Climate change would also result in change of precipitation between -40% and 40% and extreme events such as heatwaves, heavy precipitations or droughts, will increase in frequency and in intensity. [SOURCE: Page_26]

The effects of both CO$_2$ concentration and climate change on cultivated areas are described in a non-exhaustive manner in Figure 1, based on the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) special report on climate change and land (Mbow et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_26]

18 [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure 1 Potential effects of climate change on cultivated areas [SOURCE: Page_27]

The climate change context affects cultivated areas by modifying crop yield, crop phenology, crop metabolism and crop quality, i.e. reduced nutritional content of the crop. In particular, increased temperature will alter the phenological stages of crops (Liu et al., 2018), for example anthesis or crop maturity (Abbas et al., 2017). The accelerated phenological development of crops caused by rising temperatures can reduce crop yields due to a shorter radiation interception period (Craufurd and Wheeler, 2009). In addition, shifts in the growing season, such as earlier anthesis, can lead to a mismatch between crop flowering and the emergence of pollinating insects, resulting in lower crop yields due to reduced pollination (Settele et al., 2016). Rising temperatures will also increase population growth of pests and their metabolic rates in temperate zones, leading to increased yield losses (Deutsch et al., 2018). Decreased precipitation can lead to water stress and therefore reduced yields (Zhu et al., 2021). Extreme events such as droughts or heat waves can reduce crop yields, particularly when they occur during the reproductive stages of crops (Innes et al., 2015). Increasing the amount of CO$_2$ in the atmosphere can improve photosynthetic rates and yields of C$_3$ crops (Makowski et al., 2020) such as wheat, while it has limited effects on C$_4$ crops (Makowski et al., 2020), such as maize. This is because photosynthesis in C$_3$ crops (Fitzgerald et al., 2016) is not saturated with CO$_2$, unlike in C$_4$ crops (Vanuytrecht et al., 2011). On the contrary it can decrease their transpiration (Rashid et al., 2019) and quality (Myers et al., 2014). As climate change is not uniform across the globe its effects cultivated areas will be specific to each location and crop. [SOURCE: Page_27]

19 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Mitigation and adaptation solutions to climate change depend on local socio-economic, environmental and cultural contexts, as well as on the types and management of agroecosystems (Mbow et al., 2019). As a result, the dual challenge of reducing the environmental impacts of agricultural systems while adapting them to climate change (Whitfield et al., 2018) needs to be addressed at local planning level. The relevance of a territorial approach to address environmental concerns about agricultural areas has been increasingly recognized (Payraudeau and Van Der Werf, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_28]

Defining environmentally sustainable strategies for adapting agricultural territories to climate change [SOURCE: Page_28]

Adaptation to climate change is “the process of adjustment to actual or expected climate and its effects, in order to moderate harm or exploit beneficial opportunities” (IPCC, 2019). Adaptation of agricultural areas can be either incremental or transformational (Mbow et al., 2019). Incremental adaptation aims to adapt agricultural areas to climate change, while maintaining current agricultural systems (IPCC, 2019), e.g. change in crop variety or setting up irrigation for crops that were previously rainfed (Mbow et al., 2019). On the contrary, transformational adaptation aims at transforming the current agricultural systems in order to adapt to climate change (IPCC, 2019). The latter can be done by changing the crop usually cultivated in a location or by abandoning monoculture (Mbow et al., 2019; Vermeulen et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_28]

One of the main incremental adaptations being studied is securing crop water supply through irrigation at the local level (Belmin et al., 2023). To this end, infrastructures can be implemented (Piao et al., 2010), which can depend on different water resources, such as groundwater (Boretti and Rosa, 2019), surface water (IEA, 2016), salted water (Martínez-Alvarez et al., 2016) or wastewater (UNESCO, 2017). In addition, water can be sourced locally, e.g. the water stored in a reservoir (Lardy et al., 2016), or imported, e.g. with an inter-basin water transfers. These projects may themselves generate significant environmental impacts, and it is necessary to carry out ex-ante assessments to produce information that will enable identification of “no-regrets” scenarios, i.e. those that do not generate pollution transfers compared to the baseline scenario. [SOURCE: Page_28]

Strategic Environmental Assessments (SEA), i.e. “systematic decision support process[es], aiming to ensure that environmental and possibly other sustainability aspects are considered (Fischer, 2007)”, are mandatory in many countries for planning projects, such as irrigation infrastructures likely to generate significant environmental impacts (Bidstrup et al., 2015; Hedo and Bina, 1999). [SOURCE: Page_28]

20 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Various tools and methods can be used to carry out these environmental assessments at the territorial level (Loiseau et al. 2012). Among them, Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) is one of the most widely recognized (Björklund and Finnveden, 2007; Niero et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_29]

### 2. Life cycle assessment: towards a comprehensive environmental assessment of agriculture land planning scenarios

The LCA framework [SOURCE: Page_29]

LCA is a well-established, science-based and standardized tool (ISO, 2020a, 2020b) to quantify the environmental impacts of a product or service. LCA takes into account the entire life cycle of the studied system, from the extraction of raw materials to its end of life. Furthermore, LCA is a multicriteria approach covering a large range of environmental issues (e.g. climate change, water use, eutrophication, toxicity, …). To this end, LCA models the interactions between the technosphere (i.e., all human-made systems) and the ecosphere (i.e., all living and non-living components of the biophysical environment) in the form of elementary flows (i.e. consumptions of raw natural resources or emissions of pollutants) (Hauschild et al., 2017). The life cycle perspective and multicriteria approach of LCA make it possible to identify potential burden shifting from a product life cycle stage to another one, from a geographical location to another one, or from an impact category to another one (Finnveden et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_29]

As shown in Figure 2 and according to ISO 14040, the LCA framework consists in four phases, i.e. i) Goal and scope definition, ii) Life Cycle Inventory (LCI), iii) Life Cycle Impact Assessment (LCIA) and iv) Interpretation (ISO, 2020a). [SOURCE: Page_29]

Figure 2 The LCA standardized framework (ISO, 2020a) [SOURCE: Page_29]

In phase 1, the purpose of the study is defined as well as the target audience. According to the goal of the study, the functional unit (FU), i.e. the quantification of the service provided by the [SOURCE: Page_29]

21 [SOURCE: Page_29]

studied system, is determined. This will enable the environmental impacts of the studied system to be compared with other alternatives fulfilling the same function, as impacts are expressed in relation to the same reference. Secondly, system boundaries are set, i.e. its geographical and temporal limits, as well as the human activities to be included and excluded. In the LCI phase, elementary flows are collected in relation to the system boundaries. In the third phase, these elementary flows are translated into potential environmental impacts. To this end, characterization factors (CFs), i.e. impact indicators for a unit of elementary flows, are used. They are computed based on scientific modeling of environmental mechanisms that follow cause-effect chains (or impact pathways) starting from elementary flows and ending with impact categories (Hauschild et al., 2017). As shown in Figure 3, the impact indicators can either be at the midpoint level, i.e. at an intermediate level of the impact pathway, or at the damage level, i.e. at the end of the impact pathway, aggregated into three areas of protection, i.e. i) Human health, ii) Ecosystem quality and iii) Natural resources. As optional step the impacts can be normalized, weighted then aggregated in order to compute a single score (Verones et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_30]

Finally, in the Interpretation phase, the results are studied in detail in relation to the goal and scope of the study. Uncertainty and sensitivity analysis can also be performed to complete the assessment. [SOURCE: Page_30]

Figure 3 Overview of the links between elementary flows, impact categories at the midpoint and damage level. The impact categories at the damage level can be aggregated into three areas of protection (Verones et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_30]

22 [SOURCE: Page_30]

From product-oriented LCAs to territorial LCAs [SOURCE: Page_31]

LCA has been widely applied to quantify the environmental impacts of agricultural products (Sala et al., 2017). It has also been use to assess the impacts of irrigation infrastructures, i.e. water transfers (Muñoz et al., 2010), reservoirs (Ghimire et al., 2014), groundwater pumping (Pradeleix et al., 2015), and desalination plants (Raluy et al., 2005a). Few LCA studies evaluate a planning project in its entirety, from the implementation of the irrigation infrastructure to the consideration of water use for agriculture. For example, Pradeleix et al. (2020) studied different types of groundwater pumping techniques for supplying water to various crops in the Kairouan region of Tunisia. Canaj et al. (2021) compared groundwater pumping and mixing tertiary effluents from wastewater plant with groundwater, for grape irrigation in the Apulia region of Italy. [SOURCE: Page_31]

One of the main limitations of LCA is that it was initially designed to assess systems on products and services. To address this issue, Loiseau et al. (2013) proposed to adapt the LCA framework to assess the environmental impacts of a territory as a whole. A territory can be defined as an interface between a geographical area and a group of stakeholders who develop, manage and use it (Moine, 2006). One of the main adaptation relies on the handling of territorial multifunctionality, as territories provide a wide range of functions such as economic, social or environmental functions. The starting point of territorial LCA (T-LCA) is no longer the definition of the functional unit. It is rather to define a given territory and an associated land planning scenario, then to select an appropriate basket of services that they provide. At the end, two types of indicators are quantified, i.e. the environmental impacts of the land planning scenario and the related services provided. These two types of indicators are combined into a single metric, the eco-efficiency, which is the ratio of the services provided by the territory divided by its environmental impacts (Seppälä et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_31]

The T-LCA framework can be applied to perform environmental diagnoses of a land planning scenario in order to identify the main environmental issues and impacting activities on a given territory. Loiseau et al. (2014) assessed the environmental impacts of a whole territory in Southern France with a wide range of human activities including agriculture. Specific applications of T-LCA for agricultural territories have also been performed. In these cases, an agricultural territory stands for a delimited area in which the majority of land use or economic activity is based on agriculture (Nitschelm et al., 2016). Jouini et al. (2019) proposed to combine T-LCA with a participatory approach with local stakeholders to define the territorial boundaries and functions, as well as to build the LCI and interpret a T-LCA study in central Tunisia. This participatory approach created good conditions for social learning, empowered the local stakeholders in their own environmental assessment and was thus adopted by the [SOURCE: Page_31]

23 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Tunisian gouvernement in order to monitor development projects. Avadí et al. (2016) developed a methodology in order to compute the impacts of an agricultural territory based on a limited amount of data. This methodology is based on the extrapolation of the impacts of a sample of farms that are representative of the studied system. This method was applied to assess the impacts of a French region. Nitschelm et al. (2016) proposed methodological developments to use spatial differentiation in both the LCI and the LCIA phases (i.e., the emissions and the receiving environment are regionalized). Spatial differentiation of both LCI and LCIA allows increasing the representativeness of the environmental assessment, as spatial variability related to agricultural practices and biophysical conditions are taken into account. Spatial differentiation was also performed by Ding et al. (2020), who computed spatialized LCI for maize in the Walloon region of Belgium. They used database at the level of the Belgian provinces and a geographic information system (GIS) in order to spatialize the yields, the amounts of fertilizers and water supplied for irrigation, the emissions linked to fertilization (e.g. NH$_3$) into the environment, as well as the transport distance between the producers of fertilizers and pesticides and the agricultural fields. For the emissions linked to fertilization, they used the simple models of Durlinger et al. (2017), which rely solely on the amount of fertilizer supplied and some emission factors. Spatializing the LCI allowed identifying the farms contributing the more to the environmental impacts. [SOURCE: Page_32]

In addition to territorial diagnoses, the T-LCA framework can also be used to compare the environmental performance of land planning scenarios. Borghino et al., (2021) compared three scenarios for an agricultural territory in France, i.e. business as usual, development of biogas production as well as sheep grazing reintroduction. This study took into account territorial multifunctionality by using two functional units, i.e. unit area and biomass produced. These scenarios have been developed in consultation with local stakeholders to better take into account the features of the territory under study. Ding & Achten (2022) performed a T-LCA, while taking into account farmers’ behavior in response to subsidies promoting bioenergy on land currently occupied by livestock production in order to reduce the environmental impacts in different parts of the Walloon region. In addition, to the subsidies, they included in the modeling demonstration farms presenting an unknown bioenergy crop to farmers. This allowed studying the adoption of a new crop by farmers. To study these behaviors, they coupled the T- LCA framework with agent-based modeling (ABM), i.e. model allowing taking into account behavioral dynamics on both temporal and spatial scales (Wu et al., 2017). The environmental impacts due to farmers’ behavior are computed over 10 years, making this study the only T- LCA considering temporal dynamics in the LCI. They concluded that the evolution of environmental impacts differed depending on where the subsidies were granted and where the demonstration farms were set up. In fact, in the south of study region, the impacts decreased [SOURCE: Page_32]

24 [SOURCE: Page_32]

more rapidly than in the north, because the adoption of bioenergy crops was more widespread there. This is because the farms in the south were closer to the demonstration farms than those in the north, which means that bioenergy crops were adopted more quickly. [SOURCE: Page_33]

Finally, the reviewed studies focused their methodological developments on the consideration of spatial variability in the T-LCA, thus showing the importance of accounting for the spatial context. However, the studies do not compute territorial eco-efficiencies, which impedes the comparison of land planning scenarios providing different baskets of services. In addition, recent works showed the interest of considering temporal dynamics in T-LCA. However, they focused only on considering behavioral effects when defining land planning scenarios. Indeed, apart from these behavioral effects driven by socio-economic developments, changes in the environment, such as climate change, can also affect the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural territories. [SOURCE: Page_33]

The challenges facing LCA in assessing adaptation strategies to climate change in agricultural territories [SOURCE: Page_33]

A few LCA studies have considered the effects of climate change on the environmental impacts of agricultural systems. To this end, prospective LCAs were performed (the evaluated system is modeled in the future compared to the year of the study (Arvidsson et al., 2023)) and the effects of climate change were considered on the LCI, including on crop yields. Niero et al. (2015) calculated the impacts of spring barley in Denmark in the mid-21st century for various climate scenarios of the IPCC. The future yields were obtained through a phytotron, an experimental device mimicking the effects of climate change. This study showed that the effects of climate change were reducing barley yields and therefore increasing the related impact compared to when barley is grown under current climatic conditions. They showed that genetic improvement of cultivars, i.e. cultivars with higher yields and better nitrogen uptake, is an effective adaptation strategy in the face of climate change, as the impacts can be lower than when non-genetically improved crops are grown in a climate change context. Viveros Santos et al. (2022) used linear regressions to project the yields of two vineyards in France for four different periods, i.e. 2021-2040, 2041-2060, 2061-2080 and 2081-2100, and for two Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSPs) scenarios (IPCC, 2021a), i.e. SSP$_1$-2.6 and SSP$_5$- 8.5. This allowed assessing their environmental impacts in a context of climate change. In that study, extreme events, e.g. droughts or hailstorms, were also taken into account and irrigation was studied as an adaptation solution in order to avoid yield decrease. They concluded that the additional impacts of the adaptation solution were compensated by the avoidance of yield losses. Hence, in a climate change context, agricultural land planning scenarios considering adaptation solutions can have lower impacts than scenarios without adaptation. In other LCA [SOURCE: Page_33]

25 [SOURCE: Page_33]

studies, various crop models have been used, i.e. models simulating crop growth as a function of the climate, soil and agricultural practices, in order to project future crop yields (El Chami and Daccache, 2015; Tendall and Gaillard, 2015). These studies showed that the effects of climate change can affect the environmental impacts of cultivated areas depending on the spatial context and the adaptation solution. [SOURCE: Page_34]

This research highlights the importance of taking into account the effects of climate change when studying the environmental impact of agricultural systems, in order to identify no-regrets adaptation solutions. However, existing studies remain limited to the scale of a single crop or farm. Integrating the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios on the scale of a territory as a whole including other infrastructure would give stakeholders objective elements for defining scenarios that provide maximum service while limiting environmental impacts, by mobilizing the T-LCA framework. In addition, it is important to consider spatio-temporal dynamics in these assessments, in order to analyze the variability of the environmental performance of land planning scenarios in space and time. [SOURCE: Page_34]

### 3. Research questions and thesis structure

The goal of this PhD thesis is to develop a methodology that allows identifying no-regrets land planning scenarios when implementing adaptation strategies for agriculture in a context of climate change. [SOURCE: Page_34]

The main research question of this thesis was formulated as follows: [SOURCE: Page_34]

To what extent does climate change affect the environmental performance of long-term land planning scenarios for agricultural territories? [SOURCE: Page_34]

To address the main research question, three sub-questions were developed: [SOURCE: Page_34]

➢ Sub-question 1: Are the methodological framework of T-LCA and the concept of eco- efficiency adequate for assessing the environmental performance of land planning scenarios including irrigation infrastructures for agricultural territories? ➢ Sub-question 2: How to take into account the effects of climate change on the environmental     performance      of   land    planning   scenarios   including   irrigation infrastructures for agricultural territories? ➢ Sub-question 3: How to maintain the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural territories over the long-term in a context of climate change? [SOURCE: Page_34]

All the methodological developments in this thesis have been tested on a theoretical case study: an agricultural perimeter with or without irrigation. Depending on the sub-question, the [SOURCE: Page_34]

26 [SOURCE: Page_34]

irrigation of the agricultural perimeter can either be done with an inter-basin water transfer (IBWT), or with an agricultural reservoir (AR). This theoretical case study was used to carry out the various proofs of concept required to answer the different sub-questions of this PhD thesis. [SOURCE: Page_35]

The structure of the thesis is described in Figure 4. After the general introduction of the thesis in Chapter I, each of the sub-question will be addressed in the Chapters II-V, which are scientific publications that are either published or ready to be submitted. In Chapter II, related to the sub-question 1, the environmental performance of three land planning scenarios for an agricultural territory in Southern France is assessed. These scenarios are (1) a rainfed cultivated area, (2) a cultivated area irrigated with an IBWT and (3) a cultivated area irrigated with an AR. The interest of the T-LCA framework and the concept of eco-efficiency for assessing the performance of land planning scenarios has been proven. However, the effects of climate change on the performance of the land planning scenarios were not taken into account. To tackle this scientific gap, sub-question 2 is addressed through methodological developments of the T-LCA framework in two different chapters. The core of the methodology is based on a coupling between T-LCA and biophysical models (models simulating biological, e.g. crop growth, and physical processes, e.g. runoff (Keating et al., 2003)), i.e. a crop model and a hydrological model. The methodological developments are applied on six contrasted case studies over the world, i.e. in Australia, China, France, India, Russia and in the USA. Firstly, in Chapter III, a dynamic and spatialized methodology is built to compare the environmental performance of two land planning scenarios over the long-term. The two studied land planning scenarios are (1) a rainfed cultivated area and (2) a cultivated area irrigated without water constraints with an IBWT. A first coupling between T-LCA and a crop model is proposed to account for the effects of climate change on crop yields and water needs. Secondly, in Chapter IV, the methodology of the precedent chapter is upgraded by adding a hydrological model to the coupling in order to take into account the effects of climate change on the local availability of surface water. The environmental performance of two land planning scenarios was compared: (1) a rainfed cultivated area and (2) a cultivated area irrigated with an AR storing local surface water resources. Chapter V addressed sub-question 3. To this end, the design of experiment methodology was used in order to explore possible land planning scenarios that maintain the environmental performance of the territory over time, i.e. irrigated or rainfed areas occupied by combinations of wheat, maize, dry beans and sunflower, for an agricultural territory in Southern France. In that chapter, the irrigation structure is an IBWT. Finally, Chapter VI is the general discussion of the PhD thesis and Chapter VII is the conclusion. [SOURCE: Page_35]

### 1. Introduction

Agriculture is essential for humanity to meet its food requirements and employs more than a quarter of the world's population (The World Bank, 2021). However, it generates multiple impacts on the environment. Agricultural practices and soil occupation release between 15% and 24% of global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions (Vermeulen et al., 2012). On one hand, agriculture is by far the primary consumer of water in the world, reaching 70%-80% of the total consumption in the arid and semi-arid zones (Fereres and Rabanales, 2007), and is one of the main drivers of biodiversity loss, ecosystem destruction and freshwater pollution (Rockström et al., 2020). On the other hand, agriculture faces challenges posed by worldwide issues such as climate change. This global change is expected to induce rises in temperature and atmospheric carbon dioxide      (CO$_2$) concentrations, precipitation changes, more frequent occurrences of pests and diseases and of extreme heat and drought, which in turn should affect crop yields and nutritional quality (Lobell and Gourdji, 2012). Hence, due to the global changes induced by human activity and a growing world population, food security is becoming one of the challenges of the coming century and adaptation solutions must be found (Vermeulen et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_39]

Irrigation contributes to increasing agricultural yields in dryland areas and is one of the main adaptation strategies implemented for agriculture in the face of climate change, as it makes it possible to secure production yields in the face of increasing drought and temperature (Mbow et al., 2019). Large-scale planning projects can be implemented to secure the water supply of agricultural territories such as water transfers (Piao et al., 2010) or reservoirs (Gorguner and Kavvas, 2020). [SOURCE: Page_39]

These hydraulic infrastructures have a long lifetime, lasting around 50 years (Raluy et al., 2005b). Therefore an ex ante environmental assessment is required to support local decision- making as well as assist territorial planners for the selection of the least impactful alternative for the environment. Indeed, territorial planners require a help-to-decision tool in order to take decisions without regretting the construction of long-lasting infrastructures that present high economical costs and environmental impacts. This assessment should take into account multiple categories of environmental impacts and a lifecycle perspective to avoid pollution transfers within the water-energy nexus (Sharif et al., 2019). Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) is a well-established and recognized methodology to be applied for quantifying the environmental performance of products and services (ISO, 2020a, 2020b). It is a multi-criteria environmental impact assessment method used to quantify the potential impacts of the life cycles of human activities on ecosystems, mineral and fossil resources as well as on human health. [SOURCE: Page_39]

31 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Several studies have used LCA to compare the environmental impact of a variety of hydraulic structures (Byrne et al., 2017) such as water transfers (Muñoz et al., 2010), reservoirs (Ghimire et al., 2014), groundwater pumping (Pradeleix et al., 2015), water reuse (Maeseele and Roux, 2021)   and desalinization plants (Raluy et al., 2005a). However, these studies calculate impacts for a functional unit of 1 m^3 of water delivered to the end-user. The boundaries stop at the water supply gate. Therefore, they do not grasp the entire range of services provided by irrigation, such as the territorial socio-economic benefits resulting from agricultural yield conservation. These limitations are inherent of the LCA framework which is a product oriented method at a microscale and do not allow for the full integration of the territorial context, and multifunctionality (Loiseau et al., 2018). The choice of water source as well as the functional unit for water supply systems have been found to greatly contribute to the variability of the water supply impacts (Hospido et al., 2012). Moreover, the impacts of hydraulic infrastructures vary according to certain physical parameters such as lifetimes or length of the infrastructures, annual quantity of water supplied and energy consumption (Raluy et al., 2005b). Consequently, it is also paramount to consider the variability in the design of the hydraulic infrastructure parameters in order to select a specific land planning of water supply. [SOURCE: Page_40]

These limitations can be overcome by using Territorial LCA (T-LCA), an adaptation of the conventional LCA framework, to assess the performance of a territory at a mesoscale and an associated land planning scenario while considering its multifunctionality (e.g. economic, social or environmental land use functions) (Loiseau et al., 2013). Compared to a conventional LCA, the starting point of territorial LCA is no longer the definition of a main function for the studied systems, but the definition of the boundaries of a territory, and associated planning scenarios. Subsequently, two types of indicators must be quantified for each of these scenarios, i.e. environmental impacts and a set of services provided. These indicators are then used to compute eco-efficiency ratios. These have been defined by (Seppälä et al., 2005) as the ratio between services provided by the territory and its environmental impacts. These ratios can help compare the environmental performances of different agricultural scenarios such as in dairy farms (Iribarren et al., 2011) and vineyard irrigation (Canaj et al., 2021). Eco-efficiency allows for a trade-off to be identified between economy and environment to achieve a certain level in the environmental performance of a society (Huppes and Ishikawa, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_40]

A few studies have presently implemented territorial LCA approaches on agricultural areas to support decision-making in the design of land planning scenarios for example in the French Brittany region (Avadí et al., 2016) and Aube department (Borghino et al., 2021) or in the Walloon region of Belgium (Ding et al., 2020). However, none of them have compared the impacts of planning scenarios that integrate the hydraulic infrastructures. [SOURCE: Page_40]

32 [SOURCE: Page_40]

From an environmental point of view, the main objective of this study is to assess the conditions under which hydraulic projects can be selected as an efficient option for securing the water supply of agricultural areas. This can be performed thanks to territorial LCA methodology. Generic conclusions are drawn from a theoretical case study, located in the South of France. The case study is a theoretical agricultural perimeter of 700 ha (size of a small municipality), where water resources ought to become a challenging issue for agriculture within the coming next decades due to climate change (Giorgi and Lionello, 2008). Three main planning alternatives (described in detail in section 2.1.2) will be compared based on a combination of crops and on the implementation or not of hydraulic infrastructures. The latter rely on the use of surface water resources, distinguishing two types of sources, and their dedicated infrastructure, i.e. a local resource consisting of storm water run-off stored in an Agricultural Reservoir (AR), and an imported resource taken directly from a river through an Inter-Basin Water Transfer (IBWT). These water projects have long lifetimes, and local planners and decision-makers need to identify the “no regret” scenarios. This study also addresses the water-energy-infrastructure nexus, and discusses the design conditions under which hydraulic projects can be considered, from an environmental point of view, as viable options for securing agricultural territories. [SOURCE: Page_41]

### 2. Material and methods

The general methodology adopted in this study follows the territorial LCA approach, and is described according to the four main LCA stages, i.e. (i) goal and scope definition, (ii) life cycle inventory (LCI), (iii) impact assessment, and (iv) results interpretation. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Goal and scope of the T-LCA study [SOURCE: Page_41]

### 2.1.2. System boundaries

Figure 5 illustrates the studied systems that provide services to society. It comprises the territorial foreground system and the territorial background system (Loiseau et al., 2013). The first includes all the agricultural activities located within the studied area. According to Nitschelm et al. (2016), a cradle-to-gate perspective is adopted and the impacts induced by the agricultural products once they leave the territorial boundaries are not considered. Nevertheless, all the impacts induced by the use of inputs to produce crops are included. They can be divided into two categories: inputs related to water irrigation and others such as energy, fertilizers or pesticides. The latter are part of the territorial background system since they are all assumed to be imported. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Concerning water inputs, dedicated hydraulic projects comprise three sections, i.e., the Primary Irrigation Infrastructure (PII), the Secondary Irrigation Infrastructure (SII) and the Tertiary Irrigation Infrastructure (TII). PII is the infrastructure implemented for water withdrawal. For the IBWT, the PII has a water pumping station that withdraws surface water and an entirely buried water pipeline whose length depends on the location of the water scarce territory requiring a water supply (IEA, 2016). For the AR, the PII has a water reservoir that can store a given volume of storm-water run-off during the winter (Gorguner and Kavvas, 2020) and a pumping station to supply water to the agricultural perimeter. The Water Supply Network (WSN) represents the SII. The WSN is made of water pipelines that are connected to the PII, i.e. on the AR pumping station or on the IBWT water pipeline. Its goal is to transport water from the PII to the TII, which is located on the agricultural field. The TII represents the On-field Irrigation Technology (OIT). The OIT is either a drip-to-drip or a sprinkler irrigation technology [SOURCE: Page_42]

34 [SOURCE: Page_42]

depending on the type of crops growing in the agricultural field. Indeed a drip-to-drip irrigation is applied to perennial crops like grapevines whereas sprinkler irrigation is better adapted to annual crops like wheat or corn. [SOURCE: Page_43]

For the AR, the entire infrastructure is in the territorial foreground system since it is dedicated to the studied agricultural area, whereas all the material and energy used for the construction, operation and maintenance phases are in the territorial background system. IBWT is designed to supply water to an entire region, hence sharing with other agricultural areas, cities or industries (Muñoz et al., 2010).It is therefore part of the background system. [SOURCE: Page_43]

The rehabilitation of the hydraulic infrastructure sites when end-of-life is reached is not considered because their impacts are assumed to be insignificant (Risch et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_43]

### 2.1.3. System functions

Territories are multifunctional systems (Loiseau et al., 2013). In general, three main functions can be selected for agricultural systems, i.e. i) economic profit, ii) food production, and iii) land management and occupation (Tendall and Gaillard, 2015). According to Borghino et al. (2021), the total quantity of agricultural products produced annually (Mass of Agricultural Products, MAP in kg), and the total agricultural area harvested annually (Land Occupation – LO in ha of agricultural land) can be employed as proxies to quantify the latter two territorial functions. Finally, the Agricultural Turnover (AT), defined as the sum of annual sales of crop production (in €), is used as a proxy to evaluate the economic profit of the studied territory. [SOURCE: Page_43]

35 [SOURCE: Page_43]

### 2.1.4. Studied scenarios

Table 2 describes each of the three studied scenarios, which are based on the same size of cultivated area. Since Scenario 0 does not have any irrigation infrastructure, the crops are rainfed. Three types of crops have been selected to be consistent with Mediterranean agriculture and to reflect a diversity of production both in terms of type of plants (perennial or not, cultivated or not), and in terms of valorization (food security or added value creation). The other two land planning scenarios have access to a water supply with IBWT as PII in Scenario 1 whereas Scenario 2 relies on AR. The AR surface of 9.1 ha covers a negligible share of the cultivated area surface, i.e. 1.3%. Therefore, both scenarios have the same SII, TII and agricultural perimeter occupation. Due to irrigation, land occupation in Scenarios 1 and 2 is different from that of Scenario 0. Indeed, non-irrigated crops still occupy the cultivated area while irrigated crops occupy the remaining surface. With access to irrigation, it is assumed that yields could be increased and vines would no longer be registered as Controlled Designation of Origin (CDO) (yield limits) but would rather be classified within Protected Geographical Indications (PGI). [SOURCE: Page_44]

As mentioned in section 2.1.2, it is noteworthy that, unlike the AR, the IBWT allows for the irrigation of a large area, for which the studied territory only represents a small share. Therefore, only part of the IBWT impact is allocated to Scenario 1. This allocation is calculated by performing the ratio between the annual quantity of water for irrigation needed by the crops and the annual quantity of water circulating in the water pipeline. [SOURCE: Page_45]

Life Cycle Inventory (LCI) [SOURCE: Page_45]

There are three stages in the inventory phase. Firstly, the different agricultural LCI processes involved in the studied scenarios are collected. Secondly, the hydraulic infrastructures are sized according to an area of 700 ha and their LCI data are gathered. Thirdly, particular attention is paid to water balance and energy consumption calculations for hydraulic infrastructures, based on the crop water needs. This LCA phase is based both on existing LCA databases and studies. Moreover, the co-authors of the paper who are working for French water management companies, i.e. Société du Canal de Provence et d'Aménagement de la Region Provençale and BRL Ingénierie, provided detailed design studies of similar hydraulic infrastructures and expert knowledge. [SOURCE: Page_45]

### 2.2.1. Crop LCI data

Table S. 1 of the Annex I presents the data used for the LCI of the crops considered in this study, i.e. the sources of the inventories and their adaptations for this study as well as the crop yields. [SOURCE: Page_45]

The French agricultural LCA data for corn and wheat were chosen, using the Agribalyse database V$_3$.0. (Koch and Salou, 2020) for the first and World Food LCA DataBase WFLDB 3.5 (Nemecek et al., 2019) for the latter. The use of these two databases limits the risk of inconsistency, since all background processes originate from the Ecoinvent V$_3$ database (Wernet et al., 2016) (e.g. pesticide or fertilizer manufacturing). As no datasets were available with or without irrigation for PGI and CDO grapevines in these two databases, inventories were created using data from French agricultural statistics (Agreste, 2016). The quantity of water necessary for the irrigated grapevines originates from a study based in the South of France (IFV and Chambres d’agriculture Languedoc-Roussillon, 2013). The AGEC-LCI (AGricultural Emissions Calculator – LCI) VBA tool was used to calculate the amounts of nitrogen, phosphorus and metals emitted to water, soil and air related to the application of mineral and organic fertilizers as well as on metal-based fungicides (e.g. copper) inputs. Estimation are provided for a variety of annual crops (i.e. sugar beet, durum wheat, soft wheat carrots, rapeseed, alfafa, maize, barley, peas, potatoes, sunflower, triticale) and for one perennial (i.e. grapevine), as well as for temporary grassland and permanent meadow (Viveros Santos et al., [SOURCE: Page_45]

37 [SOURCE: Page_45]

2020). These calculations are based on the assumptions and equations used in the databases providing agricultural LCIs i.e. WFLDB, Ecoinvent and Agribalyse, and are adapted to the French pedoclimatic context. In this study, AGEC-LCI was used for the construction of PGI and CDO grapevines LCIs. In order to be consistent with other Ecoinvent processes all pesticides are assumed to be absorbed by the agricultural ground (Wernet et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_46]

### 2.2.2. Water supply LCI

Figure 6 describes the main design parameters, based on expert knowledge, to be considered for hydraulic infrastructures in Scenarios 1 and 2. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Figure 6 Main design parameters for the hydraulic infrastructures 𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (km), the length of IBWT’s buried water pipeline; %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 , the share of IBWT impacts which is allocated to the Scenario 1; 𝑆𝐴𝑅 (m^2), AR’s surface; 𝑉𝐴𝑅 (m^3), AR’s volume; 𝑃𝑇𝐼𝐼 𝑠𝑝 (barg), the pressure of the sprinkler OIT; 𝑃𝑇𝐼𝐼 𝑑𝑑 (barg), the pressure of the drip-to-drip OIT; 𝑃𝑆𝐼𝐼 (barg), the pressure inside the WSN; and 𝐻𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 (m), the altitude of the agricultural perimeter with respect to IBWT pumping station. [SOURCE: Page_46]

The hydraulic infrastructure LCIs were subdivided into two separate stages, i.e. i) the construction and ii) the operation phase. Inventories are based on the Ecoinvent V$_3$ cut-off database. [SOURCE: Page_46]

### 2.2.2.1. Water infrastructure construction

Lifetime is an important parameter in the environmental assessment of an infrastructure (Risch et al., 2021). In this study, AR, IBWT and the WSN lifetimes are presumed to last 50 years. All [SOURCE: Page_46]

38 [SOURCE: Page_46]

the data concerning the lifetimes of the different parts of the hydraulic infrastructures are provided in Table S. 4 of the Annex I. [SOURCE: Page_47]

According to expert knowledge, all the civil engineering operations were supposed to take place on soft grounds for IBWT and WSN. For AR, these operations were considered to take place on 5% (v/v) rocky grounds. Table S. 3 of the Annex I indicate the different civil engineering operations as well as the type of material needed for the construction of the hydraulic infrastructures. An aggregated dataset for the LCI of hydraulic infrastructure construction i.e. the amount of material and repair rates as well as the names of the LCI processes of materials and of their manufacturing processes in the Ecoinvent database, is available in Table S. 4 of the Annex I. For each of the civil engineering operations, working crews were formed including all the machines and their respective energy consumption for one hour of work (Table S. 3 of the Annex I). [SOURCE: Page_47]

All the material was considered to be provided by the globalized market except for the cast- iron pipes that are manufactured in France, and for which specific transportation distances were chosen. Adequate manufacturing processes as well as scenarios dedicated to the end- of-life of materials (disposal in a landfill, recycling or incineration) were selected for these materials, as described in (Risch et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_47]

### 2.2.2.2. Water balance and energy consumption

The operation phases of water infrastructure are characterized by water and energy consumption. Figure 7 shows the annual flows that need to be taken into account to achieve a water balance in TII and PII. No water loss is assumed to occur in the SII as well as in the IBWT water pipeline. Only the PII consumes energy, which is sourced from the French medium voltage electricity mix. This consumption depends on the hydraulic design defined in Figure 6. [SOURCE: Page_47]

39 [SOURCE: Page_47]

Figure 7 Water balance and energy calculation framework 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑝𝑖𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 (m^3/yr), the flow of water that is evapotranspirated over the agricultural perimeter; 𝑊𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑒 and 𝑊𝑔𝑟𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑑 (m^3/yr), the water losses of the agricultural perimeter due to the efficiency of irrigation; and 𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 (m^3/yr), the total quantity of water that needs to be supplied to the agricultural perimeter for irrigation ; 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 (m^3/yr), the quantity of rainwater and run-off water filling the AR; 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 (m^3/yr), the share of water that is evaporated from the AR open-surface; 𝑊𝑢𝑛𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 (m^3/yr), the water of the AR that is not consumed and stored for the next year; 𝑊𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (m^3/yr), the total quantity of water that is circulating in the IBWT infrastructure; 𝐸𝐴𝑅 (Wh/yr), the annual energy used at AR pumping station; and 𝐸𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (Wh/yr), the annual energy consumption at the IBWT pumping station. [SOURCE: Page_48]

At TII, water is either consumed directly by the crops through evapotranspiration or lost through run-off to the surface and groundwater compartments. Evapotranspiration data are already included in the Agribalyse 3 (Koch and Salou, 2020) and WFLDB databases (Nemecek et al., 2019). An irrigation efficiency factor is applied to account for water losses. According to (Nemecek et al., 2019), this factor varies between 0.75 for sprinkler irrigation and 0.9 for the drip-to-drip irrigation. The water losses include run off to rivers (80%) and to groundwater compartments (20%). [SOURCE: Page_48]

The water balance and energy consumption related calculations are in the Annex I. [SOURCE: Page_48]

Performance indicators and LCIA impacts methods [SOURCE: Page_48]

As previously mentioned, one of the main adaptations of the LCA methodological framework for assessing territorial systems is to quantify eco-efficiency ratios. The following section describes their necessary indicators, i.e. the three main functions of the studied territory and their resulting environmental impacts. [SOURCE: Page_48]

40 [SOURCE: Page_48]

For biomass production, data on crop yields are used. These are directly obtained from the information given in Table S. 1 of the Annex I. For the economic function, the agricultural turnover was assessed using market price data for the studied crops, sourced from the French agricultural statistics, which are provided in Table 3. Finally, the land planning function is estimated through the total agricultural perimeter area, i.e. 700 ha in all scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Table 3 Market price of crops [SOURCE: Page_49]

Type of crop                   Market price (€/kg)            Year           Source Corn                           0,17                           2019            (Agreste, 2019) Wheat                          0,16                           2019            (Agreste, 2019) “Pays       d’Oc”       PGI 0,70                              2019           (DRAAF grapevine                                                                    Occitanie, 2020) “Languedoc”            CDO 1,22                               2019            (DRAAF grapevine                                                                    Occitanie, 2020) [SOURCE: Page_49]

Concerning environmental impacts, both midpoint and endpoint indicators are quantified with the IMPACT World+ method (Bulle et al., 2019) to address the different sub-objectives described in Table 1. IMPACT World+ was chosen over the ReCiPe 2016 LCIA methods (Huijbregts et al., 2017) because it includes the latest development in terms of impact assessment (Bulle et al., 2019), such as the AWARE model (Boulay et al., 2018) and the USEtox model (Rosenbaum et al., 2008), which are the present consensual way of assessing water scarcity as well as ecotoxicity and human toxicity impacts (European Commission - Joint Research Centre, 2012). Endpoint indicators facilitate the comparison of alternatives because they reflect the differences between causes of stress further down the cause-effect chains that directly affect a society (i.e. human health, ecosystem quality and resources). Then, midpoint indicators allow for the identification of issues related to the water-energy-infrastructure nexus. The IMPACT World+ v.1.46 actually available for download on SimaPro software does not include an endpoint indicator for the resource area of protection. Hence, the “Fossil and Nuclear Energy Use” and “Mineral Resource Use” midpoint indicators were employed as proxies. The AWARE characterization factors (CF) for the French Mediterranean area are sourced from a GIS (Geographic Information System) map (WULCA, 2021), i.e. 46.6 for the arid sub-watershed and 1 for the temperate watershed. Penoxsulam (CAS No. 219714-96-2) a herbicide used in the CDO and PGI grapevines was not characterized in the IMPACT World+ method, thus not quantified in this study. It should not significantly affect the impact assessment because it only represents less than 0.02% in weight of the total amount of phytosanitary products used in both CDO and PGI grapevine cultivation. [SOURCE: Page_49]

41 [SOURCE: Page_49]

### 3. Results

In this section, the results of the study are presented according to the three main objectives defined in Table 1. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Sub-objective 1: Eco-efficiency comparison [SOURCE: Page_50]

The eco-efficiency ratios are calculated according to the different services provided by the land planning scenarios in Table 4. The higher the eco-efficiency of one scenario, the better it is from an environmental point of view. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Figure 8 compares the eco-efficiencies of the three scenarios in relative terms (i.e. normalized by the best scenario). [SOURCE: Page_50]

Table 4 Services provided by the three land planning scenarios [SOURCE: Page_50]

Territorial functions            S$_0$ (no irrigation)              S$_1$ (IBWT)(1)          S$_2$ (AR)(2) [SOURCE: Page_50]

Area harvested (ha)              700                             700                   700 Mass     of    agricultural 3,34                                 4,56                  4,56 products (kTons) Agricultural         turnover 2,14                               2,40                  2,40 (M€) (1) IBWT : “Inter-Basin Water Transfer” (2) AR : “Agricultural Reservoir” [SOURCE: Page_50]

The results for the environmental performance of a scenario depend on the investigated territorial function. For the Land Occupation (LO) function, Scenario 0 is better than the irrigated scenarios for all environmental impacts considered. After Scenario 0, Scenario 1 presents best performance except for impacts on abiotic resources. This is due to the different impacts induced by the hydraulic infrastructures. The same conclusion arises for the economic function, where the Scenario 0 turnover is similar to scenarios 1 and 2, mitigating the differences in biomass production between scenarios. Indeed, a greater part of the agricultural perimeter is occupied by CDO grapevine, which is rather focused on quality than on quantity in the non-irrigated scenarios than in the irrigated ones. Moreover, the market price of CDO grapes is about 7 fold higher than for corn and wheat and around 1.75 times higher than for PGI grapevines. [SOURCE: Page_50]

On the contrary, for biomass production, the calculated eco-efficiency is higher for both irrigated scenarios than for the non-irrigated when considering the Ecosystems (Eco), Human Health (HH) and Mineral Resource Use (MRU) indicators. The reason is that the higher [SOURCE: Page_50]

42 [SOURCE: Page_50]

quantity of MAP of the irrigated scenarios counteracts their higher environmental impacts in comparison with the non-irrigated scenario. They therefore have higher eco-efficiencies. Considering the environmental impacts for the Fossil and Nuclear Energy Use (FNEU) indicator, the fact that Scenario 0 does not use energy for pumping water counteracts its low amount of provided service, therefore its eco-efficiency can be higher than for the Scenario 1. [SOURCE: Page_51]

Sub-objective 2: assessing the water-energy-infrastructure nexus [SOURCE: Page_53]

A contribution analysis is carried out to identify the main impacting stages in infrastructure projects. Figure 10 compares the environmental impacts of Scenarios 1 and 2 for the Land Occupation functional unit at midpoint level. The difference between their impacts resides in their respective water-energy-infrastructure nexus. [SOURCE: Page_53]

For the water part of the nexus, unlike the AR, the IBWT supplies water from a low water stress area with a low AWARE CF. Thus, for Water Scarcity (WS), this leads to an avoided impact for the Scenario 1 “Irrigation water”. [SOURCE: Page_53]

For the energy part of the nexus, the impacts on the IBWT (i.e., “Energy – PII”) are about 2.5 fold higher than on the AR for each midpoint indicator, because the IBWT is consuming more energy. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Both scenarios have the same SII and TII, hence the environmental performances of their infrastructure reside in their respective PII, whose impact assessment results are more mixed. Indeed, for Climate Change, short term (CC, st), Climate Change, long term (CC, lt), Fossil and Nuclear Energy Use (FNEU), Ozone Layer Depletion (OLD), Water Scarcity (WS), Freshwater Acidification (FA), Terrestrial Acidification (TA) and Ionizing Radiation (IR) both [SOURCE: Page_53]

45 [SOURCE: Page_53]

scenario PIIs have almost the same impacts. For some of the midpoint indicators (Mineral Resource Use (MRU), Photochemical Oxidant Formation (POF), Human Toxicity cancer (HTc), Human Toxicity non cancer (HTnc), Marine Eutrophication (ME) and Particulate Matter Formation (PMF)), the IBWT performs worse than the AR because of the high amount of cast- iron necessary in its infrastructure. On Freshwater Ecotoxicity (FEco) and Freshwater Eutrophication (FE), AR performs worse than IBWT because the amount of bronze and copper necessary for equipping the AR pumping station is fully allocated to the studied territory. The same applies to Land Transformation, biodiversity (LT, bio) and Land Occupation, biodiversity (LO, bio) because of the high amount of land occupied by AR, unlike the IBWT, which involves a buried pipeline. [SOURCE: Page_54]

Sub-objective 3: Design parameters to define conditions under which a given hydraulic project performs better [SOURCE: Page_56]

Section 3.2 results suggest that PII dominates the impacts of both hydraulic projects due to the high consumption of material and energy. However, these impacts are highly dependent on design parameters that can differ from one territory to another, especially for the IBWT, such as 𝑊𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 , and 𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 . The Equation 1, achieved through the use of equations S.8-S.12 of the Annex I, was used to determine the sets of 𝑊𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (or the corresponding %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ) and 𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 for which the IBWT alternative is better than the AR. The four break-even areas presented in Figure 11 can be subdivided in two types of information that can help support decision making, i.e. i) Certain areas, where a hydraulic infrastructure is better than the others for all 4 indicators, ii) Uncertain areas, where a decision cannot be made about the best environmental performance of a hydraulic infrastructure, because one water supply is not better than the others for all 4 indicators. Certain areas are within break-even area 5 where the IBWT water supply is better than AR. ### 4. Discussion

Supporting decision making [SOURCE: Page_57]

This paper proposes a novel approach for defining the conditions under which the environmental performances of a hydraulic project varies higher or lower than another type of project, while taking into account biophysical constraints. The tipping lines that were computed according to the main parameters of the designed inter-basin transfer illustrate that the impacts of this type of project can vary both higher or lower than the impacts of a project based on a local water resource such as an agricultural reservoir. Such studies are necessary for planners to identify the best options within a given context, while other parameters related to agricultural practices or land use patterns could be taken into account in future studies. [SOURCE: Page_57]

One main objective of the T-LCA framework is to compare the environmental performance of land planning scenarios. For this purpose, eco-efficiency is a metric for quantifying both the [SOURCE: Page_57]

49 [SOURCE: Page_57]

services provided by different scenarios and the resulting environmental impacts (Loiseau et al., 2018). In this study, there is no scenario in which the eco-efficiency remains maximal for the three considered territorial functions. These functions have been defined according to the scientific literature, and other functions could be identified from real case studies in consultation with stakeholders. [SOURCE: Page_58]

This study also highlighted that T-LCA can be used by territorial planners to identify trade-offs in the water-energy-infrastructure nexus. Section 3.2 inferred that an environmental analysis should not focus on only one aspect of the water-energy-infrastructure nexus before a decision is taken concerning the construction of a specific type of hydraulic infrastructure. Indeed, AR performs better on the energy component of the nexus whereas it is IBWT for the water component. For the infrastructure component, the analysis cannot be as straightforward because IBWT does not perform better than AR at all the midpoint indicators. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Considering spatio-temporal variability [SOURCE: Page_58]

### 4.2.1. In LCI

LCIs are adapted to the French context, particularly for agricultural production and the electricity mix. The relatively low impacts of the water supply in comparison with farming practices are partly due to the use of the French electricity mix, which comprises 70% nuclear- based electricity (ADEME, 2018), a low-carbon form of energy. Therefore, by choosing a more carbon-intensive electricity mix, as found in China for example, the impacts of the water supply could increase, thus modifying the results of this study (Leão et al., 2019a). For agricultural production, more detailed modelling would allow for local specificities to be better accounted for, such as the pedo-climatic conditions of the studied territory, or the agricultural practices implemented by the stakeholders. To enhance spatial representativeness, regionalized LCI could be developed based on interviews of local stakeholders as in Borghino et al. (2021) or spatial databases (Nitschelm et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_58]

In addition, the inter-annual variation of crop yields has not been considered in this study. Indeed, yield data for grapevines were sourced from 2013 data (Agreste, 2016). Wheat and corn yields were also based on data over an average of 4 years: 2005-2009 for corn (Koch and Salou, 2020) and 2014-2019 for wheat (Nemecek et al., 2019). The required amount of water fluctuates between April and August depending on the irrigation periods of the different crops, with increasing needs from June to August. Yet, the water requirement of the cultivated area was overestimated, hence so was 𝐸𝐴𝑅 . Moreover, more water circulates through the IBWT during the irrigation period than during the rest of the year, when agricultural needs are lower. The annual quantity of water circulating through the IBWT was therefore also overestimated. [SOURCE: Page_58]
