### 4.2.2. In LCIA

Spatial variability was only considered for quantifying the midpoint impact on water resources with the AWARE model, at the sub-watershed scale. This significantly improves the calculation of the impact, since there are strong regional discrepancies in terms of water resource availability. However, temporal variability was not taken into account in this study as annual characterization factors (CF) were chosen for each of the sub-watersheds. Selecting monthly CFs has a significant impact on the Water Scarcity (WS) indicator, as indicated in Table S. 8 of the Annex I, where WS calculated with annual or monthly CF are compared. Indeed, for Scenario 2, the consideration of a winter CF for the water filling of the AR can lead to an avoided impact. Yet, these results are preliminary and a better assessment of the local hydrology would be necessary. Indeed, the global impacts of small reservoirs on hydrology are estimated to represent 5% of the mean discharge and 44% of the low flow (Habets et al., 2018), and should be fully addressed in AWARE calculations. Spatialized CFs for site-dependent impacts such as eutrophication or acidification could also have been employed for better territorial representativeness (Nitschelm et al., 2016). This could be done by applying new regionalized LCIA methods such as IMPACT World + (Bulle et al., 2019) or LC-IMPACT (Verones et al., 2020), when their full development will be achieved. [SOURCE: Page_59]

Water resource in LCIA methods [SOURCE: Page_59]

The impact induced by water resource consumption is crucial for the comparison between performances of the studied scenarios. In the IMPACT World+ method, the terrestrial ecosystems are only affected by water use if it is extracted from the ground because it affects the moisture of soils, which in turn impacts the growth of given plant species (Zelm et al., 2011). Therefore, the impacts on terrestrial ecosystems remain negligible in this study because the irrigation water originates from surface water. In ReCiPe 2016, impacts of water consumption on terrestrial ecosystems are based on the Net Primary Production (NPP) of vascular plants limited by water, and encompass all water bodies (Pfister et al., 2009). For aquatic ecosystems, both endpoint LCIA methods are based on the works of (Hanafiah et al., 2011) where the effects of changes in river discharge on freshwater fish species are considered. Since lotic habitats are most vulnerable to consumptive water use (Vörösmarty et al., 2010), it would also be worthwhile, in the long run, to use the HCP (Habitat Change Potential) midpoint impact category in the study. This comprises the effects of water consumption on instream species (Damiani et al., 2021). In LC-IMPACT, only the impacts of both surface water and groundwater consumption on wetlands are considered (Verones et al., 2020). Recent investigations propose to consider the impacts on terrestrial ecosystems that entail the conversion of a terrestrial habitat into an aquatic habitat (Dorber et al., 2020). Applied to AR, these new [SOURCE: Page_59]

51 [SOURCE: Page_59]

spatialized CFs would increase the impacts of AR on terrestrial ecosystems while having a beneficial effect on aquatic ecosystems. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Taking into account the effects of global changes [SOURCE: Page_60]

Within the next decades, global changes such as climate change are expected to intensify the variations in meteorological parameters (Mbow et al., 2019), which, in turn, could lead to a rise in inter-annual variations of the scenario eco-efficiencies. Therefore, prospective T-LCA of land planning scenarios with long-lasting infrastructures would result in the use of more robust data and in the consideration of potential environmental feedbacks. The effects of the different types of stress induced by the evolution of meteorological conditions on crop development should be taken into account. Prospective crop yields could be sourced from real-life experiments, for example from a phytotron (Niero et al., 2015b) or from crop growth models such as Aquacrop (El Chami and Daccache, 2015) and CropSyst (Tendall and Gaillard, 2015). Due to the spatio- temporal dynamics of water resources, inter-annual variations of water availability should also be taken into account. A projection of future availability could be sourced from databases as has been done by (Leão et al., 2019b) with the Prospective Water-Supply Mix (P-WSmix). A modification in the future water availability would lead to a difference between the values of present and future AWARE CF. [SOURCE: Page_60]

### 5. Conclusion

This study aimed to assess, from an environmental point of view, the conditions under which hydraulic projects can be considered as an efficient option in order to secure the water supply of agricultural areas. However, as the different scenarios do not provide the same range of services, it is impossible to perform conventional LCA for comparison purposes on the basis of the same functional unit. The present paper demonstrates the feasibility and relevance of the territorial LCA approach as well as the associated eco-efficiency concept for comparing such scenarios. This was achieved by comparing three different theoretical land-planning scenarios, i.e. without irrigation (scenario 0), irrigated by an IBWT (scenario 1) or by an AR (scenario 2). The major highlights and main recommendations and challenges to conduct territorial LCA approaches are listed in the following: [SOURCE: Page_60]

➢ T-LCA provides eco-efficiency metrics that provide exhaustive information about the environmental performance of land-planning scenarios for three territorial functions in this paper, “agricultural turnover”, “mass of agricultural products” and “land occupation”. Depending on the selected function, the environmental performance of the three scenarios vary. For functions “agricultural turnover” and “land occupation”, scenario 0 has a better eco-efficiency over all the endpoints indicators. For function “mass of [SOURCE: Page_60]

52 [SOURCE: Page_60]

agricultural products”, the results are more contrasted : scenario 1 is better for Human Health and Ecosystems, and scenario 2 is better on Mineral Resource Use and Fossil and Nuclear Energy Use. These outputs allow the identification of trade-offs between different functions and environmental impacts, and can support local planners and stakeholders to select scenarios that maximize the main function of their project while limiting the effects on other functions. ➢ The T-LCA framework identifies trade-offs in the water-energy-infrastructure nexus. Overall, the water for irrigation supplied from IBWT performs better than from AR regarding Ecosystems and Human Health because only a part of its total impact is allocated to the studied territory.    Results are less advantageous for the Mineral Resource Use, due to the high amount of cast-iron necessary in IBWT infrastructure, as well as for Fossil & Nuclear Energy Use, due to the electricity consumption for importing water from another watershed. ➢ Moreover, T-LCA could also apply to the eco-design of large-scale hydraulic infrastructures. Indeed this framework can provide flexible environmental impact assessments (i.e. tipping lines) that take into account several design parameters, thus contributing to the choice of an infrastructure design. ➢ The main limitation of this study is that the LCIs are built according to static environmental conditions. Therefore the LCIs do not account for the effects of environmental dynamics (i.e. climate, hydrology and soil) nor do they include the possibility of changes in the ecosphere (e.g. climate change or water resource depletion), which are expected to further intensify during the long lifetime of the hydraulic infrastructures. For this reason, it is necessary to involve direct and indirect environmental feedback into the T-LCA framework for the choice, without risk of regret, of a specific type of long-lasting hydraulic infrastructure. [SOURCE: Page_61]

53 [SOURCE: Page_61]

54 [SOURCE: Page_62]

Chapter III: Taking                              the          spatio-temporal effects of climate change into account for Life Cycle Assessment of prospective scenarios to secure water supplies in agricultural areas Chapter II highlights the usefulness of T-LCA to compare the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural areas. The use of eco-efficiency ratios is essential to compare land planning scenarios based on different territorial functions, in order to identify potential trade-offs between scenarios providing different baskets of services. Irrigated scenarios perform better than rainfed scenarios when the quantities of services they provide offset the environmental impacts induced by the hydraulic infrastructures. Thus, irrigated scenarios may perform better than rainfed scenarios for food production, but not for economic profit or for land management and occupation functions. In addition, trade-offs between impact categories for different land planning scenarios are identified. Irrigation with an IBWT leads to better performance on human health, ecosystem quality and water stress than irrigation with an AR, at the expense of using more materials and energy. Tipping lines have been drawn between the two infrastructures to determine the values of key design parameters that define one scenario as better than the other (e.g. distance between the irrigated perimeter and the IBWT withdrawal site). This chapter also points out the limitations of T-LCA when it comes to studying land planning scenarios that include long-term infrastructures, i.e. the current T-LCA framework does not take into account the effects of global changes such as climate change on their environmental performance. [SOURCE: Page_63]

This limitation is addressed in Chapter III, which aims to demonstrate that the effects of climate change can affect the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural territories. To this end, two land planning scenarios, i.e. rainfed and irrigated, for theoretical agricultural territories of 700 ha located in six contrasting locations around the world are considered. For the irrigated scenario, it is assumed that there are no constraints on water, i.e. water can be supplied without restriction on available quantities, since water is imported from an unconstrained area, i.e. where the quantity of water available per inhabitant or per hectare [SOURCE: Page_63]

55 [SOURCE: Page_63]

of arable land is high (Yang and Pang, 2006), with an IBWT. This chapter has been published in Science of the Total Environment. [SOURCE: Page_64]

### 1. Introduction

Although they are essential for humanity, agricultural systems generate multiple impacts on the environment, including climate change, resource depletion or loss in biodiversity (Rockström et al., 2020). In turn, these environmental issues can affect agricultural systems through one or several interactions. For instance, the effects of climate change such as rising temperature, change in precipitation, CO$_2$ fertilization or more frequent occurrences of pests and diseases (Lobell et al., 2011) can lead to significant changes in crop yields and nutritional quality (Lobell and Gourdji, 2012). The implementation of irrigation infrastructures in agricultural territories is one of the main adaptation strategies that secures production yields despite increasing incidences of drought and high temperature (Mbow et al., 2019). However, quantitative environmental assessment methods still must be developed in order to avoid long- term land planning projects bearing significant environmental impacts (Vermeulen et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_66]

Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) is a well-established and recognized methodology for quantifying the environmental impacts of products and services (ISO, 2020a, 2020b). This methodology has been applied to the study of the environmental performance of irrigation projects including water transfers (Muñoz et al., 2010), reservoirs (Ghimire et al., 2014), groundwater pumping (Pradeleix et al., 2015), water reuse (Maeseele and Roux, 2021) and desalinization plants (Raluy et al., 2005a). However, these studies do not take into account impacts induced by water use, e.g. agricultural or urban use, because system boundaries are considered to stop at the water-supply gate. It is crucial to broaden the scale of analysis in order to consider the entire range of services provided by irrigation projects, such as territorial socio-economic benefits resulting from agricultural yield conservation. A territory is defined as a system associating a geographical space with stakeholders that develop, manage and exploit it (Moine, 2006). Territorial-LCA (T-LCA) is an adaptation of the conventional LCA framework. It assesses the environmental performance of a territory with an associated land planning scenario while considering its multifunctionality (e.g. economic, social or environmental land use functions) (Loiseau et al., 2013). It allows for the quantification of eco-efficiency metrics, defined as the ratio between services provided by a land planning scenario and its environmental impacts (Seppälä et al., 2005). Rogy et al. (2022) confirmed the need to apply T-LCA to broaden the scope of the study in order to compare the environmental performance of agricultural land planning projects with or without irrigation. The question of how to consider future changes in the environment then arises, since land planning scenarios have been designed for long-term periods and, over time, can be affected by global changes such as climate change (Weidema et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_66]

58 [SOURCE: Page_66]

Prospective LCA is a promising framework (Thonemann et al., 2020), although it has rather been applied to emerging technologies (Buyle et al., 2019) such as solar-assisted thermal energy recovery from wastewater (Muñoz et al., 2019) or formic acid production (Thonemann and Schulte, 2019). Main methodological issues relate to data availability, the quality of the Life Cycle Inventory (LCI), and to the uncertainty of the assessment (Thonemann et al., 2020). However, a few LCA studies focusing on prospective agricultural systems have addressed these issues, with particular attention towards the effects of climate change. Viveros Santos et al. (2022) evaluated the future environmental performance of viticulture for two French vineyards while relying on linear regression for projecting the yields. Niero et al. (2015b) used results of experiments in a device mimicking climate change conditions, in order to investigate these effects on the environmental performance of barley cultivation in Denmark. On one hand, while this approach allows for an accurate prediction of the effects of climate change on agricultural systems, it remains a time-consuming and expensive experimentation. On the other hand, El Chami and Daccache (2015) and Tendall and Gaillard (2015) used crop models to simulate the effects of climate change on the yields of various crops, e.g. winter wheat or grain maize, and on certain inputs, e.g. the amount of fertilizers and of water for irrigation. They demonstrated the importance of taking these effects into account in LCA. Currently, coupling of crop models with LCA has only been performed at farm scales and at specific locations, i.e. a farm in England (El Chami and Daccache, 2015) and two farms in Switzerland (Tendall and Gaillard, 2015). Moreover, this was carried out at a specific moment in time without accounting for the dynamics of the evolution in the environmental performance. Hence, this paper will propose methodological developments where spatio-temporal dynamics are considered for the quantification of the environmental performance of land planning scenarios of agricultural perimeters, whether irrigated or rainfed, at any location or moment in time. [SOURCE: Page_67]

At the end, the aim of this paper is to provide proof of concept that climate change can affect the environmental performance of a land planning scenario over the medium-long term. To this end, the methodological developments are implemented to theoretical case studies in different spatial contexts. [SOURCE: Page_67]

The original methodology based on the coupling of a crop model with the T-LCA framework is first described. Secondly, the methodology is implemented at contrasting locations in order to compare the eco-efficiency ratios over time of land planning scenarios with or without irrigation. Finally, a sensitivity analysis is performed on the results based on the key parameters of the study. [SOURCE: Page_67]

59 [SOURCE: Page_67]

### 2. Material and methods

Goal and scope of the study [SOURCE: Page_68]

Scenarios with and without an irrigation project are defined for a theoretical agricultural area of 700 ha. To define system boundaries, a cradle-to-gate perspective is adopted (Nitschelm et al., 2016) as described in Figure 13. Only the impacts directly generated by the agricultural activities of the studied territory are taken into account as well as the impacts induced by the import of goods and services. Two main questions are addressed in this study: i) how does climate change affect the environmental performance of a land planning scenario?, and ii) does the consideration of the effects of climate change lead to different conclusions when comparing the environmental performance of two land planning scenarios? [SOURCE: Page_68]

These two questions are discussed for six contrasting locations around the world characterised by different climate and soil properties and contributing to significant shares of the crop production of their respective countries (U.S. Department of Agriculture - National Agricultural Statistics Service, 2019), i.e. the United States of America (USA) (state of Colorado), India (state of Gujarat), Russia (Krasnodar krai), France (Occitanie region), China (Henan province) and Australia (state of Victoria). In this study, in terms of greenhouse gas emissions, the most pessimistic Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) scenario was selected, i.e. the Shared Socioeconomic Pathways 5-8.5 (SSP$_5$-8.5). SSP$_5$-8.5 is an unlikely worst-case scenario and not a business as usual (Hausfather and Peters, 2020). The worst-case scenario was selected in order to assess to what extent climate change can affect the environmental performance of land planning scenarios, rather than to make realistic predictions of what the performance would be in the future. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Overview of the methodology [SOURCE: Page_68]

Figure 12 outlines the general approach. Firstly, the studied scenarios are described. Secondly, a dynamic and regionalized Life Cycle Inventory (LCI) (one LCI for each location, land planning scenario and growing season) is built, based on the coupling between a crop model and LCA. This is the heart of the approach. Thirdly, the environmental impacts of the studied scenario are evaluated using Life Cycle Impact Assessment (LCIA) and the service of edible biomass production is computed by the crop model. Finally, the performance of the scenario is assessed with eco-efficiency ratios. These four main stages are detailed in the following sections. In addition, the data required for implementing the approach on the case studies are available in section 2 of the Annex II. [SOURCE: Page_68]

60 [SOURCE: Page_68]

This work was carried out using the R software (v^4.1.2) (RC Team, 2013) and the ncdf^4 (Pierce, 2021), chron (James and Hornik, 2020), xlsx (Dragulescu, 2014), readxl (Wickham and Bryan, 2022), pracma (Borchers, 2021) and tidyverse (Wickham et al., 2019) packages. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Figure 12 General approach used in the study for the dynamic and regionalized assessment of the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios. In blue, the four steps for the coupling of the crop model with the T-LCA framework. In grey are the input data for building the dynamic and regionalized Life Cycle Inventory, as well as for calculating the service provided by the territory. The locations of the six theoretical case studies are shown on the map [SOURCE: Page_69]

Description of the land planning scenarios [SOURCE: Page_69]

Two scenarios are investigated between 1981 and 2099. One scenario does not have any irrigation, and the land occupation is composed of 90% of wheat and 10% of fallow lands. The other is an irrigated scenario allowing the introduction of more productive crops, where 60% of the surface is occupied by irrigated wheat, 30% by irrigated maize, and 10% by non-irrigated crops (fallow lands). The irrigation system is an inter-basin water transfer (IBWT) designed to supply water to an entire region. It is assumed that the pumping station of the IBWT has a continuous flow rate of 3 m^3.s-1, that the pipeline is 100 km long and that water leaks are negligible. Therefore, according to the annual amount of water for irrigation required within the agricultural perimeter, only a part of the impact of the IBWT would be allocated to the agricultural perimeter. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Dynamic and regionalized LCI [SOURCE: Page_69]

Annual LCI data must be collected for the crop production and the IBWT hydraulic project for the irrigated scenario, as shown in Figure 13, in the six locations. The data related to the IBWT (i.e., amounts of materials and civil works, and energy consumption) are obtained from the work of Rogy et al. (2022). The electricity mix is adapted to each location by using the electricity mix of each country provided by the Ecoinvent version 3 database (Wernet et al., 2016). The [SOURCE: Page_69]

61 [SOURCE: Page_69]

latter is used for supplying water to the irrigated perimeters. Only the inventory data for agricultural production vary over the years depending on the amount of CO$_2$ in the atmosphere (CO$_2$ fertilization) and climate projections. A crop model is used to estimate annual yields and water crop requirements based on the irrigation management, i.e. irrigation method as well as when and at which level to irrigate. Model selection and operation are explained below. These outputs are then used to adapt the LCI of crop production related to water use, fertilizers, and other agricultural inputs. Field emissions are also updated each year, taking into account the quantities of inputs used and the soil and climate context. All these elements are explained below. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Figure 13 System boundaries of the studied land planning scenarios. The sections describing the inputs of the crop model as well as the LCI for agricultural production are written on the figure [SOURCE: Page_70]

### 2.4.1. Choice of the crop model and input data

The choice of crop model should be made according to the main purpose of its application and based on a compromise between accuracy and ease of use (Di Paola et al., 2016). The latter compromise depends on the number of biogeochemical cycles that are taken into account, e.g. carbon, water, nitrogen or phosphorus. [SOURCE: Page_70]

In the present study, the model should firstly be capable of predicting yields of various types of crops in a context of climate change. Secondly, the model should be applicable over a daily basis at any location, while changes in the climate (e.g. temperature increase, changes in precipitations and CO$_2$ fertilization), soil properties and agricultural practices (e.g. irrigated or rainfed) are taken into account. Thirdly, the model must be easy to use and should account for water as a key limiting factor on crop production. This is the minimal requirement when [SOURCE: Page_70]

62 [SOURCE: Page_70]

comparing the performances of rainfed and irrigated agricultural perimeters. Finally, the model has to be scientifically acknowledged. [SOURCE: Page_71]

The AquaCrop model (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a) of the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), is widely used in the scientific literature (Zeleke and Nendel, 2020), and fulfils the identified requirements. It takes into account the effects of both temperature and water stress on each of the following four steps of the yield calculations (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a), i.e. i) development of the canopy cover, ii) evapotranspiration, iii) the amount of above-ground biomass produced, and iv) the edible yield. AquaCrop only considers water as a key limiting factor, as other factors such as nitrogen or phosphorus are not endogenously modeled. [SOURCE: Page_71]

In order to run simulations on AquaCrop, it is necessary to collect input data on climate, soil, crops and agricultural management. All these data are described in section 1.1 of the Annex II as well as the definition of the initial conditions. [SOURCE: Page_71]

### 2.4.2. LCI for agricultural production

LCIs for agricultural production are adapted each year for the six locations to consider the climate effects on crop yields. Particular emphasis is placed on water balance, fertilizers and other agricultural inputs, and field emissions as described below. [SOURCE: Page_71]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5239198

- Summary: Section focusing on 2.4.2.1. water balance

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 2.4.2.1. Water balance

The LCA framework allows for the impacts of water consumption, i.e. evapotranspiration, product integration, or discharge into other basins and the sea to be assessed (Boulay et al., 2018). The calculation of water consumption is based on a water balance, i.e. the difference between water withdrawal and the share of water withdrawal that is released into the environment. In the simulations, AquaCrop takes into account two water inputs, i.e. irrigation and precipitation, as well as one consumption use, i.e. evapotranspiration, and two outputs, i.e. deep percolation and runoff. The water balance was therefore performed according to section 1.2.1 of the Annex II. [SOURCE: Page_71]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

### 2.4.2.2. Fertilizers

To carry out the inventory, it is necessary to calculate the amount of fertilizers applied to the studied systems, since AquaCrop considers that they are not a constraint to crop growth. The generic estimation of fertilizer application of the World Food LCA DataBase (WFLDB) (Nemecek et al., 2019) is used and is described in detail in section 1.2.2 of the Annex II. The fertilizer supplies are assumed to reach the maximum annual yield achievable by the crops, depending on the climate and soil of the studied location. [SOURCE: Page_71]

63 [SOURCE: Page_71]

---

### 2.4.2.3. Other inputs

It is assumed that the amount and types of pesticides, soil amendments, i.e. lime and dolomite, supplied to the agricultural territory, as well as the mechanical management of the agricultural plot remain unchanged over the timespan of the study. The amount and types of pesticides for a specific crop as well as the mechanical management of the agricultural plot are sourced from the WFLDB (Nemecek et al., 2019). The amount of soil amendment is sourced from the Agri- footprint 5.0 (Van Paassen et al., 2019), where values depend on the type of crop and on the country. [SOURCE: Page_72]

---

### 2.4.2.4. Field emissions

Since AquaCrop does not take into account nitrogen and phosphorus cycles, the field emission models used for the agricultural activities are based on pre-calculated emission factors or simplified equations as in Ding et al. (2020), which are in line with the LCA principles (Avadí et al., 2022). These emission models are also used in recognized methodologies such as AGricultural Emissions Calculator – LCI (AGEC-LCI) (Viveros Santos et al., 2020), WFLDB, Agribalyse (Koch and Salou, 2020) as well as Ecoinvent (Nemecek and Schnetzer, 2012) and allow to consider different practices, soil and climatic conditions. The equations of the emission models as well as their parameters are described in detail in section Chapter VII: 1.2.3 of the Annex II. Concerning pesticides, all active ingredients are assumed to be released into the soil (Nemecek et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_72]

Performance indicators and LCIA impact methods [SOURCE: Page_72]

The environmental performance of different scenarios is assessed through eco-efficiency, which is the ratio between the services provided by a territory and its environmental impacts. The single service defined in this study is the production of edible biomass resulting from a scenario within a given year and sourced from AquaCrop simulations. [SOURCE: Page_72]

To quantify the environmental impacts, the IMPACT World+ (Bulle et al., 2019) method was used at midpoint level (version 1.29 of IMPACT World+) as it includes the latest consensual developments in terms of impact assessments, such as the USEtox model (Rosenbaum et al., 2008) or the AWARE model (Boulay et al., 2018). Spatialization of characterization factors (CF) was considered only for the water scarcity indicator. To this end, AWARE's CFs at watershed scale were extracted from a geographic information system (GIS) map (WULCA, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_72]

Endpoint indicators were not selected as they are more uncertain than at midpoint (Hauschild et al., 2017). This uncertainty could even be exacerbated by the prospective approach of this [SOURCE: Page_72]

64 [SOURCE: Page_72]

study. For example, the effects of water availability on the Human Health indicator, the second most important contributor to this endpoint in the IMPACT World+ methodology, is driven by the income of the studied region which could evolve during the time span of the study as a function of its economic development (Boulay et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_73]

Sensitivity analysis [SOURCE: Page_73]

The sensitivity analysis is only performed for irrigated territories because more variables affect their performances in comparison with rainfed territories, e.g. water and electricity of the irrigation structure. It targets different modelling compartments as described in Figure 13, namely environmental factors by studying the effect of CO$_2$ fertilization, factors related to agricultural management by considering the amounts of fertilizer or water input, and technological factors by taking into account a prospective electricity mix in the different countries studied. The sensitivity analysis aims at assessing the effects of these factors on the territorial eco-efficiencies, hence both the service provided and the environmental impacts. Inputs of AquaCrop were modified first, i.e. the fertilizing power of CO 2 on a specific crop and the amount of water supply. To target the fertilizing power of CO$_2$ on a specific crop, the sink strength was modified. The sink strength is a parameter describing the performance of the crop under elevated atmospheric CO$_2$ concentration (Vanuytrecht et al., 2011). It takes values between 100%, i.e. important CO$_2$ fertilization effects, and 0%, i.e. low CO$_2$ fertilization effects. Finally, certain processes for constructing the dynamic and regionalized LCI were changed: these include the amount of fertilizer supplies and the electricity mixes. More explanations are given in [SOURCE: Page_73]

Table 5. [SOURCE: Page_73]

### 3. Results and discussion

In the results of this section, the evolution of relative changes of [year - 5; year + 5] moving averages is presented, since the decade is a commonly used timespan for comparison in agricultural statistics (Agreste, 2021). Firstly, section 3.1 uses the relative change of the eco- efficiency ratios of a given year with respect to the year 1986 (i.e. the reference year), for irrigated scenarios. Secondly, section 3.2 uses the relative change of eco-efficiency ratios of the rainfed scenario with respect to the irrigated scenario. Finally, section 3.3 uses the relative change of the results of the sensitivity analysis applied to the irrigated scenarios with respect to the original modelling of the irrigated scenario. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Effects of climate change on the environmental performance of a land planning scenario [SOURCE: Page_74]

Figure 14 reports the relative changes of eco-efficiency ratios calculated relative to the selection of midpoint indicators, and Figure S. 8 of the Annex II presents the evolutions of the impacts. Firstly, eco-efficiency ratios related to climate change long term (CClt) and water scarcity (WS) were chosen, since agricultural systems are important drivers of both of these impacts (Rockström et al., 2020). In the Impact WORLD+ methodology, two indicators are available for the climate change impact category: CClt assessed with the global temperature change potential in 100 years (GTP100) and climate change short term (CCst) assessed with the global warming potential over 100 years (GWP100) (Bulle et al., 2019). In this study, the CClt indicator was chosen, as it is completely correlated with the CCst indicator, as shown in Table S. 21 of the Annex II. Secondly, the least correlated eco-efficiency ratios with CClt were [SOURCE: Page_74]

66 [SOURCE: Page_74]

selected based on the correlation matrix in Table S. 21 of the Annex II, i.e. freshwater eutrophication (FE) (correlation of -0.23) and the mineral resources use (MRU) (correlation of 0.31). These indicators highlight the main patterns for the evolutions of relative changes of eco-efficiency ratios among all midpoint indicators of Figure S. 9 of the Annex II. Table S. 22 of the Annex II describes the 95% confidence interval of the linear regression for the eco- efficiency ratios. This table highlights the evolution trend of the eco-efficiency ratios. As such, the eco-efficiency ratios increase when the confidence intervals are strictly positive, decrease when the confidence intervals are strictly negative and do not evolve if the confidence intervals include zero. [SOURCE: Page_75]

For CClt, the eco-efficiency ratios tend to increase over time at each location, except for India where no clear trend is found. The maximum increase of eco-efficiency ratios is 28% in India. There is also a drop of eco-efficiency after 2085 in Russia, after 2071 in the USA and after 2074 in Australia. These results can be partly explained by the evolution of the yields in Figure S. 6 of the Annex II which are steadily increasing for China, India and France because of CO$_2$ fertilization, i.e. the increase of the photosynthesis rate due to elevated concentrations of CO$_2$ in the atmosphere (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a). The crops in these locations do not suffer from significant decrease in yields, unlike Russia, the USA and Australia. The drops in yields arise from high temperature stress during the maize season for Russia as well as Australia and from water stress for the USA as irrigation is not permitted during flowering to avoid risks of fusarium disease (BRL, 2019). In addition the impacts induced by the water supply (i.e. infrastructure of the IBWT, electricity and water use) follows the evolution of the water supplied by irrigation of Figure S. 7 of the Annex II. Overall, it can increase in France, decrease in the USA and Russia or present no clear trend in countries like China and India where the amount of precipitation during the cropping seasons can vary. The amount of water supply explains the peaks of eco- efficiency in China, i.e. in 2017 and 2069, and in India, i.e. in 2029 and 2068, since a lower amount leads to less use of electricity from carbon intensive mixes (see Table S. 24 of the Annex II). [SOURCE: Page_75]

For MRU, eco-efficiency ratios increase in India, Russia and in France, decrease in Australia, and present no clear trend in the USA and in China. The maximum increase of eco-efficiency ratio is 5% in France. The production of mineral fertilizers to support the yields is the major contributor to MRU. Therefore, when the eco-efficiency ratios increase, the yield increase counters the increase of impacts of the fertilizer supplies. [SOURCE: Page_75]

For FE, the eco-efficiency ratios increase in the USA, France as well as Russia, decrease in India and China, and present no clear trend in Australia. The maximum and minimum eco- efficiency ratios are respectively 61% in France and -54% in India. The use of fertilizers [SOURCE: Page_75]

67 [SOURCE: Page_75]

contributes to more than 80% of the impacts at each of the locations. Therefore, these contrasting results mainly stem from the impacts of phosphorus that leach into rivers due to soil erosion, and are directly related to the amount of annual precipitations of Figure S. 5 of the Annex II. [SOURCE: Page_76]

For WS, the eco-efficiency ratios increase for the USA, China, Russia, and Australia, with a maximum of around 36% for the USA. On the contrary, they decrease in France with a minimum of -10%. In India there is no clear trend, since they reach a peak of 82% and decrease to -25%. These results stem from the evolution of the amount of water use for irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_76]

68 [SOURCE: Page_76]

69 [SOURCE: Page_77]

Figure 14 Relative change of eco-efficiency of the irrigated perimeter with respect to the year 1986. The y-axis of both climate change long term and mineral resources use are different from the y-axis of both water scarcity and freshwater eutrophication. The eco-efficiency ratios are calculated with the annual production of edible biomass over the irrigated perimeters and four selected midpoint indicators of Impact WORLD+, i.e. climate change long term, freshwater eutrophication, mineral resources use and water scarcity. The x-axis is the relative change of eco-efficiency ratios of the irrigated scenario with respect to the year 1986. The y-axis is the year. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Comparison of the environmental performance of two land planning scenarios in a specific territorial context [SOURCE: Page_78]

Figure 15 presents the relative changes of eco-efficiency ratios for scenarios without irrigation compared to the scenario with irrigation calculated according to CClt, MRU, FE and WS. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In Australia, the irrigated perimeter performs much better than the rainfed. The difference of eco-efficiency ratios is around -100%, over the entire timespan of the study for all of the impact categories in Figure 15, except for WS where the conclusions of the study are evolving. For WS (see Figure S. 11 of the Annex II), there is a combination between exceptional years when the rainfed scenario improved the yields, and the significant impacts of water consumption in Australia, i.e. high AWARE CF of 87.4 m^3world eq. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In Colorado, the irrigated scenario performs better than the rainfed for CClt, FE and MRU while the conclusions can change for WS. For the latter, water is withdrawn in a watershed with a high AWARE CF of 63.9 m^3world eq, compared with that of the agricultural perimeter, which has a low AWARE CF of 1.1 m^3world eq. Therefore, the conclusion can only change for WS for the same reasons as in Australia. [SOURCE: Page_78]

At both of these locations, the irrigated perimeters perform better than the rainfed ones for the majority of impact categories (see Figure S. 12 of the Annex II). This is due to the yield preservation afforded by the irrigated scenario (i.e. more than 75%), as illustrated in Figure S. 10 of the Annex II. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In Russia, the rainfed perimeter is more performant than the irrigated one over the entire timespan of the simulation for WS while the opposite is observed for CClt, FE and MRU. This is due to the yield preservation afforded by irrigation not being important enough to counter the impacts of water consumption of the irrigated perimeter. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In China, the irrigated perimeter is better than the rainfed perimeter for FE, MRU and WS. For the latter, this is due to the low AWARE CF of 0.2 m^3world eq. The conclusions of the T-LCA only change for the CClt indicator. This arises from a combination between years when the two perimeters have a lower difference of yields and years with a lower amount of water supplied to the irrigated perimeter. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In India, the irrigated perimeter is always better than the rainfed perimeter for CClt, FE and MRU, whereas the conclusions vary for WS. This is also due to the combined effects of yields and amount of water supplied. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In France, the rainfed perimeter performs better than the irrigated for WS mainly due to the small difference in yields between the two scenarios and the opposite for FE. For CClt and [SOURCE: Page_78]

70 [SOURCE: Page_78]

MRU, conclusions are evolving due to the combined effects of yields and amounts of water supplied. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Two main observations can be derived from this section. Firstly, for a given location, the conclusions of the T-LCA can change according to the midpoint indicators with which the eco- efficiency ratios were calculated. Secondly, the conclusions of the T-LCA can also change for a given indicator, according to the location of the case study and to the year of the assessment. Both of these observations highlight the spatio-temporal effects of climate change as a crucial factor to consider for making “no regret” decisions when implementing long-term land planning scenarios of agricultural territories. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Sensitivity analysis [SOURCE: Page_81]

Figure 16 provides an overview of the main factors of the sensitivity analysis affecting the eco- efficiency of the irrigated land planning scenarios. The amount of water used for irrigation and the type of electricity mix have the greatest influence on results. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Environmental factors                      Agricultural practices                   Technological factors [SOURCE: Page_81]

CO$_2$ concentration in the              Amount of            Amount of                Prospective regionalized atmosphere                       fertilizers          water                        electricity mix [SOURCE: Page_81]

Low                             Low                  High                            High sensitivity                     sensitivity          sensitivity                     sensitivity [SOURCE: Page_81]

Environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios [SOURCE: Page_81]

Figure 16 Overview of the results of the sensitivity analysis of the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios to different factors including environmental, technological and agricultural management factors [SOURCE: Page_81]

A reduction in the water amount of each irrigation affects the yields of each irrigated perimeter, the impacts of the water supply as well as the emissions due to fertilization. As presented in Figure S. 16 of the Annex II, the effects of this change of modelling depend on the location and on the midpoint indicator. This modification leads to relative differences of eco-efficiencies between -20% and 10%. [SOURCE: Page_81]

In addition, the amount of water supplied by each irrigation can be further optimized to increase the environmental performance of the irrigated perimeters. This is corroborated by the evolution of the relative difference in yields presented in Figure S. 14 of the Annex II, which can be almost null in France, China and Russia, thus suggesting that the quantity of water supply could be further reduced. This optimization is not as straightforward for other locations such as the USA or India because most of the time, yields are reduced. Furthermore, in Australia the results are more contrasting, i.e. the yield can be lower or, in case of mild water stress during leaf expansion growth, even higher than the original modelling. [SOURCE: Page_81]

For some indicators, the prospective electricity mix can be more performant than the original mix, leading to relative changes of eco-efficiency ratios that can be as high as 350% for ionizing radiations (IR) in France because of the low share of nuclear power in the prospective electricity mix compared with the true electricity mix (Wernet et al., 2016). This choice of modelling could also lead to a zero relative difference of eco-efficiency ratios, e.g. for CClt and climate change short-term (CCst) in the USA, as the amount of fossil fuel in both mixes remains [SOURCE: Page_81]

73 [SOURCE: Page_81]

almost the same, i.e. 53% in the USA (Wernet et al., 2016) and 50% in the prospective mix. The prospective mix could also be less performant than the original mix, leading to relative changes that can be as significant as -52% in Australia for IR, because it does not include nuclear power in its original mix (Wernet et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_82]

Hence, the use of a prospective electricity mix can lead to significant effects on the eco- efficiency ratios of the studied territories. This highlights the fact that in addition to changes in the ecosphere associated with a SSP scenario, e.g. climate change, it is also important to consider the associated changes in the technosphere, e.g. electricity mix, when assessing the environmental performance of land planning scenario over the long-term. All the detailed results are presented in Figure S. 18 of the Annex II. [SOURCE: Page_82]

The environmental factor related to the reduction of CO$_2$ fertilization effects leads to a decrease in production of edible biomass, as illustrated in Figure S. 13 of the Annex II and to a decrease in use of fertilizers. Figure S. 15 of the Annex II illustrates how the diminution of CO$_2$ fertilization leads to a less than 6% relative change of eco-efficiency ratios for all the impact categories. Therefore, the eco-efficiencies are not sensitive to the sink strength parameter, i.e. the parameter that modulates the effects of CO$_2$ fertilization on crop growth. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Finally, the modification of the amount of fertilizer leads to a relative difference in eco-efficiency ratios between 0% and 5% for all the indicators and for each location (see detailed results in in Figure S. 17 of the Annex II). The sensitivity analysis was carried out by changing the calculation assumption for the amount of fertilizer to be applied to crops. The quantity of fertilizer is therefore still not a limiting factor for crop growth. The amount of fertilizer is therefore still not a limiting factor for crop growth. Therefore, the eco-efficiencies are not sensitive to a modification of the amount of fertilizer. The relative difference of eco-efficiencies are due to the computing method used for estimating the Phenological Heat Units (PHU) of crops, which sometimes overestimates the amount of Growing Degree Days between senescence and harvesting. It can lead to a maximum relative difference of 8% for the crop yields compared to the maximum yield achievable, and therefore to an overestimation of the amount of fertilizer supplied to the field. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Key learning points for decision making [SOURCE: Page_82]

Results from section 3.1 report that the environmental performance of the irrigated scenario evolves differently depending on the impact category and the location of the case study. In all cases but India, the effects of climate change tend to increase the eco-efficiency calculated based on the CClt indicator, although there is a decline at the end of the timespan of the study for locations such as USA, Australia or Russia. Extreme effects of temperatures or the [SOURCE: Page_82]

74 [SOURCE: Page_82]

occurrence of water stress during flowering, which is a non-irrigated period, can explain this. In addition, the results for the other impact indicators are more contrasted, with eco-efficiencies tending to fall in some cases, as in France for WS. This suggests that irrigation alone will not enable agricultural areas to adapt to the effects of climate change and that an answer to this issue could be to grow crops resistant to abiotic stresses (Belmin et al., 2023). For FE in China or India, eco-efficiencies tend to decrease as the annual rainfall amounts increase, leading to more phosphorus reaching rivers due to soil loss. In this case, measures reducing erosion could be considered to complement the irrigation project in order to preserve the eco-efficiency, such as the use of cover crops between two cropping seasons, reducing tillage or leaving crop residues over the field after the harvest (Panagos et al., 2015). For India, the evolution in eco- efficiency ratios has no clear trend for CClt and WS, although overall, eco-efficiencies appear to be rising through peaks of increase. This happens when the sowing takes place at the part of the rainfall season with the maximum precipitations. Therefore, a change of agricultural practice, i.e. a choice of more adequate sowing dates (Mbow et al., 2019), could be effective in order to increase the eco-efficiency of the irrigated scenario. [SOURCE: Page_83]

The comparison of the environmental performance of a scenario with and without irrigation displayed in section 3.2 reveals that the project with irrigation is not the most environmentally friendly in some cases. Indeed, even though it allows for more production of biomass, depending on the location and the year of the assessment, irrigation can lead to worst environmental performances over the long-term for impact indicators linked to the water- energy nexus. For the water part of the nexus, the rainfed perimeter performs globally better than the irrigated in each location except Australia and China. In Australia, this is due to the quasi-absence of biomass production without irrigation, whereas in China the reason is the low AWARE CF of 0.1 leading to low impacts for water consumption. For the energy part of the nexus, the irrigated scenario can perform worse than the rainfed in China for the CClt indicator, due to the electricity consumption for pumping water. The section 3.3 further identified the importance of the food-water-energy nexus, by showing that the environmental performance of the irrigated scenarios is highly sensitive to changes in the electricity mix (prospective mix) and to the amounts of water supplied during each irrigation. The increase of the amount of biomass provided by irrigation is also made at the expense of the impacts caused by its infrastructure, for example in France, China and Russia for the Human Toxicity cancer (HTc) indicator, as shown in Figure S. 12 of the Annex II. Therefore, the opportunity of a hydraulic project must be assessed at an early stage of the decision-making process considering its environmental performance according to a regionalized, dynamic and multi-criteria methodology to reveal potential trade-offs, and identify “no-regret” scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_83]

75 [SOURCE: Page_83]

AquaCrop yields: comparison with other studies [SOURCE: Page_84]

Comparing the yields from this study with others carried out in China and the USA using AquaCrop under unlimited conditions shows the same trends in projected yields with values in the same range. (Irmak et al., 2022; Rashid et al., 2019). The overall evolution of the simulated yields, i.e. increase for wheat and stagnation for maize, is also consistent with studies using ensembles of crop models, such as Rosenzweig et al. (2014) for mid to high latitudes in crop models that do not explicitly take into account nitrogen stress. [SOURCE: Page_84]

However, the values of simulated yields until 2020 are always higher than yields of historical statistics of FAO (FAO, 2020). Indeed, in truth, crops are facing various types of stress on top of water stress (e.g. weeds or nutritional stress) and are cultivated with a variety of agricultural practices (Irmak et al., 2022). Furthermore, AquaCrop does not take the effects of climatic hazards such as hail on crop yields into account. These are not critical points because the proposed methodology is built for a comparative approach at specific locations where the same hypotheses are made for both scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Addressing main sources of uncertainty [SOURCE: Page_84]

The aim of this paper was to achieve proof of concept that the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural territories were important to account for. Even though the methodological developments have been tested on theoretical case studies, the preliminary results allow the main sources of uncertainty to be identified. Uncertainties arise from different aspects of the modelling approach, i.e. the climate scenario and climate model, and the crop model. In addition, there are also uncertainties linked to the spatial representativeness of the data used. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Firstly, the climate projections are inherently uncertain since they consist in a variety of scenarios of emissions related to different socio-economic pathways, i.e. the SSP scenarios of the most recent IPCC report (IPCC, 2021b). These climate projections can be derived from different GCM that provide diverse results (Eyring et al., 2016). In this article, the most pessimistic SSP scenario was chosen. However, in order to make more realistic projections of environmental performance, climate data computed from business as usual SSP scenarios, such as SSP$_4$-6.0 or SSP$_2$-4.5, could be used (Hausfather and Peters, 2020). Further investigations are therefore necessary in order to perform a sensitivity analysis on the eco- efficiency ratios by using different climate scenarios in the methodology of this study. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Secondly, crop models can also provide different results for the projection of yields according to their structure (Asseng et al., 2013), e.g. the number of biogeochemical cycles taken into [SOURCE: Page_84]

76 [SOURCE: Page_84]

account, and how the model parameters are set (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014b), e.g. the amount of PHU of a crop. Asseng et al. (2013) performed a multi-model uncertainty analysis of crop responses to climate change. It pointed out that a greater share of the uncertainty of yield predictions lies rather within the variations among crop models than within the variations among climate models. Further work would be necessary for performing a sensitivity analysis on the eco-efficiency ratios by using an ensemble of crop models, e.g. pDSSAT or GEPIC, similarly to the Agricultural Model Inter-comparison and Improvement Project (AGMIP) (Rosenzweig et al., 2018), in the methodology of this study, in order to compute confidence intervals of eco-efficiencies projections. Applying these different models to real case studies would also allow their calibration and validation, therefore reducing uncertainties about crop yields. [SOURCE: Page_85]

There are also limitations related to the nature of AquaCrop itself, which only models water as a limiting factor on production. As it does not consider the nitrogen and phosphorus cycles, it is not possible to model fertilizer needs and field emissions. As a result, fertilizer needs are estimated each year without taking into account the potential residues from the previous year. However, crop rotations are practices that can reduce fertilizer inputs, and thus the environmental impact of crops (Brankatschk and Finkbeiner, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_85]

Thirdly, climate data has not been downscaled to the territory scale. The latter could lead to uncertainties in the crop simulations (Pasquel et al., 2022) as well as in the LCI of the studied land planning scenarios. In addition, the spatial variability of other regionalized impact categories than WS, e.g. terrestrial acidification, was not considered and CFs at the global resolution were selected. It would be more relevant to use native or country values of CFs for regionalized impact categories, in particular the ones affecting ecosystem quality, i.e. acidification, eutrophication and land use. Indeed, the variability of these spatialized CFs is higher than variability between the CFs of different elementary flows (e.g. emissions of substances or consumption of natural resources) (Bulle et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_85]

Taking other environmental effects into account [SOURCE: Page_85]

The goal of this work was to consider the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural perimeters in the T-LCA framework. As such, other environmental effects that influence yields, e.g. tropospheric ozone (Mbow et al., 2019) or modifications in the areas of pest and disease (Niero et al., 2015a), were not taken into account in this paper. In addition, change of agricultural activities linked to famers’ behavior having effects on the performance of agricultural area, e.g. green consciousness (Marvuglia et al., 2022), were also not considered. One of the main assumptions made in this paper was that water for irrigation remains unlimited over the entire timeframe of the study. This is [SOURCE: Page_85]

77 [SOURCE: Page_85]

debatable, since water availability can obviously change in the future (Leão et al., 2019b). This would limit possibilities for water withdrawal for irrigation, therefore modifying the environmental performance of agricultural perimeters. Further studies should therefore make allowance for the local effects of water availability on agricultural perimeters, while taking into account both rainwater and irrigation water on agricultural perimeters (Falkenmark and Rockström, 2006). These effects could be considered by coupling T-LCA and integrated modelling of the interactions between society and environment (Rugani et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_86]

### 4. Conclusion

This study aimed at considering at any location or moment in time the spatio-temporal dynamics of the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios of agricultural areas. To that end, an original methodology based on the coupling between the crop model AquaCrop and the T-LCA framework was built. The methodology was applied on rainfed and irrigated perimeters at six contrasted locations in terms of climate and soils, i.e. Australia, China, France, India, Russia and the USA. This led to the following conclusion: it is important to take into account the effects of climate change on the performance of agricultural areas as they affect their eco-efficiencies and the results of the comparison between rainfed and irrigated land planning scenarios as a function of the year, the location and the impact category. A sensitivity analysis was performed on key parameters of the study, highlighting the importance of including prospective electricity mixes in the LCI for the calculation of eco-efficiencies. Finally, further work is needed for considering the effects of climate change on water availability as irrigation was considered unlimited in this paper. [SOURCE: Page_86]

78 [SOURCE: Page_86]

Chapter IV: Considering the effects of climate change on the environmental performance                       of         agricultural                   territories irrigated with reservoirs Chapter III confirms that the effects of climate change are important to take into account when assessing the environmental performance of long-term land planning scenarios for agricultural areas. To this end, a methodology is developed based on a coupling between the AquaCrop crop model and the T-LCA framework, in order to build dynamic and regionalized LCIs. The results in Chapter III report that it is important to consider the trade-offs between the assessment years, i.e. the conclusion of the comparison of the performance of land planning scenarios can change over time, in addition to the trade-offs between territorial functions and impact categories. Furthermore, it is required to take account of the spatial context as the effects of climate change are very specific to each location. Depending on the location of the study and the impact category, irrigation may increase or decrease eco-efficiency over time. In addition, the results highlight the presence of a nexus between food, water and energy over the long term. Irrigation allows to produce more biomass than under rainfed conditions, at the cost of increased water and energy consumption. At the end, for certain locations and impact categories, the T-LCA conclusions may change over time, i.e. in some years the most favourable scenario is the irrigated scenario, while in other years it is the rainfed scenario. In Chapter III, no water constraints are taken into account as the water is imported using an IBWT without restriction on available quantities. This is a limitation, however, as climate change can also affect water availability. [SOURCE: Page_87]

Chapter IV aims to address this limitation by improving the methodology of Chapter III to take account of the effects of climate change on local water availability. The methodology is applied to the same six locations as in Chapter III. The hydraulic structure considered in this chapter is an AR with a finite volume of 700,000 m^3 filled by local surface water. It can supply water to a cultivated area of 235 ha. Chapter IV is ready for submission to Science of the Total Environment. [SOURCE: Page_87]

### 1. Introduction

Irrigation and the implementation of local hydraulic infrastructure are one of the main adaptation strategies available to agricultural territories, in a context of climate change (Belmin et al., 2023). Climate change is a direct threat on cultivated areas as it affects water availability and crop water needs (Zhu et al., 2021) by changing precipitation patterns and increasing the incidence of extreme events such as droughts (Mbow et al., 2019). To cope with water stress and secure water resources, two main types of hydraulic infrastructures can be distinguished according to the origin of water (Leão et al., 2019b). The first one consists in importing water resources from low stress locations (Piao et al., 2010), and the second is based on the temporal storage of local water resources (Lardy et al., 2016). For the latter, Agricultural Reservoirs (ARs) can be built to secure the water supply of cultivated areas from the rainfall variability induced by climate change (McCartney and Smakhtin, 2010). AR is an irrigation infrastructure that increases water management flexibility, i.e. the ability to cope with and to adapt to climate change in an economically profitable and timely way (Difrancesco and Tullos, 2014), at the local level (Iglesias and Garrote, 2015). For their filling, the ARs are either relying on groundwater, on surface water streams of the watershed, or on stormwater runoffs (Lardy et al., 2016). The filling is performed during the high flow period, to be used during the low flow period, therefore not impacting low water discharges (Habets et al., 2014). Quantitative environmental assessment tools are required because the implementation of hydraulic infrastructures have important impacts on the environment (Muñoz et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_89]

Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) is a well-established and recognized methodology to quantify the environmental impacts of products and services (ISO, 2020a, 2020b). It has been widely applied to assess the environmental impacts of irrigated systems (Kalboussi et al., 2022), with few case studies on ARs. Ghimire et al. (2014) compared the environmental performance of an AR harvesting rainwater with groundwater pumping to irrigate maize. System boundaries stop at the water supply gate without considering the end user, i.e. the farmer, thus not taking into account the benefits offered by irrigation, such as increasing agricultural productivity (McCartney and Smakhtin, 2010) and maintaining yields (Reidsma et al., 2009) thus preserving farm income. The LCA was pursued further by Yuguda et al. (2020), as they included the agricultural area, i.e. irrigated sugarcane, as well as the water supply with an AR in the assessment of the production of 1 kg of sugar. However, they did not consider other functions provided by irrigation such as the socio-economic benefits linked to yield conservation. [SOURCE: Page_89]

Rogy et al. (2022) went a step further by applying the Territorial LCA (T-LCA) approach to quantify the environmental performance of an entire agricultural area irrigated with an AR. T- [SOURCE: Page_89]

81 [SOURCE: Page_89]

LCA allows considering the multifunctionality of territorial systems by quantifying two types of indicators, i.e. a set of services provided by the territory under study with a given land planning scenario and the induced environmental impacts (Loiseau et al., 2013). These two indicators are combined in a single metric, the eco-efficiency, which is a ratio between the services provided and the environmental impacts (Seppälä et al., 2005). The agricultural territory studied by Rogy et al. (2022) included different crops, i.e. wheat, maize, grapevines and fallow lands. In addition, they considered three main services, i.e. economic profit, food production and land management and occupation. This study was made for a given year, without taking into account climate change and its possible effects on the environmental performance of the land planning scenario. This is a limitation because climate change can affect crop yields and management (Mbow et al., 2019). In addition, they also affect the functioning of AR, e.g. higher amount of evaporation of the water stored (McCartney and Smakhtin, 2010). If climate change reduces the local amount of precipitation, then the inflow to the AR is decreased and less water is stored in the AR for the cultivated area (McCartney and Smakhtin, 2010). The multiplication of ARs in the same watershed to adapt to climate change also has significant cumulative effects on local hydrology, i.e. it reduces local river flows to a greater extent in dry years than in wet ones (Habets et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_90]

To address the effects of climate change on crop yield and water management, Rogy et al. (2024) proposed a methodology based on a coupling between a crop model, AquaCrop (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a), and T-LCA. It has been applied to quantify the annual eco- efficiencies of land planning scenarios including or excluding irrigation infrastructure for cultivated areas for the Shared Socioeconomic Pathways 5-8.5 (SSP$_5$-8.5) climate scenario developed by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) on six contrasted locations around the world. In this study, it was assumed that there were no water constraints, water being supplied by inter-basin transfer from a location with no stress on resources, i.e. where the quantity of water available per inhabitant or per hectare of arable land is high (Yang and Pang, 2006). However, in the case of AR filled with local surface water, this hypothesis is not valid. [SOURCE: Page_90]

This paper aims to make a proof of concept of the necessity of accounting for the effects of climate change on hydrology, on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios including an AR for agricultural territories. To this end the proposed model of Rogy et al. (2024) is refined by integrating a module based on a hydrological model to account for the effects of climate change on local water availability. Hydrological models are used for computing the input flows of the ARs, i.e. the local water streams, as a function of meteorological variables affected by climate change, e.g. precipitation and reference evapotranspiration (Biglarbeigi, [SOURCE: Page_90]

82 [SOURCE: Page_90]

2019). The methodological developments are implemented to theoretical case studies in different spatial contexts. [SOURCE: Page_91]

The original methodology based on a coupling between a crop model, a hydrological model and the T-LCA framework is first described. Then the methodology is implemented on six contrasted locations to compare the spatio-temporal eco-efficiency dynamics of land planning scenarios with or without irrigation. Finally, a discussion on the main assumptions and perspectives of the approach is provided. [SOURCE: Page_91]

### 2. Material and methods

Goal and scope of the study [SOURCE: Page_91]

The methodological developments are applied on two land planning scenarios, i.e. a rainfed scenario and a scenario irrigated with an AR, over a theoretical agricultural area of 235 ha. The two scenarios are studied in the context of the most intense climate change. To this end, the worst-case scenario in terms of greenhouse gas emissions according to the IPCC is selected, i.e. the SSP$_5$-8.5. Climate data at a spatial resolution of 0.25° x 0.25° was extracted from the simulations results available in the NASA Earth Exchange Global Daily Downscaled Projections (NEX-GDDP-CMIP$_6$) (Thrasher et al., 2022, 2021). Both historical simulations (1950 – 2014) and future simulations (2015 – 2100) are provided by this project. The environmental performance of the scenarios are assessed between 2021 and 2099, i.e. the simulation period, in six different territorial contexts, i.e. the United States of America (USA) (state of Colorado), India (state of Gujarat), Russia (Krasnodar krai), France (Occitanie region), China (Henan province) and Australia (state of Victoria). These locations were chosen as they have different climates, soil properties and electricity mixes, as well as because they represent important shares of crop production of their respective countries (U.S. Department of Agriculture - National Agricultural Statistics Service, 2019). In this study, outputs from the Global Circulation Model (GCM) IPSL-CM6A-LR were selected, for the scenario SSP$_5$-8.5. For the boundaries of the system, a cradle-to-territorial gate perspective is adopted (Nitschelm et al., 2016). Therefore, only the impacts induced by the imports of goods and services and the impacts directly generated by the agricultural activities of the studied territory are taken into account. [SOURCE: Page_91]

The application of the methodological developments of this chapter in the different locations will meet two objectives. Firstly, to capture the effects of climate change, including the local change of water availability, on the environmental performance of the irrigated scenarios. Secondly, to compare the performance of the irrigated and the rainfed scenarios over time to see if the results of the comparisons are changing over the long-term. [SOURCE: Page_91]

83 [SOURCE: Page_91]

General approach of the study [SOURCE: Page_92]

Figure 17 presents the methodology used in the study following the general framework of T- LCA. Firstly, a land planning scenario is defined. Secondly, at the heart of the methodology, a coupling of models between a crop model and a hydrological model allows to build the dynamic and regionalized Life Cycle Inventory (LCI). To model the operation of the AR in the studied territory, two steps are necessary (Habets et al., 2018): 1) the amount of water available for human activities in the territory is estimated with a hydrological model, i.e. the inflow to the AR, then, and 2) a water balance is performed on the AR. This modeling is further coupled with a crop model to calculate the crop water requirements, which are needed for the water balance of AR, and the crop yields, as well as with LCA databases to construct the LCI. For the rainfed scenario, only the crop model is necessary for building the LCI. Thirdly, both the impacts of the land planning scenario and the amount of edible biomass produced are evaluated, i.e. the only service provided by the territory considered in this study. Finally, eco-efficiency ratios are computed to study the environmental performance of the scenario. [SOURCE: Page_92]

The overall approach was carried out with the R software (v^4.1.2) (RC Team, 2013) and the packages ncdf^4 (Pierce, 2021), chron (James and Hornik, 2020), xlsx (Dragulescu, 2014), readxl (Wickham and Bryan, 2022), pracma (Borchers, 2021) and tidyverse (Wickham et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_92]

Figure 17 General approach used in the study to take into account the effects of climate change, including the changes of water availability, on the environmental performances of land planning scenarios. The latter are evaluated with the Territorial Life Cycle Assessment (T-LCA) framework. The blue boxes display the refinement of the proposed model of Rogy et al. (2024), i.e. the inclusion of hydrological modeling in the proposed model. [SOURCE: Page_92]

84 [SOURCE: Page_92]

Design of the land planning scenarios [SOURCE: Page_93]

The rainfed scenario consists entirely of wheat crops. The scenario irrigated with an AR allows for more crop diversity (Shahpari et al., 2021). The irrigated scenario is thus a perimeter occupied by 60% of wheat and 40% of maize. All data for the LCI of the AR infrastructure come from Rogy et al. (2022). As shown in Figure 18, the AR is filled through diverting a share of a water stream in the watershed of the agricultural territory. To this end, the AR is plugged at the outlet of a sub-unit of the watershed in which the agricultural territory is located, i.e. a sub- watershed. In addition, as described in its technical report, the AR has a maximum filling rate of 2500 m^3.h-1. In this study, it is assumed that the AR was designed for the 2000-2010 period to be able to irrigate an area of 235 ha occupied with the crop occupation of the irrigated scenario. It is assumed that the AR can be emptied entirely through a pumping station. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Figure 18 Description of both land planning scenarios, i.e. the rainfed scenario is on the left, and the irrigated scenario is on the right. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Modeling the water flows of the territory [SOURCE: Page_93]

Figure 19 describes the different water flows of the territory. The assessment of the latter will both allow computing the amount of water available for crops as well as calculating the amount of water consumption over the territory, i.e. due to evapotranspiration, product integration, or [SOURCE: Page_93]

85 [SOURCE: Page_93]

discharge into other basins and the sea (Boulay et al., 2020). The latter is therefore the sum of the evaporation over the AR and the evapotranspiration over the irrigated area due to irrigation. To this end, at first, a hydrological model is used to compute the flow rate of the water stream on which AR is relying for its filling. The choice of the model is explained below. Secondly, it is required to compute environmental flow requirement (EFR), i.e. the water needs of ecosystems, as the filling of the AR cannot be done at the sake of the water needs of the ecosystems (Habets et al., 2014). Thirdly, these two outputs are used to calculate the amount of water available for human activities by substracting the EFR from the flow rate of the water stream on which the AR is relying for its filling. It is assumed that irrigation is the only human activity taking place over the share of the sub-watershed supplying the AR. Fourthly, a water balance is performed on the AR. Finally, a water balance is performed over the irrigated area. The steps 1 to 5 are applied during two distinct period, i.e. during the design period to calculate the area of the sub-watershed supplying the AR with water and during the simulation period. As described in section 2.2, the crop model is used to compute both the irrigation of step 4 and the water flows of step 5. [SOURCE: Page_94]

Figure 19 Overview of the different water flows of the territory. Each of the flows are affected by climate change. The consumption of water due to human activities are displayed in red. For the evapotranspiration over the irrigated scenario, only a share of it is due to irrigation, i.e. human activities, whereas the remaining is due to rain, i.e. natural. The steps 1 to 5 are applied both during the design period of the AR and during the simulation period [SOURCE: Page_94]
