### 2.4.1. Flow rate of the water stream on which the AR is plugged

The flow rate of the water stream on which the AR is plugged needs to be calculated at a daily timestep and at the local scale. In addition, the local climate, e.g. precipitation and reference evapotranspiration, should be considered in the calculations in order to account for the effects of climate change. This can be achieved by using a hydrological model, i.e. “set of mathematical functions transforming meteorological variables, typically rainfall and reference evapotranspiration, to which a watershed is subjected, into a flow at its outlet” (Lerat, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_94]

86 [SOURCE: Page_94]

The choice of hydrological model used in this study for modeling the flow rate of the water stream on which the AR is plugged, is based on the following requirements. First, the model should be scientifically recognized and parsimonious, i.e. obtaining reasonable results while having a small amount of input data required (Shin and Kim, 2017). Furthermore, it needs to be applicable in any locations for various climatic conditions, e.g. arid or temperate climates, for providing the amount of water runoff at the outlet of a watershed, i.e. the water stream on which the AR is plugged, with a daily timestep. [SOURCE: Page_95]

There is a wide range of hydrological models with various mathematical structures and levels of spatial discretization (Sitterson et al., 2018). The mathematical structure can be empirical, i.e. based on non-linear relationship, conceptual, i.e. based on simplified equations representing the hydrology of the watershed, or physical, i.e. based on physical laws. For the level of spatial discretization, it can be lumped, semi-distributed or distributed. For lumped representations, the entire watershed is considered as one homogenous entity with average parameters for the entire area. On the contrary, spatial variability is taken into account in semi- distributed and distributed representations, where the watershed is either discretized in homogeneous subzones or entirely discretized. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Based on these requirements, the model GR4J (Perrin et al., 2003), a widely used (Karki et al., 2023) lumped model with both conceptual and empirical mathematical structures has been selected. GR4J represents the hydrology of the watershed with two modules, i.e. a production module and a routing module (Perrin, 2000). The production module calculates the amount of water runoff. It consists in a production store, which allows computing the net amount of rainfall participating in the runoff, as well as an intercatchment groundwater exchange function, describing water imports or exports to nearby watersheds (Le Moine et al., 2008). The routing module allows for taking into account the time lag between rainfall events and water runoffs. In that module, a share of the net amount of rainfall participate in quick flow while the remaining part, i.e. the slow flow, goes through a routing store (Le Moine et al., 2008). GR4J is a parsimonious model representing a watershed with only four parameters. As such, GR4J includes two production parameters, related to the production module, i.e. x^1 [mm] the maximum capacity of the production store, x^2 [mm] the groundwater coefficient, and two routing parameters, related to the routing module, i.e. x^3 [mm] the one day ahead maximum capacity of the routing store and x^4 [day] the time base unit of the hydrograph. GR4J performs well in various climatic conditions, i.e. it has been tested in 429 watersheds in the world (Perrin et al., 2003), and in data-deficient conditions (Kumari et al., 2021). In addition, GR4J has already been used to project runoffs in a changed climate (Chazot et al., 2012). The inputs of the model consist in local daily climate variables per unit of watershed area, i.e. reference evapotranspiration and precipitation. The output of GR4J is the daily runoff at the outlet of a [SOURCE: Page_95]

87 [SOURCE: Page_95]

watershed per unit of watershed area. This will permit the calculation of the sub-watershed area supplying the AR with water. [SOURCE: Page_96]

The parameters of GR4J need to be calibrated with daily observed data, i.e. runoff at the outlet of the watershed, reference evapotranspiration and precipitation. However, there is hardly any databases compiling large samples of observed runoffs at the outlets of watersheds at a global scale (Mathevet et al., 2020). Therefore, the four parameters of GR4J were assumed the same in each one of the studied locations. To this end, the median values of GR4J parameters obtained for the 429 tested watersheds were selected, i.e. x^1 = 350 [mm], x^2 = 0 [mm], x^3 = 90 [mm] and x^4 = 1.7 [day] (Perrin et al., 2003). These parameters were chosen as they lead to satisfying approximations of the runoffs at the outlets of the tested watersheds without calibration (Perrin, 2000). Therefore, only the effects of climate change on the runoffs will be considered, thus excluding the effects of regional characteristics such as topography and soil types. The GR4J model used in this study is available in the R package airGR (Coron et al., 2022, 2017). The reference evapotranspiration is estimated with the climate data and FAO Penman-Monteith equation (Allen et al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_96]

### 2.4.2. Water needs of ecosystems

EFR per unit of watershed area, i.e. daily runoff ensuring fair conditions to the ecosystems, were calculated based on the Variable Monthly Flow (VMF) method of Pastor et al. (2014), with the mean annual and monthly runoffs per unit of watershed area of the reference period of 1950-1980. This period was selected as a proxy of the climate unaltered by human activities, because the temperatures are more or less stable before 1980 (IPCC, 2021a), therefore adapted for estimating pristine runoffs. Depending on the value of mean monthly runoffs compared to mean annual runoff, the month is characterized as high-flow month, intermediate month or low-flow month. The EFR are thus shares of the mean monthly runoffs, i.e. 0.3, 0.45 and 0.6 respectively for high-flow month, intermediate month and low-flow month. It is assumed that the EFR are constant during both the design period and simulation period, for the original ecosystems to be affected minimally by flow alteration. [SOURCE: Page_96]

---

### 2.4.3. Water balance model of the AR

The water balance of the AR usually consists in four input flows, i.e. filling from the water stream, precipitations falling directly in the reservoir, stormwater runoffs from the area around the reservoir (not part of the water stream), and subsurface flows (Lardy et al., 2016). The water balance has also four output flows, i.e. evaporation, irrigation, EFR and overflow of excess water (Lardy et al., 2016). In the present work, the EFR is already taken into account in the filling of the reservoir. Subsurface flows and stormwater runoffs from the area around the reservoir were not considered as it is assumed that reservoir bottom is waterproof, and the [SOURCE: Page_96]

88 [SOURCE: Page_96]

AR only relies on a water stream for its filling. The daily water balance over the AR is displayed in Eq.2 and further details are available in section I of the Annex III. The area of the sub- catchment filling the AR is the one allowing the filling of the AR at least 8 years out of 10 (U. S. Department of Agriculture, 2005) at the last month of the high-flow season during the design period. The last month of the high-flow season is the last month for which the mean monthly runoff at the outlet of the sub-watershed is superior to 80% of the mean annual runoff (Pastor et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_97]

𝑉(𝑡 + 1) = 𝑉(𝑡) + ∆𝑡 ∗ (𝑃(𝑡) + 𝐹(𝑡) − 𝐸(𝑡) − 𝐼(𝑡) − 𝐸𝑥𝑐(𝑡))                  (2) [SOURCE: Page_97]

With 𝑡 [day] the day of the assessment; ∆𝑡 [day] the timestep, i.e. 1 day; 𝑉 [m^3] the amount of water in the AR; 𝑃 [m^3.day-1] the amount of precipitations; 𝐹 [m^3.day-1] the amount of water filling the AR; 𝐸 [m^3.day-1] the amount of water evaporated from AR surface; 𝐼 [m^3.day-1] the amount of water used for irrigation; 𝐸𝑥𝑐 [m^3.day-1] the excess water in the reservoir, i.e. the water overflowing the reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_97]

### 2.4.4. Filling of the AR and evaporation

The filling of the AR is done with the amount of water available for human activities. As such, the AR is filled continuously whenever water is available, with a maximum rate equal to the maximum filling rate of the AR. The evaporation from the surface of the AR is assumed to be a fraction of the reference evapotranspiration, i.e. 0.6 (Lardy et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_97]

---

- Parent_Topic: 5239198

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

### 2.4.5. Amount of water used for irrigation

As described in the section 2.2, the amount of water for irrigation is calculated based on crop water requirements. The latter are calculated with the crop model AquaCrop. It is a widely used crop model (Zeleke and Nendel, 2020), which considers the water biogeochemical cycle as well as the effects of climate, soil and agricultural management including irrigation on crop growth at a daily timestep. The same methodology as in Rogy et al. (2024) was used in this study to estimate the following inputs of AquaCrop: the soil parameters, the crops non- conservative parameters as well as the initial conditions of the simulations. For the agricultural management, fertilization and weeds are managed optimally and there is no mulching nor field surface practices changing the amount of surface runoff over the agricultural perimeter, e.g. soil bunds. In this paper, it is assumed that crops require water on two distinct occasions. Firstly, when 100% of the readily available water (RAW) in the soil is depleted, i.e. the water that can be extracted without stress (Raes et al., 2018). The quantity of water required by crops at this time is their maximal evapotranspiration (Di Paolo and Rinaldi, 2008), i.e. the amount of water to achieve full production in well wetted soils under the given climatic conditions (Allen et al., 1998). Secondly, crops also require water on their sowing days for their germination, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_97]

89 [SOURCE: Page_97]

germination cannot be triggered if the soil contains less than 20% of the total amount of water that it can hold (Raes et al., 2018). If crops cannot germinate, the requirements are the missing quantity of water for reaching the germination threshold. The daily crop water requirements are assessed for both design and simulation periods while assuming that there are no water constraints. These requirements are then used to compute the amount of water used for irrigation, which is constrained by the amount of water available in the AR. To this end, when there is not enough water in the AR to satisfy both crops requirements in a context of no water contraints, no water is supplied to the agricultural perimeter and crops suffer from water stress leading to lower yields. [SOURCE: Page_98]

### 2.4.6. Water balance over the irrigated area

The water balance on the irrigated area is performed during the simulation period. The evapotranspiration linked to irrigation is calculated with the same methodology as in Rogy et al. (2024). The reason is that AquaCrop does not differentiate the amount of evapotranspiration that is due to irrigation, i.e. human activities, or precipitations, i.e. natural. [SOURCE: Page_98]

LCI of agricultural activities and irrigation [SOURCE: Page_98]

The LCI related to agricultural activities, i.e. fertilizers and pesticides use and emissions, amount of seeds as well as mechanical works, are constructed with the methodology of Rogy et al. (2024). Therefore, fertilizers are assumed to be supplied to reach the maximum annual yield achievable by the crops depending on the climate and soil of the studied location. The LCI related to irrigation, electricity consumption and infrastructure of the AR are computed with data from Rogy et al. (2022). The actual electricity mix is adapted to each location using the Ecoinvent version 3 database (Wernet et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_98]

Performance indicators and LCIA impact methods [SOURCE: Page_98]

The production of edible biomass by a scenario in a given year is assessed with the yields calculated by AquaCrop. [SOURCE: Page_98]

The IMPACT World+ method (Bulle et al., 2019) at the midpoint level (version 1.29 of IMPACT World+) was used to assess the environmental impacts of each of the land planning scenarios as it includes the latest consensual development in terms of impact assessment, such as the USEtox model (Rosenbaum et al., 2008) or the AWARE model (Boulay et al., 2018). As explained in Rogy et al. (2024), endpoint indicators were not selected as they are more uncertain that midpoint (Hauschild et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_98]

90 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Sensitivity analyses [SOURCE: Page_99]

Sensitivity analyses were performed on the amount of water available to fill the AR, because it affects the yields simulated by AquaCrop and thus the environmental performance of the irrigated scenario. As shown in Table 6, this amount of water can be affected by the methodology used for calculating the EFR and by the GR4J parameters chosen for calculating the amount of runoff at the outlet of the watershed. ### 3. Results

Eleven year moving averages, i.e. year – 5 to year + 5, are presented in this section because in agricultural statistics 10 years is a common timespan for comparison (Agreste, 2021). Firstly, the section 3.1 presents the relative changes of eco-efficiency ratios of a given year with respect to the year 2026 (i.e. the reference year), for the irrigated scenarios. Secondly, the section 3.2 presents the relative change of eco-efficiency ratios of the rainfed scenario with respect to the irrigated scenario. Finally, the section 3.3 uses the relative change of the results [SOURCE: Page_99]

of the sensitivity analysis applied to the irrigated scenarios with respect to the original modelling of the irrigated scenario. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Evolution of the irrigated scenarios eco-efficiency ratios [SOURCE: Page_100]

The different tendencies for the evolutions of eco-efficiency ratios of Figure S. 25 of the Annex III can be described by the evolutions related to three midpoint indicators, i.e. climate change long term (CClt), water scarcity (WS) and freshwater eutrophication (FE). CClt indicator was selected as agriculture is an important global driver of climate change (Rockström et al., 2020). The latter two midpoint indicators were selected based on the correlation coefficients of Table S. 25 of Annex III, which show that their evolutions are the least correlated with CClt, i.e. 0.86 for WS and 0.63 for FE, whereas for the other indicators the correlation coefficient are superior to 95%. The tendencies of the evolutions of eco-efficiency ratios are grasped by Table S. 26 of the Annex III, which presents the 95% confidence interval of their linear regression. When this interval includes 0, there is no clear tendency for the evolution of the eco-efficiency ratios. On the contrary, if the interval is positive, the eco-efficiencies are increasing and they are decreasing if the interval is negative. [SOURCE: Page_100]

For CClt, the eco-efficiencies are decreasing in the USA, in India as well as in France. On the contrary, they are increasing in China and the evolution shows no clear tendency in Russia and in Australia. For the USA and for France, the precipitations (in Figure S. 20 of the Annex III) are decreasing, which, coupled with increases of crop water demand (in Figure S. 22 of the Annex III), lead to more irrigation requirements. As shown in Figure S. 24 of the Annex III, the increases in irrigation requirement cannot be satisfied as the amount of water available at the end of the filling period in the AR is decreasing in these locations. This is due to fewer precipitations leading to fewer runoffs for filling the AR. The yields of both the USA and France in Figure S. 23 of the Annex III are therefore diminishing, leading to the drops of eco- efficiencies. For India, there is a decrease of eco-efficiency even if the yields have the slight tendency to increase. Therefore, the increase of biomass produced over the territory cannot counter the increasing impacts induced by both fertilizers and of the energy use for irrigation for satisfying the crops needs. For China, the eco-efficiencies are increasing because of AR being full most of the years and the increasing yields due to CO$_2$ fertilization, i.e. the increase of the photosynthesis rate due to elevated concentration of CO$_2$ in the atmosphere (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a), which counter both the impacts of energy and fertilizer supplies. In Russia, the yields and the impacts are increasing at the same rate, hence not leading to an evolution of eco-efficiency ratios. For Australia, the increasing of yields cannot be supported anymore by irrigation after 2086 because the amount of water in the AR is dropping. Therefore, the eco-efficiency ratios have no clear tendency of evolution. [SOURCE: Page_100]

92 [SOURCE: Page_100]

For WS, the eco-efficiencies are decreasing in the USA, in India as well as in France, increasing in China and in Australia and showing no clear tendencies in Russia. For the USA and France, it is due to yields not being supported by irrigation due to the filling level of the AR, whereas for India it is due to the increase of yields not being able to counter the impact increase of the water supplied for irrigation. For China and Australia, the increase of eco- efficiency is due to the yields, whereas in Russia the eco-efficiency results from the evolutions of the impacts on WS having no clear tendencies. [SOURCE: Page_101]

For FE, the eco-efficiencies are decreasing in the USA and in India, increasing Russia, and having no clear tendency in China, France and Australia. In each of the location, the amount of phosphorus leaching to the river contributes to more than 80% of the impacts on FE. The leaching is caused by erosion, hence by precipitations. The latter are increasing in China as well as in India and decreasing in France, Australia, the USA and Russia. In the USA, the decrease of eco-efficiency is also due to the decrease of yields. At the end of the simulation period, the increasing yields cannot counter the increasing impacts in China and similarly in France and in Australia, the decreasing of impacts is countered by the decreasing of yields. [SOURCE: Page_101]

93 [SOURCE: Page_101]

94 [SOURCE: Page_102]

The x-axis is the relative change of eco-efficiency ratios of the irrigated scenario with respect to the year 2026. The y-axis is the year. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Comparison between the irrigated and rainfed scenarios eco-efficiency ratios [SOURCE: Page_103]

The relative change of eco-efficiency ratios, for the CClt, FE and WS indicators, of the rainfed scenario with respect to the irrigated scenario are presented in Figure 21. [SOURCE: Page_103]

For Australia, the irrigated scenario is more performant than the rainfed scenario on the CClt and FE indicators and the results are more contrasted for WS. For CClt and FE, the results are explained by the important difference between the yield of the rainfed and the irrigated scenarios, as shown in Figure S. 26 of the Annex III. For WS the irrigated scenario can be more performant than the rainfed scenario, i.e. a minimum difference of -94%, when the difference of yield is important. On the contrary, when the difference of yield is smaller, the rainfed scenario can perform better than the irrigated, i.e. for a maximum difference of 711%, because of the important amount of water supplied, as shown in Figure S. 27 of the Annex III, with a high characterization factor (CF), i.e. high AWARE CF of 87.4 m^3world eq.. [SOURCE: Page_103]

For China, the irrigated scenario performs better than the rainfed for CClt and FE and the results are more contrasted for WS. For the latter, when the difference of yield is important and the amount of water supplied is low, the irrigated scenario can perform better, i.e. at a minimum difference of -34%. The rainfed scenario can also be better than the irrigated scenario, i.e. for a maximum difference of 11%, when the difference of yield is low and the amount of water supply is high. [SOURCE: Page_103]

For France, the rainfed scenario is more performant than the irrigated for WS, and the results are changing for CClt and for FE. For WS, it is due to the increases of yields allowed by irrigation not being important enough to counteract the increase of impacts due to irrigation and fertilizer use. For FE and CClt, the results are changing when the difference of yields between both scenarios is low enough. Moreover, the difference of eco-efficiencies between the rainfed and the irrigated scenarios is increasing from years to years. This is caused by the amount of water available in the AR at the end of the filling season decreasing during the simulation period, as shown in Figure S. 24 of the Annex III, hence leading to a drop in the yields of maize. Therefore, at the end of the simulation period, the yields and eco-efficiencies of the rainfed scenario can be better than the irrigated scenario. [SOURCE: Page_103]

For India and Russia, the irrigated scenario performs better than the rainfed on CClt and FE and the opposite for WS. For the latter, it is also due to the increase of yield not able to counter the increasing impacts due to irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_103]

For the USA, the irrigated scenario is more performant than the rainfed scenario for CClt, FE and WS, because of the important difference of yields between the two scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_103]

95 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Therefore, as in Rogy et al. (2024), the conclusion of the study in a specific location can change over time depending on the midpoint indicator. Comparing the results to the ones of Rogy et al. (2024), the consideration of constrained local water resources leads to important difference of results for the WS indicator in the USA and for the CClt and FE indicators in France. In the USA, the results are different for the WS indicator because of the use of local water resources with a low AWARE CF, i.e. 1.1 m^3world eq, contrarily to the CF of the imported resource, i.e. 63.9 m^3world eq. In France, the results are different for CClt and FE due to the limited amount of water available in the AR for the irrigation of maize. Therefore, it underlines the fact that it is also important to take into account the effects of climate change on water availability, while assessing the eco-efficiencies for making “no regret” decision about a type of land-planning scenario. [SOURCE: Page_104]

### 3.3.1. EFR calculated according to French law

As shown in Figure S. 31 of the Annex III, this change of modeling leads to important improvements of eco-efficiency ratios at the end of the simulation period for the USA, Australia, France and Russia, i.e. respectively to a maximum of 208%, 74%, 36% for land occupation biodiversity (LObio) and 21% for FE. On the contrary, in China and in India, the eco-efficiencies do not improve at the end of the simulation. Nevertheless, there is still an improvement of the eco-efficiency in China, i.e. up to 20%, at the start of the simulation. These results are caused by the increasing amount of water available in the AR, as shown in Figure S. 30 of the Annex III. In each of the location, this amount can increase when the EFR is calculated according to French law because, as shown in Table S. 27 of the Annex III, for month with high and intermediate flows they are always much lower than when the EFR are calculated according to Pastor et al. (2014). As such, more water is available in the watershed for filling the AR, as shown in Figure S. 30 of the Annex III, and more biomass can be produced than in the original modeling, as shown in Figure S. 29 of the Annex III. This change of modeling could also lead to decrease of -14% of the eco-efficiency calculated relatively to the Ionizing Radiation (IR) indicator in France. This is due to the increase of yields allowed by more water for irrigation being lower than the increase of the impacts of the water supply. Indeed in France, water is supplied with an electricity mix constituted of 70% of nuclear energy (Wernet et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_106]

### 3.3.2. High amounts of runoffs produced by the watershed

As shown in Figure S. 33 of the Annex III, this change of modeling also leads to important improvements of eco-efficiency ratios for the USA, Australia, France and Russia, i.e. respectively to a maximum of 208% and 85% for LObio, 60% for Mineral Resource Use (MRU) and 22% for FE. There is also a decrease of eco-efficiencies in France for the IR indicator, i.e. -17%. These results are also due to the increasing amount of water available for filling the AR in Figure S. 32 of the Annex III, as the watersheds produce more runoffs. [SOURCE: Page_106]

### 3.3.3. Low amounts of runoffs produced by the watershed

As presented in Figure S. 36 of the Annex III, this change of modeling leads to lower eco- efficiency in each of the locations to a minimum of -97%, -22%, -16%, -60% for FE as well - 34% and -100% for LObio respectively for the USA, India, China, Russia, France and Australia. As shown in Figure S. 35 of the Annex III, the reduction of runoffs in the watersheds lead to less water available for filling the AR, therefore to the lower yields of Figure S. 34 of the Annex III. A reduction of the runoffs produced by the watersheds can also lead to better eco- efficiencies in France for the IR indicator, i.e. increase of 41%, and in Russia and India for the [SOURCE: Page_106]

98 [SOURCE: Page_106]

WS indicator, i.e. increases of respectively 3% and 9%. This is due to the decrease of yield being less important than the decrease of the water supply impacts caused by less water available for irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_107]

### 4. Discussion

Choice of the hydrological model [SOURCE: Page_107]

This paper showed that it is important to model the water available in a watershed, in order to assess the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios. However, further research is required because, as shown in the section 3.3, the amount of water in the AR is an important variable affecting the environmental performance of the studied irrigated scenarios. In fact, a modification in the amount of water filling the AR either due to a change of EFR or of some watersheds’ parameters lead to important variations of eco-efficiencies compared to the original modeling. Therefore, the calculation of water available for human activities should be one of the main point of focus when conducting environmental assessments in a climate change context. The choice of both climate and hydrological model is also of importance for predicting the water availability in a watershed as different combinations of them could lead to the computation of projected water flows with opposing trends (Zheng et al., 2019). Therefore, to assess the uncertainty of the projected amount of water availability in a context of climate change, an ensemble of climate scenarios, climate models and hydrological models as well as calibration methods for the latter should be used (Lemaitre-Basset et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_107]

In this study, we chose to combine a crop model with a hydrological model, rather than using models encompassing both of these modules. The latter can be semi-distributed or distributed, which increase the spatial relevance of their results. Therefore, instead of using lumped models such as in this study, T-LCA could be coupled with models encompassing multiple modules such as Modelling of socio-Agro-Ecological system for Landscape Integrated Assessment (MAELIA) (Therond et al., 2014) or Soil & Water Assessment Tool + (SWAT+) (Bieger et al., 2017). In their hydrological modules, the latter models account for groundwater dynamics, which could be interesting when studying the environmental performance of a land planning scenario including an AR filled with groundwater. On top of the hydrological and crop growth modules, models with multiple modules can account for other biogeochemical cycles than water, e.g. nitrogen, carbon or phosphorus, as well as management practices such as tillage (Bieger et al., 2017). The outputs of these models could be used for the computation of eco- efficiency ratios with more spatial representativeness. However, these models require an important amount of parameters (Therond et al., 2014), which would be an obstacle for the [SOURCE: Page_107]

99 [SOURCE: Page_107]

present article, because the developed methodology was applied on a variety of spatial contexts. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Representativeness of the impacts on water scarcity [SOURCE: Page_108]

The present article focuses on modeling water flows at the territorial scale. However, the impacts on WS might not be representative enough of the temporality and the scale of the assessment of environmental performance as they are calculated based on annual, non- mechanistic and marginal CF calculated for the year 2010 (Boulay et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_108]

For the temporality of the LCIA, it is important to account for the seasonal changes of the impacts on WS through better modeling of water flows at the scale of the watershed. To this end, it is important to have mechanistic CFs considering the different water compartments, i.e. surface water, groundwater, soil and atmosphere, because withdrawal or release of water in one compartment can propagate in other compartments (Núnez et al., 2018). These propagations could result from environmental interventions in addition to consumptive water use, i.e. land use and land use change as well as water stream use and management (Núnez et al., 2018). For example in the present study, the water consumption and the land use linked to the AR could affect the hydrology of the watershed (Pastor et al., 2022) by increasing the soil moisture due to irrigation (Pierrat et al., 2022) or by reducing the runoffs of the watershed (Habets et al., 2018). The changes in soil moisture can modify the water cycle, e.g. more summer precipitation (Lo and Famiglietti, 2013), due to increased evaporation changing the energy and water balance of the atmosphere. The reduction of water infiltration in the soil due to the AR harvesting precipitations can lead to less groundwater recharge, hence reducing the base flow toward rivers downstreams (Núnez et al., 2018). Further research is therefore needed in the use of mechanistic CF for WS, taking into account the interactions between different water compartments, such as the one proposed by Pierrat et al. (2022). [SOURCE: Page_108]

For the scale of the LCIA, marginal CF were used in this study because the water consumption of the territory was assumed to be small relatively to the water consumption of the human activities already happening on the watershed of the studied territory (Hauschild et al., 2017), i.e. the background situation. However, large-scale intervention can change this background situation, therefore calling for the use of non-marginal CF (Boulay et al., 2020). The latter could be used to assess the environmental performance of a territory with multiple ARs, to account for their cumulative effects on the water available locally (Habets et al., 2018). There is no consensus yet in the LCA community about what is the “non-marginality” threshold of an environmental stressor (Boulay et al., 2020) and about how to model the non-marginality of a CF (Heijungs, 2020), with a recent debate between the use of average or integrative (non- linear) CF (Boulay et al., 2020; Forin, 2020). Nonetheless, a marginal water consumption can [SOURCE: Page_108]

100 [SOURCE: Page_108]

be defined as being less than 5% of the background human consumption of the watershed (Frischknecht et al., 2016). Depending on the size of the studied territory, some of the activities could lead to non-marginal water consumption, e.g. the added evapotranspiration linked to irrigated agriculture. It would therefore be interesting to use the average CF proposed by Boulay et al. (2019) to assess the impacts of these large scale interventions, as using marginal CF may lead to an overestimation of the resulting impacts (Crenna et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_109]

### 5. Conclusion

The methodology developed in this paper enables to take into account the effects of climate change, including on local water availability, on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for cultivated areas including or excluding an AR. It is based on a coupling between a crop model, i.e. AquaCrop, a water balance on the AR, a hydrological model, i.e. GR4J, and the T-LCA framework. This coupling allows computing dynamic and spatialized eco-efficiencies while taking into account the spatial and temporal effects of the effects of climate change on the performance of the studied territories. The effects of climate change affect the performance of a territory and can change the results of the comparison between two land planning scenarios, depending on the location and the midpoint indicators. When conducting environmental assessments of agricultural area in a climate change context, yields and runoffs projections should be main points of focus, as the eco-efficiency ratios are highly sensitive to these variables. This could be done by using semi-distributed or distributed models with multiple modules, e.g. hydrological and crop growth modules. Finally, further research is needed to use scale, i.e. marginal or non-marginal, and temporal representative mechanistic CF for the WS indicator to model water flows between all territorial water compartments. [SOURCE: Page_109]

101 [SOURCE: Page_109]

102 [SOURCE: Page_110]

Chapter V: Maintaining the environmental performance of cultivated areas in a context of climate change, effects of crop choice and irrigation Chapter IV shows that it is important to take into account the effects of climate change on water availability, in addition to the other effects of climate change, when assessing the performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural areas over the long-term. In most cases, irrigation can improve the environmental performance of the planning scenarios studied. However, in some years there is not enough water in the reservoir to support crop yields, which generally results in the irrigated scenario performing less well than the rainfed scenario. This is the case in France, for example. Irrigation is not the only strategy for coping with climate change. Crop selection is also important to produce crops that are adapted to rising temperatures and decreasing water availability. In Chapters III and IV, the environmental performance of land planning scenarios is calculated over the long term, and their moving average are analyzed. In addition to the average, it is also necessary to study other criteria describing the evolution of eco-efficiency, for example their variability. This would make it possible to identify a land planning scenario that maintains environmental performance over time in a context of climate change. [SOURCE: Page_111]

In order to address these research gaps, Chapter V aims to develop a methodology to explore the environmental performance of a variety of land planning scenarios for climate change adaptation, i.e. crop occupation and irrigation management. The average and variability of eco- efficiencies will be studied in order to formulate recommendations about a land planning scenario that maintains the environmental performance over time of a 700 ha agricultural territory located in the south of France, in a context of climate change. This agricultural territory can be equipped with an IBWT. Chapter V is ready for submission in The International Journal of Life Cycle Assessment. [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 1. Introduction

Territories are mesoscale systems associating a geographical space with the stakeholders who develop, manage and exploit it (Moine, 2006), and Territorial Life Cycle Assessment (T- LCA) is an adaptation of the standard LCA framework to assess the environmental performance of them Loiseau et al. (2013). T-LCA can be used to perform a territorial diagnostic, by identifying the impact hotspots of a territory (Loiseau et al., 2014), or to compare the performance of different land planning scenarios for the same territory (Borghino et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_113]

New agriculture production methods are required to reduce their environmental impact, and to adapt them to climate change (Whitfield et al., 2018). T-LCA has been applied to agricultural territory with this purpose. Rogy et al. (2022) compared the environmental performance of three land planning scenarios including or excluding irrigation structures, which are solutions for adapting cultivated areas to climate change (Belmin et al., 2023). This first approach allowed a prospective assessment of environmental benefits and drawbacks of scenarios. However, this previous study does not grasp the future effects of climate change over the productions, e.g. changes in precipitation, increase of temperature and of CO$_2$ concentration in the atmosphere (Mbow et al., 2019). This is limiting because infrastructures permitting irrigation, e.g. inter basin water transfer or agricultural reservoir, have long lifetimes that can reach 50 years (Raluy et al., 2005b) and their operations will be affected by climate change (Biglarbeigi, 2019). To meet this research gap, T-LCA has been successfully coupled with biophysical models, i.e. crop and hydrological models, to assess the evolution of eco- efficiencies of land planning scenarios in a climate change context at various locations (Rogy et al., 2024, to be submitted). In the latter two studies, irrigation was the only climate change adaptation solution studied. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Land planning scenarios including other adaptation solutions, such as the cultivation of crops genetically adapted to climate change or the diversification of the crops cultivated (Belmin et al., 2023) need to be assessed. Among these many scenarios, recommendations for an optimal land planning of the territory are expected (Ding et al., 2023). To that end, it is necessary to study the evolution of the environmental performance of a scenario in depth through criteria of dynamics, such as its mean and stability, in order to gain knowledge about how well the scenario performs in a climate change context (Peterson et al., 2018). These criteria of dynamics are thus quantifying the resilience of a system (Dardonville et al., 2021), i.e. the ability of a system to ensure the provision of its functions in the face of shocks and stresses (Meuwissen et al., 2019). Therefore, there is a need of a tool allowing the exploration and the comparison of criteria of dynamics related to the environmental performance of a [SOURCE: Page_113]

105 [SOURCE: Page_113]

variety of land-planning scenarios for the adaptation of agricultural territories to climate change. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Such tool could be the Design of Experiment (DOE) framework, which allows exploring an experimental domain. This is done by optimizing the number of experiments to be performed, while postulating a mathematical model of the experimental response, i.e. a metric describing the phenomenon under study, as a function of factors, i.e. possible causes of the response (Gauchi, 2016). This mathematical model can be used to find the factors optimizing the response (Jourdan, 2005). LCA has already been used on par with the DoE framework. Aleisa & Heijungs (2020) used the DoE framework to explore the environmental impacts of different blends of fossil fuels for water desalination, in order to find the blend with the minimal impacts. Corral-Bobadilla et al. (2022) applied the DoE framework to find the process conditions, e.g. reaction temperature and time or stirred speed, at which biodiesel can be produced with the least impacts. These studies showed that the coupling between the DoE framework and LCA is adequate and that it successfully allows exploring different production scenarios to find the optimal scenario in terms of environmental impacts. However, these studies were not applied on territories, nor do they consider the evolution of the environmental performance. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Therefore, this paper aims at closing this research gap by developing an approach that couples DoE with T-LCA and crop modeling. This approach will permit the exploration of criteria of dynamics related to the evolution of the environmental performance of a variety of land planning scenarios in a climate change context. These scenarios will be irrigated or rainfed and including different types of crops. [SOURCE: Page_114]

### 2. Material and methods

Figure 22 Steps performed in order to explore the experimental domain of this study. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Figure 22 presents the steps to follow in order to explore the experimental domain of this study. Firstly, a DoE method must be chosen. Secondly, a mathematical model is postulated and an [SOURCE: Page_114]

106 [SOURCE: Page_114]

optimized number of experiments to perform is chosen. Thirdly, the experiments, i.e. T-LCA coupled with crop modeling, are performed. According to the framework of Loiseau et al. (2013), the steps of T-LCA are (1) to define a territory and an associated land planning scenario; (2) to build the LCI of the production and consumption activities taking place over the territory; (3) to quantify two types of indicators: the amount of services provided by the land planning scenario, e.g. economic profit or provision of jobs, and its environmental impacts; and finally (4) to assess the environmental performance through eco-efficiency ratios, i.e. ratios of the service with the impacts (Seppälä et al., 2005). Fourthly, the coefficients of the mathematical model are evaluated and the model is statistically assessed. If the model is not adequate statistically, another model and experiments must be chosen. Finally, the experimental domain can be explored. The five steps of Figure 22 are described in the following sections. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Case study [SOURCE: Page_115]

A theoretical agricultural area of 700 ha located in the Mediterranean south of France is studied. The question of whether irrigation infrastructure should be put in place to ensure water supply in a context of climate change arises. The studied infrastructure is an Inter-Basin Water Transfer (IBWT) importing water from an unstressed area (Rogy et al., 2022), i.e. an area where the quantity of water available per inhabitant or per hectare of arable land is high (Yang and Pang, 2006). The IBWT consists in a buried pipeline of 100 km supplying water with a pumping station functioning at a 3 m^3.s-1 rate. It is assumed that there is no water leaks in the pipeline and that the IBWT is designed to supply water to an entire region. Therefore, only a share of the impacts of the IBWT is allocated to the territory according to the amount of water for irrigation required by the crops. In terms of land use, this area can be occupied by a variety of crops with different characteristics, e.g. nutrient contents, water needs, yields, energy contents or selling prices. Hence, in this study, four crops are studied, i.e. maize and wheat, which are two of the most cultivated crops globally (FAO, 2021), dry beans, a protein crop (Brick et al., 2022) and sunflower, one of the main oilseed cultivated globally (Pilorgé, 2020). A cradle-to-gate perspective was adopted for the T-LCA, hence excluding the activities linked to the consumption or processing of the agricultural production of the studied territory when they take place outside its boundaries (Nitschelm et al., 2016). The environmental performances of land planning scenarios for the studied territory are assessed over a 30-year period, i.e. from 2026 to 2055, for the worst-case climate scenario according to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), i.e. the SSP$_5$-8.5 scenario. To explore environmental performances of land planning scenarios, the DoE methodology is used. [SOURCE: Page_115]

107 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Design of Experiment [SOURCE: Page_116]

### 2.2.1. Method selection

The choice of the DoE method results from responses variables and types of factors. The responses of the DoE to be observed are related to the environmental performance of a land planning scenario over time. This is quantified with eco-efficiency ratios. A variety of eco- efficiency ratios can be calculated for a land planning scenario and its different environmental impacts, but it is assumed that a land planning scenario for the studied agricultural territory provide four different services, i.e. land management, food production, the provision of calories and economic profit. In addition, if future work could encompass a broader assessment, only impacts on climate change were considered in the present study as agriculture is an important global driver of this impact (Rockström et al., 2020). Two criteria of dynamics were selected to study the evolution of each eco-efficiency ratio, i.e. the level and the variability (Dardonville et al., 2022). First, the level of the eco-efficiency is studied through an average over the timeframe of the study (Dardonville et al., 2022). Second, the variability is assessed through the coefficient of variation of the eco-efficiency (Li et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_116]

Factors of the DoE are linked to the composition of the land planning scenario, i.e. land occupation and irrigation. Firstly, the factors related to land occupation are of continuous nature, i.e. the shares of wheat, maize, sunflower and dry beans in the agricultural area. These factors are constrained by the fact that their sum is equal to one (Goupy, 2000). Therefore, they are called mixing factor. Secondly, the factor linked to irrigation is defined as process factor because it is not a part of the blend composition of the cultivated crops (Gozálvez-Zafrilla et al., 2013). This process factor is assumed to be discrete, as the crops are either rainfed or irrigated. Therefore, the chosen DoE method is a crossed mixture process design (MPD) (Zolgharnein et al., 2017). The latter is a combination between a DoE method encompassing mixing factors with another one encompassing process factors (Czitrom, 1989). [SOURCE: Page_116]

### 2.2.2. Model definition

In this study, eight different DoE are conceived because eight different responses are evaluated, i.e. two criteria of dynamics are evaluated for four eco-efficiency ratios. The mathematical model for each experimental response is postulated, then the experimental design and levels of the factors, i.e. value that the factors will take in each of the experiment (Gauchi, 2016), are chosen. [SOURCE: Page_116]

First, it is postulated that the responses to mixing factors follow a second order model (Czitrom, 1989). Therefore, the experimental design encompassing mixing factors is assumed to be a simplex-centroid design (SCD) with 4 constituents and 15 experiments, i.e. 4 for one- [SOURCE: Page_116]

108 [SOURCE: Page_116]

component mixtures and 11 for 2-component, 3-component and 4-component mixtures equally shared (Goupy, 2000). Second, it is postulated that the responses to the process factor follow a first order model and that the process factor has two levels, i.e. presence or absence of irrigation. Hence, the experimental design encompassing the process factor is assumed to be a two level full factorial design with 2 experiments, i.e. a 21 full factorial design (Czitrom, 1989). Finally, as shown in the section 1.1 of the Annex IV, both of these 2nd order and 1st order models are combined in the 3rd order model with 20 parameters of Eq.3 to express each response as a function of both mixing and process factors (Lawson, 2015). In Eq.3, mixing factors take values between 0 and 1, whereas the process factor is 1 in presence of irrigation and -1 in absence. As shown in Figure 23, the resulting experimental domain has 5 dimensions, i.e. one for each factors. For each DOE, it is necessary to perform the 30 experiments displayed in Table S. 28 of the Annex IV. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Figure 23 Construction of the DoE by combining an experimental design for mixing factors, i.e. Simplex-centroid design with 4 constituents, with an experimental design for process factors, i.e. a 2 1 full factorial design. The resulting design can be described as the experimental design for mixing factors applied on each points of the experimental design for process factors (III.) (Goupy, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_117]

### 2.2.3. Evaluation of the coefficient of the mathematical model

Multilinear regressions are used to evaluate the coefficients of the mathematical model in Eq.3 for each response. After that, the statistical validities of the mathematical models are assessed through analyses of variances (ANOVA). For each of the model, the 30 experiments of the DoE are used to calculate the 20 coefficients, leaving 10 degrees of freedom for the ANOVA. Then, each 20-parameter model is simplified through backward elimination, i.e. coefficients are eliminated one by one while making sure that the model do not lose its predictive characteristics (Derksen and Keselman, 1992). The coefficients with the highest probability to be null according to a partial F-test are removed first, while statistics relative to the overall qualities of models are monitored, i.e. R$_2$, adjusted R$_2$, Fisher’s F, the residual variance and Mallows’ Cp (Goupy, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_118]

The coupling between T-LCA and crop modelling as an experimental tool [SOURCE: Page_118]

The environmental performances of land planning scenarios are computed according to the methodology of Rogy et al. (2024) for each year of the timeframe of the study according to the climate scenario SSP$_5$-8.5 from the IPCC. Climate data was extracted from simulation results of the Global Circulation Model (GCM) IPSL-CM6A-LR, available in the NASA Earth Exchange Global Daily Downscaled Projections (NEX-GDDP-CMIP$_6$) (Thrasher et al., 2022, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_118]

The core of the methodology relies on a coupling between the T-LCA framework and a crop model to calculate dynamic and spatialized Life Cycle Inventory (LCI) for agricultural territories. FAO’s crop model AquaCrop (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a) is used to compute annual data on yields and water needs for the different crops. Other data for computing crop LCIs are found in existing literature and databases. As AquaCrop does not model the nitrogen cycle, nor phosphorus, some assumptions are made to compute data on fertilizer inputs and field emissions. For fertilizers, it is assumed that they are supplied with respect to the nutritional contents of the crops to reach the maximal annual amount of edible biomass that can be produced in unstressed conditions, i.e. no weed, fertilizer or water stresses. Contrarily to the other crops of this study, dry beans have the ability to fixate nitrogen, therefore reducing the required amount of fertilizer (Reinprecht et al., 2020). For this crop, values for the amount of nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium supplies were taken from a LCA study (Tidåker et al., 2021). The LCI of the water infrastructure was built according to the LCI for IBWT of Rogy et [SOURCE: Page_118]

110 [SOURCE: Page_118]

al. (2022). The inventory for fertilizers were sourced from the World Food LCA (Nemecek et al., 2019) and the Ecoinvent (Wernet et al., 2016) databases. The remaining agricultural activities, e.g. mechanical works or pesticides use, are staying at the value of their respective crop LCI in the Agribalyse database as shown in Table S. 29 of the Annex IV (Koch and Salou, 2020). [SOURCE: Page_119]

Four indicators were selected for the functions of the land planning scenarios. Firstly, for land management, the indicator is constant and is the amount of land occupied (LO), i.e. 700 ha. Secondly, for food production, the total mass of edible agricultural products (MAP) that is produced by each land planning scenarios is selected. AquaCrop computes the MAP. Thirdly for provision of calories, it is evaluated with the total amount of food energy produced (PE). The energy contents of the different crops are sourced from Feedipedia (INRAE et al., 2012). For economic profit, it is evaluated with the agricultural turnover (AT) of the territory, calculated with the market prices of crops in France for the year 2019, which are displayed in Table S. 30 of the Annex IV (Agreste, 2022, 2019; Comte, 2019). The impacts of the territory are assessed with the climate change long term (CClt) indicator from the IMPACT World+ methodology (Bulle et al., 2019). In this study, the CClt indicator, assessed with the global temperature change potential in 100 years (GTP100) was chosen, as it is completely correlated with the Climate Change short term (CCst), assessed with the global warming potential over 100 years (GWP100) (Rogy et al., 2024). [SOURCE: Page_119]

### 3. Results and discussion

First, the models of the responses resulting from the DOE methodology are built and statistical analyses are performed to assess their validities over the experimental domain. Finally, the responses are established for the entire experimental domain. In this section, the responses are represented for each 3-component mixtures of the experimental domain, i.e. all the combinations of crops possible on the faces of the tetrahedrons of step III in Figure 23. Each level of eco-efficiency was normalized with the highest value of eco-efficiency, encompassing rainfed and irrigated perimeters, calculated with the same service provided by the land planning scenario. In the case of the variabilities, they were normalized with the maximum variability of all eco-efficiencies combined. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Statistics of models resulting from the DOE methodology [SOURCE: Page_119]

Table 7 presents each model’s coefficients as well as the statistics of the mathematical models. If the coefficients of the models are positive, it means that single factors or their interactions are evolving in the same way as the DoE responses, and the opposite if the coefficients of the models are negative. Some coefficients of the model (in bold with an asterisk) for which the [SOURCE: Page_119]

111 [SOURCE: Page_119]

results of the partial F-tests are superior to 0.05, i.e. more than 5% of chance that the coefficients are null, were kept in the models. The reason is that their removal would lead to worst statistical characteristics of the models, i.e. lower R$_2$ and adjusted R$_2$, higher p-values and residual variances, as well as a Mallows’ Cp higher than the number of models coefficients. Nevertheless, the models have good predictive characteristics. The R$_2$ and adjusted R$_2$ of each model are superior to 95%, highlighting the facts that the models are fitting almost perfectly the responses of the DOE. This is further justified by the small p-values and residual variances of the models. In addition, Mallows’ Cp are lower than the number of coefficients in each of the models, highlighting the fact that the simplified models can have similar statistical properties than the original models. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Levels of eco-efficiency ratios [SOURCE: Page_124]

As shown in Figure 24 and Figure 26 for MAP and PE, the presence of irrigation leads to better level of performance than without irrigation. According to Table 7, this is because the coefficients of the interaction between irrigation and individual crops, i.e. 𝛾1,1 , 𝛾1,2 , 𝛾1,3 and 𝛾1,4 , are all positive. Indeed, there is no water stress when irrigation is supplied to the crops. Therefore, it leads to both higher services of MAP and PE than in rainfed conditions. In irrigated conditions, combinations of irrigated wheat and maize obtain high levels of eco-efficiencies. This is explained by the combination of the important coefficients for the effects of individual crops, i.e. 𝛾0,1 and 𝛾0,2 , and for the interactions between crops and irrigation, i.e. 𝛾1,1 and 𝛾1,2 . In fact, in irrigated conditions, wheat and maize produce the highest yields. Irrigated area containing dry beans and sunflower do not have the highest level of performance because their coefficients are lower than the coefficients of the remaining crops. This is due to the yields of sunflower and dry beans calculated by AquaCrop being much lower than the other crops, i.e. more than 2.5 fold lower than the yields of maize. Dry beans have higher performance than sunflower for MAP due to nitrogen fixation leading to less fertilizer necessary. For PE, sunflower performs better than dry beans because the seeds contain around 1.5 fold energy per weight unit than the other crops (INRAE et al., 2012). When the agricultural area is not irrigated, wheat monoculture leads to the highest levels of eco-efficiency because 𝛾0,2 being much higher than 𝛾1,2 , while the eco-efficiencies are diminishing the more we add other crops. The less reduction of eco-efficiency ratios is achievable by adding dry beans to wheat cropping, while the maximum reduction is achievable by adding maize. For the latter, it is due to 𝛾1,1 having high values, therefore leading to an important diminution of eco-efficiency in absence of irrigation, i.e. when 𝑧1 equals -1. In fact, maize yields are relying heavily on irrigation as maize grows during the dry season, i.e. the summer, contrarily to wheat. [SOURCE: Page_124]

As shown in Figure 25 for LO, the presence of irrigation leads to a worst level of eco-efficiency than the absence of water supply because the coefficients of the interactions between the shares of crops and irrigation are negative. Both irrigated and rainfed scenario produce the same amount of service. Therefore, the difference in the level of eco-efficiencies is due to the presence of IBWT infrastructure as well as to the energy consumed for supplying water for irrigation. In both rainfed and irrigated conditions, the monoculture of dry beans have the best level of environmental performance as dry beans have the highest coefficient for the effect of individual crop. [SOURCE: Page_124]

As shown in Figure 27 for AT, for both in presence or absence of irrigation, the monoculture of dry beans performs much better than the other crops, because of 𝛾0,4 . Indeed, the price of dry [SOURCE: Page_124]

116 [SOURCE: Page_124]

beans is around 5 fold higher per unit of mass than maize and wheat and around 3 fold higher than sunflower. [SOURCE: Page_125]

Variabilities of eco-efficiency ratios [SOURCE: Page_130]

As shown in Figure 28 as well as in Figure S. 37 and Figure S. 38 of the Annex IV, the variabilities of MAP, PE and AT eco-efficiencies are similar because their provided services are both calculated based on crop yields. In the presence of irrigation, the variabilities are low for any combinations of crops. This is due to 𝛾1,1 , 𝛾1,2 , 𝛾1,3 and 𝛾1,4 being negative as well as to their absolute value being close than the value of 𝛾0,1 , 𝛾0,2 , 𝛾0,3 and 𝛾0,4 , therefore countering them. In fact, irrigation allows safeguarding yields of crops in a climate change context (McCartney and Smakhtin, 2010). In the absence of irrigation, combinations of wheat and dry beans have the less variabilities. This is due the low values of 𝛾0,2 and 𝛾0,4 compared to 𝛾0,1 and 𝛾0,3 as well as to the coefficients for the effects of the interaction between dry beans and maize, i.e. 𝛾0,24 and 𝛾1,24, countering the added variability due to the absence of irrigation. The variabilities of the performance are increasing when the share of maize occupying the rainfed agricultural area is increasing. This is also due to the sensitivity of maize to water stress, therefore to the high value of 𝛾0,1 and 𝛾1,1 . The important absolute values of 𝛾0,12 and 𝛾1,12 show that the variability of the performance of maize monoculture can be reduced by diversifying the agricultural area with other crops such as wheat, because crop diversity reduces the variability of yields (Dardonville et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_130]

As shown in Figure 29 for LO, the variability are low for any crops on the agricultural area as well as for the presence and absence of irrigation. This is due to the service provided being constant and to the impact on climate change being mostly driven by the emissions linked to the use of fertilizers, i.e. they contribute to over 50% of the impacts for dry beans and 70% of the impacts for the remaining crops. The latter has a low variability as the fertilizers are supplied to reach the maximum achievable yields in a given year, i.e. without temperature or water stresses, which is stable. [SOURCE: Page_130]

The variabilities were normalized with the maximum variability of the combined MAP, LO and PE eco-efficiencies calculated relatively to the CClt indicator. [SOURCE: Page_131]

Practical implications for decision support [SOURCE: Page_133]

The application of the DoE methodology for the evaluation of the environmental performance of land planning scenarios leads to the following conclusions. Except for the LO eco- efficiencies, irrigation is paramount for increasing the environmental performance of cultivated area in a climate change context. In addition, irrigation stabilize the environmental performance. For MAP and PE in irrigated conditions, maize monoculture is the optimal scenario, whereas it is the monoculture of dry beans for LO and AT. However, these optimal scenarios in terms of environmental performance are not realistic from a hydrologic or agronomic point of view. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Firstly, for the hydrologic point of view, the water resource was supposed to be unconstrained in this study, by assuming that the IBWT is importing water from an unstressed area. However, the precipitation changes induced by climate change in the study area will lead to a reduction in surface water flows (Chauveau et al., 2013) and groundwater recharge (McCartney and Smakhtin, 2010). In France, this reduction of water availability diminishes the environmental performance of agricultural areas including maize (Rogy et al., to be submitted). [SOURCE: Page_133]

Secondly, from an agronomic point of view, the effects of crop rotations were not considered in this paper. Considering crop rotations would increase the levels of environmental performance of land planning scenarios with diversified crops of this chapter. Indeed, introducing legumes in crop rotations, e.g. dry beans, reduce the inputs of fertilizers and the energy use for tillage (Nemecek et al., 2008). [SOURCE: Page_133]

In view of these results, an irrigated area occupied by different crops in rotation, i.e. wheat, maize and dry beans, could be recommended for maintaining MAP, LO, PE and AT environmental performance at a high level. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Perspectives in terms of modeling [SOURCE: Page_133]

Further research is necessary in order to better model nitrogen and phosphorus emissions as well as the services provided by the land planning scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_133]

For the emissions of nitrogen and phosphorus in the environment, they were calculated based on simple models widely used by LCA practitioners, i.e. Agribalyse (Koch and Salou, 2020), World Food LCA Database (Nemecek et al., 2019), Ecoinvent (Nemecek and Schnetzer, 2012) and AGEC-LCI (Viveros Santos et al., 2020). However, these simple models do not account for the dynamics of the nutrients supplied by fertilizers in the agricultural area, e.g. timing between the fertilization and the crop uptake or fertilization beyond the plant needs (Avadí et al., 2022), which affect their related emissions. The nutrients dynamics could be taken into [SOURCE: Page_133]

125 [SOURCE: Page_133]

account by using models considering both the nitrogen and phosphorus cycles effects on crop growth, e.g. the Erosion-Productivity Impact Calculator (EPIC) crop model (Williams et al., 1989). [SOURCE: Page_134]

For the services provided by the territory in this study, the economic profit was assessed through the annual turnover of the territory calculated with constant crops’ selling prices. However, this is a strong hypothesis, as these prices are highly dependent on market mechanisms. Further research is therefore necessary for coupling the methodology of this paper with economic models such as general or partial equilibrium models to compute the prices of crops over the long-term (Valin et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_134]

Assessing other eco-efficiency ratios in this study [SOURCE: Page_134]

Other eco-efficiency ratios could have been calculated based on other indicators of services and of impacts. To assess the service of provision of healthy food, in addition to the energy content, the nutrients contents of the edible biomass produced by the territory could have been assessed. To that end, nutritional scores, e.g. the Nutrient Rich (NR) scores, can be used to aggregate the different nutrients contents of foods in a single score (Drewnowski et al., 2009). However, the latter is calculated based on a serving size, e.g. 100 g, thus it cannot be used to assess the nutritional indices of the total amount of biomass produced by the territory. Therefore, further research is necessary for calculating nutritional indices based on the total production of a territory. In this study, only impacts on the CClt indicator were considered. As shown in Rogy et al. (2024), it would be interesting to consider other indicators such as Water Scarcity or Freshwater Eutrophication, because the evolution of eco-efficiency ratios calculated relatively to these indicators are different from CClt. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Towards an assessment of land planning resilience [SOURCE: Page_134]

This paper provides quantitative information about the evolution of the environmental performance of land planning scenarios for agricultural territories in a climate change context. The level and the variability are two criteria of dynamics allowing grasping the resilience of a system (Dardonville et al., 2021). Other criteria of the dynamics of the environmental performance of land planning scenarios could have been studied in this chapter to further study the resilience: trend and resistance (Dardonville et al., 2021). The trend can be studied by looking at the slope of the linear regression of the eco-efficiency (Dardonville et al., 2022). As for resistance, it can be evaluated with the number of times eco-efficiency exceeds a low performance threshold (Li et al., 2019). For this, it is necessary to set both a low threshold for the service provided, and a high threshold for the environmental impact. For the service, the threshold could be assessed based on socio-economic studies, e.g. bankruptcy threshold of a [SOURCE: Page_134]

126 [SOURCE: Page_134]

farm. For environmental impact, the threshold could be assessed using science-based targets such as planetary boundaries (Rockström et al., 2009), which could be defined at a territorial scale (Bjørn and Hauschild, 2015). Nevertheless, the eco-efficiency is a ratio and it may not exceed its threshold if only one of the service or environmental impact variables exceeds it. Therefore, the assessment of the resistance of the service and the environmental impacts should be assessed separately. [SOURCE: Page_135]

### 4. Conclusion

This paper successfully developed an approach combining the DoE method with T-LCA and crop modeling. This approach made it possible to explore criteria of dynamics linked to the evolution of the environmental performance of a variety of land planning scenarios in a climate change context. Consequently, this study paves the way for research into the resilience of the environmental performance of land planning by studying the dynamic of the environmental performance. [SOURCE: Page_135]

This study showed that the choice of crops is important because they perform differently depending on the territorial service, for example irrigated dry beans perform better on AT and irrigated maize on MAP. In addition, crop diversity increases the stability of environmental performance under rainfed conditions. This study also showed that for most of the services provided by the territory, irrigation increased and stabilized eco-efficiencies. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Further research is needed to be able to include crop rotation as well as the effects of climate change on the amount of water available for irrigation in the modelling. In addition, more adequate modeling of fertilization-related emissions and crop selling prices is needed to better assess environmental performance. [SOURCE: Page_135]

127 [SOURCE: Page_135]

128 [SOURCE: Page_136]

Chapter VI: General discussion of the thesis Chapter V couples the design of experiments (DoE) methodology with the methodological developments made to T-LCA to explore how the environmental performance of land planning scenarios evolves according to the type of crops cultivated in the area under irrigated or rainfed conditions. To this end, a mixture-process design (a DoE method) is used in order to assess the environmental performance of various combinations of contrasted crops (e.g. legumes, oilseeds, C$_3$ and C$_4$ crops). Chapter V is also innovative because it deepens the concept of eco-efficiency by studying in detail its dynamics, with its average and its variability. This is important because a no-regrets land planning scenario for an agricultural territory should be able to maintain its environmental performance in the face of shocks and stresses, such as climate change. [SOURCE: Page_137]

The main contributions, limitations and perspectives of the methodological developments are addressed in Chapter VI, which constitutes the general discussion of the PhD thesis. [SOURCE: Page_137]
