### 1. Main contributions of the PhD thesis

Eco-efficiency: a metric to identify no-regrets scenarios [SOURCE: Page_139]

The methodological developments proposed in the PhD thesis have allowed an in-depth exploration of T-LCA approaches and the concept of eco-efficiency. Eco-efficiency, defined as a ratio between the services provided by a land planning scenario in a given territory and its related impacts, has been computed to compare the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios under different climate conditions. [SOURCE: Page_139]

A first implementation of T-LCA discussed the opportunity of deploying a hydraulic infrastructure to secure water supply on an agricultural perimeter in current climate conditions in Chapter II. The results reported potential trade-offs according to the chosen service studied and the environmental impact category. A rainfed scenario could have better environmental performance than irrigated scenarios for two services, i.e. economic profit and land management. In this case, the environmental impacts generated by hydraulic projects are not offset by the selected services provided. However, the environmental performance of the scenario with an irrigation infrastructure is better if the biomass production service is considered. In addition, a scenario with an inter-basin water transfer (IBWT) can perform better than a scenario with an agricultural reservoir (AR) on the human health and ecosystems indicator but not on mineral resource use and fossil and nuclear energy use. For agricultural land planning scenarios, these preliminary results on eco-efficiency are directly associated with the type of crop cultivated. Chapter V showed that for the average environmental performance calculated over 30 year with the climate change long term indicator (CClt), 100% irrigated maize is the best scenario when services related to food production and calorie supply are assessed, while 100% rainfed dry beans are the best for land management service and 100% irrigated dry beans for economic profit. [SOURCE: Page_139]

To be as representative as possible of the studied territory, T-LCA needs to be implemented as part of a participatory process. This would make it possible to take into account the values of stakeholders and their different points of view in terms of territorial development. Jouini et al. (2019) proposed to define land planning scenarios and their related services with stakeholders, through surveys, interviews and workshops, when implementing T-LCA. In this way, the selected crops and any irrigation infrastructure will be adapted to the specific features of the territory studied, as will the services provided. [SOURCE: Page_139]

This PhD thesis also established that when studying agricultural land planning scenarios over the long term it is important to account for the effects of climate change on their environmental performance. Climate change affects crop growth, yield, fertilizer and water needs as well as [SOURCE: Page_139]

131 [SOURCE: Page_139]

the amount of surface water available for irrigation. As a result, climate change alters the environmental performance of land planning scenarios by affecting both the service provided and their environmental impacts. These effects depend on the location, the impact category and the type of water resource used for irrigation, i.e. unconstrained (where the quantity of water available per inhabitant or per hectare of arable land is high) or constrained. [SOURCE: Page_140]

First, the PhD thesis focused on assessing the evolution of the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios involving irrigation infrastructure. In the case of unconstrained resources with IBWT, for some midpoint indicators, e.g. CClt, and locations, e.g. France and Russia, an irrigation project could increase the eco-efficiency over time of a land planning scenario. This is mainly due to the yield increase induced by the effects of CO$_2$ fertilization in absence of water stress, offsetting the increase of the impacts related to the higher use of fertilizers and water. This is not always the case for other impact categories such as Freshwater Eutrophication (FE) in China or Water Scarcity (WS) in France, where the increase in yields could not offset the increase of these environmental impacts. In the case of Agricultural Reservoir (AR), the amount of available water is directly linked to the precipitations falling over the territory during a year. In dry years, required amounts of water cannot be supplied to crops by irrigation and the resulting biomass production is affected. This can lead to drops in eco-efficiencies for the CClt indicator in France and in the USA for instance. At the end, under both constrained and unconstrained conditions, the results underline the fact that other adaptation solutions to climate change may be needed in addition to irrigation to maintain the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios over time. For instance, growing water-stress resistant crops to reduce the water needs (Belmin et al., 2023), or, agricultural practices that prevent soil erosion (Panagos et al., 2015), and reduce phosphorus run-off into surface water could be applied. Irrigation alone is not an option, and other agricultural practices need to be explored further. [SOURCE: Page_140]

Second, when comparing the environmental performance of land planning scenarios with and without irrigation, in some cases irrigation is not the best option. The increase of biomass production, due to the cultivation of more productive crops such as maize, or its stability (yields are maintained in dry years) afforded by irrigation is made at the expense of additional impacts due to the hydraulic project. Consequently, when the supplementary amount of biomass produced by an irrigated scenario is enough to offset the additional impacts associated to a hydraulic project, the irrigated scenario performs better than the rainfed one from an environmental point of view. It is the case for both IBWT and AR in Australia and in the USA, for CClt and FE indicators. On the contrary, when the difference of yield is not enough to offset the additional impacts, the rainfed scenario performs better than the irrigated ones, such as for WS and Human toxicity cancer indicators in France and in Russia. There are cases where [SOURCE: Page_140]

132 [SOURCE: Page_140]

the land planning scenario with the better environmental performance depends on the year of the assessment, highlighting that there can be a potential temporal burden shifting. As such, this is case for the WS indicator for both scenarios with IBWT and AR in Australia. For the CClt indicator, the rainfed perimeter can perform better in locations such as China for the scenario with an IBWT and in France for the scenario with an AR depending on the year. In China, this occurs in years of high electricity and water consumptions, as the country's electricity mix is highly carbon-intensive. In France, this occurs when there is not enough water in the AR to support crop yields, the environmental impacts of the hydraulic project are not offset by a sufficient amount of biomass production. Ultimately, this PhD thesis pointed out the existence of a long-term food-water-energy-infrastructure, leading to potential burden shifting between biomass production, impact categories and between the years of the assessment. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Thirdly, the PhD thesis also proposed to compute two criteria to assess the temporal dynamics of the environmental performance of a land planning scenario, namely the average and the variability. Potential trade-offs between these two criteria can arise. In certain years, a rainfed scenario can perform better than an irrigated scenario, which is reported through the average criterion. Yet the environmental performance of the irrigated scenario may be more stable over time, which is considered using the variability criterion. Calculating these criteria is a first step towards assessing the resilience of a land planning scenario, i.e. the ability of a system to perform its functions in the face of shocks and stresses (Meuwissen et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_141]

Ultimately, the methodological developments of this PhD thesis allow to assess the environmental performance of long-term land planning scenarios through a regionalized and dynamic methodology to identify potential trade-offs between services provided, impact categories, locations, and years of the assessment. They can provide local stakeholders with a dashboard for identifying false good ideas, and discussing the opportunity to carry out projects, particularly major infrastructure projects, over the long-term. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Using biophysical models to deepen T-LCA [SOURCE: Page_141]

T-LCA is a static methodology that does not take into account the effects of environmental change on the environmental performance of land planning scenarios. It could not be used to assess accurately the performance of long-term land planning scenario in a climate change context. This point is addressed by coupling T-LCA with biophysical models, i.e. the crop model AquaCrop, and the hydrological model GR4J, which allows building dynamic and regionalized LCIs for agricultural territories in a climate change context. The use of biophysical models such as AquaCrop can also complement agricultural LCI databases, such as Agribalyse or World Food LCA Database (WFLDB). More specifically, AquaCrop and the emission model proposed [SOURCE: Page_141]

133 [SOURCE: Page_141]

can compute data on crop yields, field emissions and water needs, in the actual climate context, in locations and crops not covered by these databases. [SOURCE: Page_142]

AquaCrop model can also be used to build LCI of crop production for background processes in prospective LCI databases, for which there is a growing interest in the LCA community. The environmental impacts of products or services are highly dependent on global supply chains and energy systems, which are rapidly evolving according to socio-economic developments (Sacchi et al., 2022). Existing tools such as PREMISE (PRospective EnvironMental Impact asSEment) (Sacchi et al., 2022) compute prospective databases for any given year until 2100 for 15 different Shared Socio-economic Pathways (SSP) scenarios, and for large geographical regions, e.g. western Europe. PREMISE focuses on prospective technologies for five economic sectors, i.e. power generation, cement and steel production, freight and passenger road transportation, and supply of conventional and alternative fuels. The core of the methodology is based on a coupling between the Ecoinvent database and integrated assessment models (IAMs) that simulate “the human decision-making about energy systems and land use with biogeochemistry and the natural Earth system” (Edmonds et al., 2012). PREMISE uses output data from the IAMs REMIND (REgional Model of Investment and Development) (Aboumahboub et al., 2020) and IMAGE (Integrated Model to Assess the Global Environment) (Stehfest et al., 2014). REMIND has a special focus on the energy sector and its GHG emissions, whereas IMAGE has been developed to model the processes of global environmental change by analyzing large-scale and long-term interactions between the natural environment and human development. IMAGE includes a crop growth module with a coarse resolution of 30 arcmin (i.e., around 55 km at the equator), that computes data on the yields of 12 crops according to local climate, soil and agricultural management. These data are not used in PREMISE. However, they would be of interest to the bioenergy and food sectors. The approach proposed in this PhD thesis would provide crop yields at a spatial resolution of 15 arcmin, i.e. the spatial resolution of the climate data, computing more representative prospective and regionalized LCI datasets for use in PREMISE. [SOURCE: Page_142]

### 2. Main limitations and perspectives

Considering uncertainties [SOURCE: Page_142]

Uncertainties are linked to the data used, to the models adopted and to certain methodological choices (Huijbregts, 1998). Climate data are an important input of both crop growth and hydrological models. In this PhD thesis, the worst-case scenario in terms of projection of GHG emissions and of climate change was selected, i.e. the SSP$_5$-8.5 scenario. This scenario was chosen to provide a proof of concept that climate change can affect the environmental [SOURCE: Page_142]

134 [SOURCE: Page_142]

performance of agricultural territories. This scenario projects a global warming of 5°C in 2100, which is highly unlikely to happen as this scenario relies on more coal than the amount of resource available in recoverable reserves (Hausfather and Peters, 2020). It would be interesting to test other scenarios such as business as usual scenarios (e.g. SSP$_4$-6.0 or SSP$_2$-4.5), which project global warming of 3°C and 2.5°C respectively (Hausfather and Peters, 2020). In addition, the climate data following one SSP scenario are obtained using the downscaled results of only one General Circulation Model (GCM). Important uncertainty is also linked to the choice of GCM, as different GCM can provide diverse results (Eyring et al., 2016), for instance in terms of precipitation for the same location (increase or decrease) (Asseng et al., 2013). To go further, different SSP scenarios and GCM could be used to address the uncertainty due to climate projections. [SOURCE: Page_143]

There is also uncertainty linked to the choice of crop model, as different crop models can give different results (Pasquel et al., 2022). This is due to crop models simulating differently crop growth according to increasing [CO$_2$], rising temperature and changes in precipitation (Rosenzweig et al., 2018). Uncertainty related to the choice of hydrological model can also exist. The projection of runoff can differ according to the structure of the model, e.g. number of parameters considered (Arsenault et al., 2015). The issue related to the choice of a model can be addressed by using multi-model ensembles. To this end, mean or median of the main outputs of different models can be used instead of using outputs of a single model. Multi-model ensembles can give better prediction of crop yields or of runoffs than single crop or hydrological models (Arsenault et al., 2015; Wallach et al., 2018). In addition, to consolidate the results of this PhD thesis, calibration (i.e., “the estimation and adjustment of model parameters and constants to improve the agreement between model output and a data set” (Rykiel, 1996)), and validation (i.e., “demonstration that a model within its domain of applicability possesses a satisfactory range of accuracy consistent with the intended application of the model” (Rykiel, 1996)) of both crop and hydrological models could be performed (Asseng et al., 2013; Lemaitre-Basset et al., 2021). Lack of calibration and validation could lead in AquaCrop to poor estimation of crop yields and water requirements compared to actual field experiments, and in GR4J, to poor estimation of water runoff compared to the runoff at the outlet of a real watershed. Hence, both calibration and validation are necessary for the outputs of both crop and hydrological models to be more representative of the studied location. To this end, non- conservative parameters of AquaCrop, e.g. the amount of Growing Degree Days (GDD) of the development stages (or Phenological Heat Unit (PHU)) or the plant density, and the four parameters of GR4J need to be chosen according to observational datasets of the studied location. These observed datasets should consist into two time series, i.e. one time series for calibration and another one for validation. For AquaCrop, observational datasets encompass [SOURCE: Page_143]

135 [SOURCE: Page_143]

observed yields and observed non-conservative crop parameters (e.g., time to reach flowering). There is no such complete dataset at the global scale as the ones currently available only provides time series for crop yields (Müller et al., 2020). For GR4J, observational dataset should include daily precipitations and reference evapotranspiration for real watersheds, as well as daily runoffs at their outlet. However, there is hardly any databases compiling large samples of observed data for watersheds at a global scale (Mathevet et al., 2020). The coupling between biophysical models and T-LCA developed in this PhD thesis is intended for use in any location of the world. To improve the local representativeness of the modeled processes, data collected on real cases could be used in the future, and compared with current results to analyze the importance of model calibration and validation. [SOURCE: Page_144]

Uncertainties are also linked to the LCI of agricultural activities besides crop water consumption. For fertilization, nutrient inputs are calculated based on crop nutritional contents and the field emissions of N and P were computed based on regression models or pre- calculated emission factors. These emissions are the main contributors to impact categories such as climate change or freshwater eutrophication. It is thus paramount to select methodologies quantifying them with as little uncertainty as possible, for example by taking into account the timing of fertilization and of nutrient uptake by crop. Uncertainty is also linked to the LCI of background processes, e.g. electricity mix, which was taken at the national scale, whereas it can vary at the subnational scale (Weber et al., 2010) . [SOURCE: Page_144]

To quantify the uncertainty of the environmental performance, Monte Carlo simulation could be performed by computing the uncertainty distribution of the environmental performance. Random values in the uncertainty distribution of the model parameters (e.g., crop yields, crop water consumption or background LCI data) can be used to calculate outputs. The uncertainty distribution of crop yields and crop water consumption can be estimated using outputs from multiple crop and hydrological models. For the remaining data, i.e. emissions as well as background LCI data, the uncertainty distribution is available in LCI databases from which background data and emission models were sourced. [SOURCE: Page_144]

Biophysical model scope [SOURCE: Page_144]

### 2.2.1. Climate hazards considered in the modeling

AquaCrop considers the effects of CO$_2$ concentration, temperature and precipitation on crop growth as well as the effects of extreme events such as heatwaves and droughts. However, AquaCrop does not consider the effects of some climate hazards affecting crop production, such as late-spring frost (LSF) or hailstorms. Both of these effects on crops could be amplified by climate change. LSF are “frost events occurring after germination and budburst of [SOURCE: Page_144]

136 [SOURCE: Page_144]

herbaceous and woody plants, respectively” (Lamichhane, 2021) and are detrimental to crop yields (Carter and Wiersma, 2020; Xiao et al., 2018). The occurrence of LSF will increase in the future due to the advance of crop phenology, i.e. earlier crop germination and flowering, caused by climate change (Lamichhane, 2021). Hailstorms are affected by climate change due to convective instability, changes in moisture, microphysical processes and vertical wind shear (Raupach et al., 2021). Changes in hailstorm frequency due to climate change will depend on the location. They could increase in Australia and Europe and decrease in North America and East Asia (Raupach et al., 2021). Hailstorms can damage crops, and decrease crop yields (Botzen et al., 2010), especially if they occur close to harvesting (Elahi et al., 2022). Most crop models do not consider the effects of hailstorms and LSFs on crop development. Nevertheless, a cold stress function can be added to the crop models in order to account for LSFs (Bai et al., 2022) and a coupling with a hail module can be implemented to take into account the effects of hailstorms (Wang et al., 2012). The consideration of LSF and hailstorms in crop modeling could diminish the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenario, as these events could reduce crop yields. [SOURCE: Page_145]

### 2.2.2. Biogeochemical cycle considered in the modeling

In this PhD thesis, the focus is on water biogeochemical cycle as the aim is to investigate the environmental performance of irrigation projects on a territorial scale in a context of climate change. The model AquaCrop fits this purpose well. However, the hydrological model GR4J accounts only partially for the water cycle. In fact, it calculates surface water resources on the basis of local precipitation and reference evapotranspiration, but does not consider groundwater resources, such as alluvial groundwater or fossil groundwater (isolated water in a confined aquifer). The computation of the latter would allow assessing other land planning scenarios including AR filling with alluvial groundwater resources (Lardy et al., 2016). Such withdrawal could be performed at a non-renewable rate, i.e. when the withdrawal exceeds the recharge afforded by precipitation (Maeseele and Roux, 2021), leading to problem with filling the AR as the groundwater stock would be depleted. The latter would lead to low environmental performance as the amount of water available in the AR would not be sufficient to meet water crop requirements. Other biogeochemical cycles than water would be important to consider, especially the nitrogen and phosphorus cycles, as these flows are important drivers of crop growth and environmental impacts. In addition to water stress, it would be important to account for the effects of nutritional stresses on crop growth as they can interact with climate change effects such as CO$_2$ fertilization. If the quantity of nitrogen applied is insufficient, the yields cannot fully beneficiate from the effects of CO$_2$ fertilization (Vanuytrecht et al., 2011), which would reduce the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios. No consideration of annual nutrient balances or fertilization calendars, e.g. more leaching of [SOURCE: Page_145]

137 [SOURCE: Page_145]

nutrients if fertilization is performed during rainfall events, can lead to misestimating nutrient inputs and field emissions, as well as their related impacts. In addition, beneficial effects induced by crop rotations, i.e. reduction of fertilizer inputs for the current crop due to the presence of residues from the previous crop (Brankatschk and Finkbeiner, 2015), cannot be considered in the assessment. In order to overcome these limitations, more exhaustive crop models than AquaCrop can be used, such as STICS (Simulateur mulTIdisciplinaire pour les Cultures Standard) (Brisson et al., 2003) or EPIC (Erosion-Productivity Impact Calculator) (Williams et al., 1989). STICS considers both water and nitrogen cycle in the crop growth model, whereas EPIC considers water, nitrogen and phosphorus cycles. These models could be used to account for nutrient uptakes and losses as well as crop rotations. However, to use such crop models, a high level of expertise is required as well as an important amount of data for the model inputs(Avadí et al., 2022). The choice of a crop model for coupling with T-LCA should be based on a compromise between available data and expertise, and representativeness of the processes to be modeled. [SOURCE: Page_146]

### 2.2.3. Spatial coverage

AquaCrop is a point-based model, i.e. a model computing crop growth on one spatial unit without considering the interactions between spatial units, such as erosion or plant dispersal (Heuvelink et al., 2010). AquaCrop was upscaled at the territorial level. Crop growth over the agricultural area is hence computed with a multitude of independent simulations, thus not taking into account the spatial interactions between simulations (Pasquel et al., 2022). This modeling do not allow for taking into account some agricultural practices, e.g. intercropping or crop rotations, which could modify the LCI of an agricultural territory as they affect fertilization and the use of pesticides. GR4J is a lumped model computing the runoff at the outlet of a homogeneous watershed in terms of soil, slope and climate. The location of AR within the watershed cannot be captured in a lumped model (Payan et al., 2008). Hence, GR4J cannot be used to take into account the interactions between multiple ARs and local water streams as the effect of an AR on a water stream depends on its position along the stream network, nor the cumulative effects generated by the construction of several ARs in the same watershed (Habets et al., 2018). The non-consideration of cumulative effects leading to non-linear reduction of water flows over the territory, i.e. a more important reduction during the low-flow season, can lead to overestimations of the water available for filling the ARs and of the environmental performance of the irrigated agricultural territory (Habets et al., 2018). To study these effects it is necessary to use distributed hydrological models, e.g. MHYDAS-Small- Reservoirs (Lebon et al., 2022), that considers the spatial variability of the territory and in which the multiple ARs can be spatialized (Habets et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_146]

138 [SOURCE: Page_146]

Considering spatio-temporal dynamics in Life Cycle Impact Assessment [SOURCE: Page_147]

In this PhD thesis, characterization factors (CFs) of the LCIA method IMPACT World+ at the global scale are considered, except for the Water Scarcity impact category for which annual CFs of the AWARE method at the scale of the sub-watershed are selected. [SOURCE: Page_147]

In order to have a better spatial representativeness of the impacts, CFs for regional impact categories from IMPACT World+ could be used at a native scale, i.e. a resolution starting from 0.5°x^0.5° for freshwater eutrophication to 2°x^2.5° for terrestrial acidification (Bulle et al., 2019). This would be especially important for the impact categories related to human health and ecosystem quality, i.e. acidification, eutrophication, and land use, as there is high spatial variability for these CFs (Bulle et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_147]

The AWARE CFs used are characterizing water consumption in a specific watershed based on the water consumption of human activities, the water needs of the ecosystems and the availability of surface water (Boulay et al., 2018). While the AWARE method allows to differentiate between watersheds that are subject to water stress and those that are not, it does not consider the exchange of water flows between different compartments in the same watershed, i.e. between surface water, groundwater, soil and atmosphere. This is limiting as a withdrawal or release of water in a compartment, e.g. groundwater, can affect the amount of water available in other compartments, e.g. surface water (Núnez et al., 2018). To address this issue, mechanistic CFs should be developed to assess the impacts of water consumption on the different water compartments of a watershed. These mechanistic CFs could be based on the multicompartment depletion factors of Pierrat et al. (2022). This study is a first step towards the quantification of a fate factor linked to water withdrawal and release in a specific compartment, for quantifying the impacts on water scarcity. Considering the fate of water in the different water compartment would enable to better model the effects of AR on water scarcity, not considered in the current LCIA methods. For example, the interception of precipitation by the AR can affect three different compartments: the soil (less infiltration), the groundwater (less recharge) and the surface water (reduction of the base flow) (Núnez et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Finally, the CFs used are retrospective, e.g. they are calculated based on human interventions and water availabilities of the year 2010 in the case of AWARE, which is an important limitation for prospective LCA studies (van der Giesen et al., 2020). Therefore, it is necessary to develop prospective and regionalized CF for each impact categories. For the AWARE CFs, human water consumption and water availabilities in sub-watersheds will evolve in the future due to socio-economic developments (Leão et al., 2019b). Prospective and regionalized AWARE CFs could be computed such as in the study of Baustert et al. (2022) where they used the IAM [SOURCE: Page_147]

139 [SOURCE: Page_147]

IMAGE and computed CFs at the scale of the sub-watershed for the decades between 2010 and 2050 for the SSP$_2$-6.0 and SSP$_2$-2.6 scenarios. The difference of CFs between 2010 and 2050 can be important, i.e. between -100 and 99.9, showing the importance of using prospective instead of retrospective CFs. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Towards the use of Integrated Assessment Modeling in T-LCA [SOURCE: Page_148]

In the PhD thesis, the hydrological modeling and the crop model are run separately. In fact, two simulations are performed with AquaCrop. The first simulation is used to calculate crop water requirements assuming that water resources are unlimited. The second simulation links the hydrological model outputs with the crop model. In this way, crop water requirements will be met when sufficient water is available in the AR. This modelling is not problematic as long as the AR is sufficiently full. On the other hand, when there is not enough water in the AR to irrigate crops, the lack of strong coupling between hydrological and crop modules can lead to inaccurate estimates of crop water requirements, resulting in lower yields and lower eco- efficiencies. In addition, this lack of strong coupling does not allow taking into account the effect of agricultural practices such as rotations or cover crops on the hydrology of the watershed, which can affect the recharging of the water table in winter (Tribouillois et al., 2022). To address these issues, distributed or semi-distributed models such as SWAT (Soil & Water Assessment Tool) can be used (Neitsch et al., 2011). SWAT accounts for hydrological cycle, including surface and groundwater compartments, as well as crop growth, while considering spatial variability and interactions between spatial units. The smallest spatial units in SWAT are hydrological response units (HRUs), which are “lumped areas within a subbasin with a unique combination of land use, soil type, and slope, for simulating all hydrologic processes occurring in the landscape” (Bieger et al., 2017). SWAT can also account for water, phosphorus and nitrogen cycles. Therefore, it can be used to build LCI, including water balance and field emissions, which are more representative of agricultural practices and of studied location. In addition, this model can be useful to compute dynamic and regionalized CFs for the LCIA. The potential of SWAT has been recognized to compute regionalized CFs for freshwater eutrophication (Morelli et al., 2018). Models such as SWAT can also be used to account for the effects of other environmental changes than climate change, such as erosion or soil quality (e.g., level of soil salinity), on the environmental performance of agricultural land planning scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_148]

In addition to modelling biophysical interactions in T-LCA approaches, it may be important to consider socio-economic dynamics at territorial level. Ding and Achten (2022) coupled agent- based modeling (ABM) with T-LCA to into take account farmers’ behaviors, on both temporal and spatial scale, in the choice of cultivated crops (i.e., DC-miscanthus a known bioenergy [SOURCE: Page_148]

140 [SOURCE: Page_148]

crop, AD-miscanthus an unknown bioenergy crop or livestock). These behaviors were studied over 20 years and were influenced by subsidies to encourage bioenergy crops cultivation as well as by the implantation of demonstration farms. The choice of a type of a cultivated crop is based on a probability of adoption (i.e. probability at which the farmer changes land occupation) according to farm size, familiarity with the bioenergy crop (distance to the nearest adopter of the bioenergy crop) and the potential profit increase due to a change of land occupation. Bayram et al. (2023) also used ABM to assess the environmental impacts of mixed crop-livestock farms in the Grand Duchy of Luxembourg, to identify sustainable scenarios. For the decisions of farmers linked to choice of crops, they are based on their green consciousness, crop prices and crop rotation constraints. For the decisions of farmers linked to livestock, e.g. reduction of stocking rate, they are based on subsidies. Both of these studies reported the interest of considering socio-economic interactions (here farmers’ behavior) to elaborate dynamic land planning scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_149]

There are IAM integrating both exhaustive biophysical modeling as well as ABM at the territorial scale, such as MAELIA (Modelling of socio-Agro-Ecological system for Landscape Integrated Assessment) (Therond et al., 2014). MAELIA includes a strong coupling between a crop and a hydrological model that allows considering water, nitrogen and carbon cycles at a sub-watershed level. The ABM considers farmers’ behavior in the choice of a cropping plan according to four criteria, i.e. similarity to the last cropping plan, maximization of the profit, minimization of income variability and number of days without work (Therond et al., 2014). Coupling this IAM with T-LCA could allow to design more representative land planning scenarios, to build regionalized and dynamic LCI, as well as to propose some dynamic, regionalized and prospective CFs. This is a promising avenue of research to deepen T-LCA even further. [SOURCE: Page_149]

141 [SOURCE: Page_149]

142 [SOURCE: Page_150]

Chapter VII: Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_151]

143 [SOURCE: Page_151]

Chapter I highlighted the dual challenge facing agricultural systems, i.e. 1) adapting to climate change and ii) reducing their environmental impact. Irrigation is one of the main solutions for adapting agricultural systems to climate change. However, it relies on large-scale and long- term infrastructures, such as agricultural reservoirs (AR) or inter-basin water transfers (IBWT). It is necessary to assess the environmental performance of these long-term land planning scenarios to identify “no-regrets” solution in a context of climate change, scenario that do not generate pollution transfers compared to the baseline scenario. Territorial LCA (T-LCA) is a promising methodological framework for assessing the environmental performance of a territory as a whole, considering its multifunctionality. It proposes to compute eco-efficiency metrics (i.e. a ratio between the services provided by a land planning scenario and its related environmental impacts) to compare territorial trajectories. To date, T-LCA has never been fully applied to the point of calculating eco-efficiencies for different land planning scenarios. Moreover, it is a static approach and the effects of climate change on the environmental performance of a land planning scenario are not considered. The main research question of this PhD thesis was therefore formulated: To what extent does climate change affect the environmental performance of long-term land planning scenarios for agricultural territories? This question was addressed by tackling three objectives. [SOURCE: Page_152]

Chapter II sought to assess the usefulness and feasibility of the eco-efficiency concept for comparing the environmental performance of three land planning scenarios in an agricultural territory in the south of France. Eco-efficiencies were calculated for three different territorial functions, i.e. economic profit, food production, and land management and occupation, and three environmental damages for two irrigated scenarios, i.e. with a IBWT or a AR, and a rainfed scenario. The environmental impacts of all agricultural activities in the area and hydraulic infrastructure have been quantified according to a “from cradle to territorial gate” perspective. This chapter pointed out that T-LCA and the concept of eco-efficiency make it possible to highlight potential burden shifting between impact categories and between different territorial functions, and the need to adopt an exhaustive assessment to identify “no-regrets” land planning scenarios. However, for long-term land planning scenarios such as hydraulic projects, it is required to consider the effects of global change including climate change on their environmental performance. [SOURCE: Page_152]

Chapters III and IV proposed innovative methodological developments to consider the effects of climate change in T-LCA. The core of the methodology is based on a coupling between biophysical models and T-LCA. First, Chapter III used the AquaCrop crop growth model to build dynamic and regionalized life cycle inventories (LCIs) that consider the effects of climate change on both crop yields and water needs. Outputs in crop yield are also used to compute annual eco-efficiency for a given land planning scenario based on a ratio between annual [SOURCE: Page_152]

144 [SOURCE: Page_152]

biomass production and the related environmental impacts. The methodology is applied to compare the environmental performance of two land planning scenarios in a theoretical agricultural territory in six contrasted locations around the world (i.e. USA, China, France, Russia, India and Australia). The first scenario is rainfed, and the second one is an irrigated scenario equipped with a IBWT without considering any constraints on water resources. Second, Chapter IV compared the environmental performance of a rainfed scenario with an irrigated scenario with an AR, while taking also into account the effects of climate change on the quantity of surface water locally available (i.e. in the case of limited water resources). In addition to AquaCrop, the GR4J hydrological model, was coupled with T-LCA to model the annual water availability for irrigation use. As a result, the environmental performance of land planning scenarios varies over time and locations, and it is necessary to take these potential burden shiftings into account. In most cases, the benefits of irrigation in terms of maintaining or increasing yields offset the additional environmental impacts of hydraulic infrastructure. However, in some cases, e.g. the scenario with an AR in France, irrigation is not the best option from an environmental point of view, and must in all cases be combined with other strategies to cope with the effects of climate change while improving the environmental performance of agricultural areas. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Chapter V aimed at exploring the environmental performance in a context of climate change of irrigated or rainfed land planning scenarios occupied by different crops. To this end, the design of experiment (DoE) methodology was coupled with the methodological developments of Chapter III focusing on the IBWT infrastructure. The DoE method is a crossed mixture process design, in which four different crops, i.e. maize, wheat, dry beans and sunflower, irrigated or rainfed have been studied in order to analyze the variability of the eco-efficiencies and to find the optimal land planning scenario. To this end, several functions have been selected, i.e. food production, economic profit, land occupation and management as well as provision of calories. Chapter V deepened the concept of eco-efficiency by studying in detail its dynamics, with its average and its variability. It identified that to maintain environmental performance over time in a climate change context, irrigated maize monoculture is optimal for food production and provision of calories, whereas it is rainfed dry beans for the land occupation and management function, and irrigated dry beans for economic profit. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Chapter VI discussed the main contributions, limitations and perspectives of this PhD thesis. These works highlighted that it is required to identify potential trade-offs between the services provided by the territory, between the impact categories, between the years and between the locations of the assessment to make a no-regrets choice in terms of land planning scenario in a context of climate change. The proposed methodological developments allow to address these trade-offs based on a coupling between T-LCA and biophysical models. The main [SOURCE: Page_153]

145 [SOURCE: Page_153]

limitations are related to the biophysical models used. They do not allow to differentiate agricultural practices other than irrigation management, nitrogen and phosphorus cycles are not considered as well as all water resources, and the lack of a strong coupling between the biophysical models can lead to inconsistencies in the calculation of crop water needs. In addition, the behaviors of stakeholders is not considered, which is problematic because cropping plan are farmers decision. Thus, in order to overcome these limitations, the main perspective of this thesis is to move towards coupling T-LCA with integrated assessment models at territorial scale to consider a wide range of biophysical and socio-economic interactions. [SOURCE: Page_154]

### 1. Life Cycle Inventory (LCI)

Crop LCI data [SOURCE: Page_184]

The standard doses of pesticides used to calculate the Indicator of Frequency of Treatment (IFT) were sourced from the French Agency for Food, Environmental and Occupational Health & Safety database (République Française, n.d.). The IFT for herbicides was chosen to be 0.5 as the mean of the herbicide use for viticulture in the Languedoc-Roussillon region in 2013 (DRAAF-DREAL Occitanie, 2018). The number of hour of mechanical works were calculated based on performances (in ha/hr) of the agricultural machinery that were sourced from a French catalogue (APCA, 2018). LCI of agricultural machinery from Agribalyse database were used. [SOURCE: Page_184]

Table S. 1 Data used for crop LCIs [SOURCE: Page_184]

Crop                 LCI sources                         Adaptations            Yield (kg/ha/yr) Non                World Food LCA Database Full mass allocation of the 6,560 irrigated          3.5                                 LCI to the wheat grain wheat              "Wheat grain, non-irrigated, at farm (WFLDB)/FR U" CDO          (1)   (Agreste, 2016)                     Full dataset development   4,300 Grapevines Irrigated          Agribalyse                      3 None                         10,843 corn               "Grain maize, average from 6 optimized case study, basis   scenario,      at    farm gate/FR U" Irrigated          World Food LCA Database Full mass allocation of the 9,038 wheat              3.5                                 LCI to the wheat grain "Wheat grain, irrigated, at farm (WFLDB)/FR U" PGI          (2)   (Agreste,                   2016) Change in crop yield and 9,945 Grapevines         (IFV        and      Chambres add of water consumption d’agriculture      Languedoc- in CDO grapevines Roussillon, 2013) (1) CDO : “Controlled Designation of Origin” (2) PGI : “ Protected Geographical Indication” [SOURCE: Page_184]

For some crops, Agribalyse is based on statistical data of French technical itineraries mixing different agricultural practices such as irrigated and non-irrigated production (Koch and Salou, 2020). The LCI for irrigated and non-irrigated wheats were hence sourced from the World Food LCA DataBase WFLDB 3.5 (Nemecek et al., 2019). Yet, cereal coproducts are not considered in Agribalyse 3 (Koch and Salou, 2020), contrary to the WFLDB 3.5 scope (Nemecek et al., 2019). For consistency in crop inventories, economic allocation of the flows linked to wheat grain production and wheat straw production was replaced by a full-economic allocation to wheat grain production. [SOURCE: Page_185]

The quantity of water necessary for the irrigated grapevines comes from a study based on 51 irrigation trials between 1999 and 2009 in the South of France. Results showed that the mean annual yield increase compared to a non-irrigated control grapevine was 17% for a 78 mm quantity of irrigation water (IFV and Chambres d’agriculture Languedoc-Roussillon, 2013). Hence, to take into account the irrigation in PGI’s LCI, the statistic’s PGI yield was increased and a quantity of water for irrigation was added to the inventory. [SOURCE: Page_185]

Water supply LCI [SOURCE: Page_185]

𝐂𝐫𝐨𝐩𝑖            𝑾𝒑𝒆𝒓𝒊𝒎𝒆𝒕𝒆𝒓                 𝑾𝒆𝒗𝒂𝒑𝒐𝒕𝒓𝒂𝒏𝒔𝒑𝒊𝒓𝒂𝒕𝒊𝒐𝒏𝒊         𝑾𝒔𝒖𝒓𝒇𝒂𝒄𝒆𝒊   𝑾𝒈𝒓𝒐𝒖𝒏𝒅 𝒊                                                                 𝒊 3 (m /yr)                        (m^3/yr)                 (m^3/yr)       3 (m /yr) Corn                     1,15E+05                      8,60E+04                 2,29E+04    5,73E+03 Wheat                    2,81E+05                      2,11E+05                 5,63E+04    1,41E+04 PGI                      5,46E+04                      4,91E+04                 4,37E+03    1,09E+03 (1) Grapevine (1) PGI : “Protected Geographical Indication” [SOURCE: Page_195]

Where the 𝑖 index is a type of crop; 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑝𝑖𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑖 is the flow of water that is evapotranspirated by the crop 𝑖 ; 𝑊𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑒𝑖 and 𝑊𝑔𝑟𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑑 are the water losses for each crop 𝑖 𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_195]

that are going to the river compartment and the groundwater compartment respectively; 𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 𝑖 is equal to the sum of all previous outflows and represents the total quantity of [SOURCE: Page_195]

water necessary at the agricultural perimeter for the irrigation of the crop 𝑖. [SOURCE: Page_195]

𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 (m^3/yr), the total quantity of water that needs to be supplied to the agricultural perimeter for irrigation, was calculated by summing all 𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 , i.e. 4,51E+05 m^3/yr. 𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_195]

To perform a water balance at AR level, it is necessary to consider three different annual water flows and one annual water stock as shown in Figure 7, i.e. i) 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 (m^3/yr), the quantity of rainwater and run-off water filling the AR, ii) 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 (m^3/yr), which is the share of water that is evaporated from the AR open-surface, iii) 𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 (m^3/yr), which is the main output and iv) 𝑊𝑢𝑛𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 ,which corresponds to the water that is not consumed and stored for the next year. [SOURCE: Page_195]

The AR is emptied every 25 years to change the geotextile and the geomembrane (see in the Table S. 4). Hence, the first year after being empty, 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 is 700 000 m^3 corresponding to the maximum storage capacity of the reservoir. The next 24 years, 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 corresponds to the sum of 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 and 𝑊𝑤𝑖ℎ𝑑𝑟𝑎𝑤𝑎𝑙 , with the hypothesis that the same quantity of precipitation falls each years. Hence, the mean yearly quantity of rainwater that fills the AR, 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 𝐴𝑅 (m^3/yr) is calculated with equation S.1. [SOURCE: Page_195]

700000 + 24 ∗ (𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 + 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 ) 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 𝐴𝑅 =                                                                 (𝑆. 1) 25 [SOURCE: Page_195]

Equation S.2, the aerodynamic equation of (Mcjannet et al., 2012) also used in (Risch et al., 2014) to calculate the quantity of evaporated water over a free surface of Waste Water Treatment Plant, allows computing 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 : [SOURCE: Page_196]

12            12 −0.05 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 = ∑ 𝐸𝐿𝑎𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑖 = ∑(2,36 + 1,67𝑢2 )𝑆𝐴𝑅    ∗ (𝑣𝑤∗ 𝑖 − 𝑣𝑎𝑖 )           (𝑆. 2) 𝑖=1           𝑖=1 [SOURCE: Page_196]

Where the 𝑖 index is a specific month; 𝐸𝐿𝑎𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑖 (mm/month) is an estimate of the monthly evaporated water over the open-surface; 𝑢2 is the wind speed (m/s) over land at a 2 m height; 𝐴 (m^2) is the area of the open-surface; 𝑣𝑤∗ 𝑖 (kPa) is the mean monthly saturated vapor pressure over the open-surface; and 𝑣𝑎𝑖 (kPa) is the mean monthly partial vapor pressure at air temperature. [SOURCE: Page_196]

𝑣𝑤∗ 𝑖 were calculated based on mean monthly temperature from a meteorological station in the South of France for the 1961-1990 period (World Meteorological Organization (WMO), 2021). 𝑣𝑎𝑖 were calculated using the relative humidity computed daily by the Modern-Era Retrospective analysis for Research and Applications, Version 2 (MERRA-2) Web service (Gelaro et al., 2017) in the South of France for the 1980-1990 period. A 𝑢2 of 2 m/s was chosen as in Risch et al. 2014. It is the global average of wind speed at a 2 m height (Allen et al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_196]

Then, 𝑊𝑢𝑛𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 (m^3/yr) is provided by equation S.3: [SOURCE: Page_196]

𝑊𝑢𝑛𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 = 𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 − 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 − 𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟                    (𝑆. 3) [SOURCE: Page_196]

IBWT supplies water for different use, such as agricultural or urban (Muñoz et al., 2010). Hence, water is circulating through the IBWT all year long. It is assumed that 𝑊𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (m^3/yr), the total quantity of water that is circulating in the IBWT infrastructure, is equal to the annual water withdrawal as water losses are not considered significant. This amount of water is provided in equation S.4. [SOURCE: Page_196]

### 1.2.3. Energy consumption

To calculate the annual energy consumed at AR pumping station to irrigate the agricultural perimeter, Equation S.5 based on the Bernoulli’s principle is used. [SOURCE: Page_197]

(𝑃𝑆𝐼𝐼 − 𝑃𝐴𝑅 ) ∗ 𝑊𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 ∗ 𝑙𝑜𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐 𝐸𝐴𝑅 =                                                                      (𝑆. 5) 𝜂ℎ𝑦𝑑𝑟𝑜 ∗ 3600 [SOURCE: Page_197]

Where 𝐸𝐴𝑅 (Wh/yr) is the annual energy used at AR pumping station; 𝑙𝑜𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐 are the electric loss in the pump : 5 % ; 𝜂hydro is the hydraulic yield : 70 %; PAR (barg) is the pump suction pressure and is equal to the hydrostatic head at the constant depth of the AR, calculated with Equation S.6 based on Pascal’s Law. [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝑊𝑢𝑛𝑢𝑠𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 𝑃𝐴𝑅 = (𝜌𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 ∗ 𝑔 ∗                ) ∗ 10−5                               (𝑆. 6) 𝑆𝐴𝑅 [SOURCE: Page_197]

Where 𝜌𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 (kg/m^3) is the density of water at 25°C; 𝑔 (m/s^2) is the Earth free fall acceleration. [SOURCE: Page_197]

IBWT pumping station is functioning every day of the year, and the annual energy consumption is computed with equation S.7, based on the same principle as equation S.5. Results of the energy balance are shown in Table S. 7. [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝑃𝑆𝐼𝐼             𝑊 𝜌𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 ∗ 𝑔 ∗ (𝐻𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 + 𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝐹 +              ∗ 105 ) ∗ 𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝑙𝑜𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐 𝜌𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟 ∗ 𝑔          3600 𝐸𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 =                                                                                        (𝑆. 7) 𝜂ℎ𝑦𝑑𝑟𝑜 [SOURCE: Page_197]

Where 𝐸𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (Wh/yr) is the annual energy consumption at the IBWT’s pumping station ; 𝐹 is the friction factor in the IBWT water pipeline that is equal to a conservative value of 0.0015 (i.e. 15 mm/m) based on expert knowledge. [SOURCE: Page_197]

### 2. Results

Equations S.8-S.20 were used to find at a given 𝐻𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 , for which sets of 𝑄𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 and 𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 the IBWT alternative is better than the AR one. [SOURCE: Page_198]

𝐼𝐴𝑅 = 𝐼𝐴𝑅 𝑖𝑛𝑓𝑟 + 𝐸𝐴𝑅 ∗ 𝐼𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑚𝑖𝑥𝑀𝑉 + (𝑊𝑅𝑜 & 𝑅𝑤 − 𝑊𝑒𝑣𝑎𝑝𝑜𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑑 𝐴𝑅 ) ∗ 𝐼𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝐴𝑅                 (S. 9) [SOURCE: Page_198]

𝐼𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 = %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑇𝑊 ∗ (𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑇𝑊 ∗ 𝐼𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇𝑊𝑃 + 𝐸𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝐼𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑚𝑖𝑥𝑀𝑉 + 𝑊𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝐼𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 + 𝐼𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇𝑃𝑆 )       (𝑆. 10) [SOURCE: Page_198]

𝐼𝐴𝑅 − 𝐼𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 = IAR − %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ (𝐿𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝐼𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇𝑊𝑃 + 𝐸𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝐼𝑒𝑙𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑐𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑚𝑖𝑥𝑀𝑉 + 𝑊𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 ∗ 𝐼𝑤𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 + 𝐼𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇𝑃𝑆 ) = 0   (𝑆. 11) [SOURCE: Page_198]

The Figure 11 was delineated as following. For the ordinates, it starts at a LIBWT of 20 km to underline the fact that IBWT supplies water from a watershed to another and ends at 350 km to have a realistic maximum size of the IBWT. For the abscissas, it starts at %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 = 0.5%, for the maximum 𝑄𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 (3 m^3/s), and ends at %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 = 7.8%. [SOURCE: Page_199]

Figure S. 1, Figure S. 2 and Figure S. 3 indicate that when 𝐻𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑚𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟 lies between 0 and 150 m, there is a decrease of around 5% of the tipping point corresponding to %𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑜𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝐼𝐵𝑊𝑇 = 5% for the HH, Eco and MRU indicators. On the contrary, the tipping points corresponding to the FNEU indicator can be reduced by more than 100%. After that point, the AR is always more performant than the IBWT on this indicator. This is caused by more energy requirement at the IBWT pumping station when the altitude of the irrigated perimeter increases. [SOURCE: Page_199]

### 1.1.1. Climate data

Input climate data over a daily time frame include minimum and maximum temperatures, total precipitations, relative humidity and wind speed at 10 m. [SOURCE: Page_205]

All these climate data are extracted from the simulation results of the Global Circulation Model (GCM) IPSL-CM6A-LR, available in the NASA Earth Exchange Global Daily Downscaled Projections (NEX-GDDP-CMIP$_6$) (Thrasher et al., 2022, 2021) at a spatial resolution of 0.25° x 0.25° for the entire globe. [SOURCE: Page_205]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5239198

- Summary: Section focusing on 1.1.2. soil data

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 1.1.2. Soil data

The AquaCrop model uses two types of soil inputs: the description of the groundwater table and the soil profile. Concerning the groundwater level, it is assumed that there is no shallow groundwater table near the studied agricultural areas and that water can only enter the system by precipitation and irrigation. Concerning the soil profile, the following data are required: depth of the soil horizons (i.e. soil layers), soil water content at the permanent wilting point, at field capacity and at saturation, the hydraulic conductivity of the soil at saturation, and the stoniness of the soil. In order to collect the values of these different parameters for any location on Earth, the equations S.21 – S.41 of the S.I. provided by Saxton and Rawls (2006) have been applied, using the Harmonized World Soil Database (HWSD) (Nachtergaele et al., 2009) to collect input data. The input data collected from the HWSD is the textural class of the soil, the percentages of clay and sand, the percentage of organic matter, the fraction of gravel in the soil, as well as the soil salinity. The terms of equations S.1 to S.21 are described in Table S. 9. [SOURCE: Page_205]

### 1.1.2.5. Salinity effects

𝛹𝑜 = 36 ∗ 𝐸𝐶                                  (𝑆. 37) [SOURCE: Page_206]

𝜃𝑆 𝛹𝑜𝜃 =      ∗ 𝛹𝑜                               (𝑆. 38) 𝜃 [SOURCE: Page_206]

𝛹𝑡𝑜𝑡 𝜃 = 𝛹𝑜𝜃 + 𝜓(1500−33)                              (𝑆. 39) [SOURCE: Page_206]

𝜃𝑆 𝛹𝑡𝑜𝑡 𝜃 =       ∗ 𝛹𝑜 + 𝐴 ∗ 𝜃 −𝐵                         (𝑆. 40) 𝜃 [SOURCE: Page_206]

The Equation S.40 needs to be solved for 𝛹𝑡𝑜𝑡 𝜃 = 1500 𝑘𝑃𝑎 to find 𝜃1500 𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 , the soil water contents at the permanent wilting point adjusted with the soil salinity. The resolution of this [SOURCE: Page_206]

198 [SOURCE: Page_206]

equation is not straightforward analytically. Therefore, Equation S.41 is solved numerically using the Newton-Raphson method of the R package pracma (Borchers, 2021), starting with 𝑓(𝜃) = 𝜃1500. [SOURCE: Page_207]

### 1.1.3. Agricultural management data

Field and irrigation management are AquaCrop inputs that describe agricultural practices. Concerning field management, non-limiting conditions in soil fertility were assumed, while the management of weeds on the agricultural perimeter was presumed to be totally under control, i.e. no weed cover the agricultural perimeter. In addition, mulches and field surface practices were not used. Indeed, the first affects soil evaporation, while the latter, e.g. tillage, changes the surface runoff of the agricultural perimeter. For irrigation management, specifications relative to the irrigation method, irrigation period, amount of extractable soil water that triggers the irrigation and criteria that stop irrigation, had to be provided. Firstly, the irrigation method depends on the type of crop, e.g. sprinkler irrigation for field crop or drip irrigation for other crops. Secondly, the choice of periods of the crop cycles during which irrigation is performed is based on Brouwer et al. (1989), where the period of irrigation extends from early season (i.e. germination) to late season (i.e. harvest). Finally, irrigation is assumed to be triggered during these periods when 50% of readily available water is depleted, i.e. amount of water from the soil that the crop can extract without being stressed, until the soil is at field capacity Mialyk et al. (2022). In addition, irrigation is not permitted during flowering to avoid risks of fusarium disease (BRL, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_208]

---

### 1.1.4. Crop data

In the AquaCrop model, two types of crop parameters can be distinguished: conservative parameters which are valid for the growth of a crop anywhere in the world and non- conservative parameters that need to be calculated for each individual crop and location. For example, the first are related to the effects of water and temperature stress on crop development. These parameters have already been included for 13 crops in AquaCrop. The non-conservative crop parameters include the amount of Growing Degree Days (GDD) of the development stages (or Phenological Heat Unit (PHU)) and other non-conservative parameters, namely the planting method, the sink strength of the crop, the maximum effect of rooting depth, the maximum canopy cover, the reference harvest index and the sowing parameters. Non-conservative crop parameters are not assumed to change over time. The methodology for obtaining these non-conservative crop parameters is described bellow. [SOURCE: Page_208]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5239198

### 1.1.4.1. Phenological Heat Unit (PHU) of the development stages

The different PHU from sowing until each development stage of the crops, i.e. 90% seedling emergence, maximum canopy cover, start of flowering and its duration, maximum rooting depth, beginning of canopy senescence and physiological maturity need to be defined. They are determined in three steps that need to be applied in each location and type of crop, as for [SOURCE: Page_208]

200 [SOURCE: Page_208]

the model CGMS-WOFOST in Müller et al. (2019). The first step is to determine sowing and harvesting dates. To this aim, the “Crop Calendar Dataset” (Sacks et al., 2010) is used. It is a global database containing the sowing and harvest dates of 22 crops with a resolution of about 55 km. The second step is to calculate the mean amount of Growing Degree Days (GDD) between sowing and harvest. This mean is performed over 20 years (1980 – 1999) using the climate data and Eq. S.42 – S.43 (Raes et al., 2018) that describe the mean amount of GDD accumulated by the crop during its growing season, 𝑃𝐻𝑈𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑛 . Finally, the third step is to multiply 𝑃𝐻𝑈𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑛 by a ratio of parameters in the original crop file of AquaCrop, i.e. the ratio of PHU for a specific development stage with the PHU from sowing to harvest. [SOURCE: Page_209]

𝑖𝑓 𝑇𝑎𝑣𝑔 (𝑡(𝑦)) > 𝑇𝑢𝑝𝑝𝑒𝑟 𝑡ℎ𝑒𝑛 𝑇𝑎𝑣𝑔 (𝑡(𝑦)) = 𝑇𝑢𝑝𝑝𝑒𝑟 [SOURCE: Page_209]

With 𝑡, the day of the crop cycle; 𝑛, the number of days between sowing and harvest; 𝑦, the year of the growing season; 𝑇𝑎𝑣𝑔 (K) is the mean temperature of the day; 𝑇𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒 (K), is a conservative parameter of AquaCrop which is the lower limit of temperature for GDD accumulation; 𝑇𝑢𝑝𝑝𝑒𝑟 (K), is a conservative parameter of AquaCrop which is the upper limit of ∗ temperature for GDD accumulation; 𝑇𝑚𝑖𝑛 (K), is the minimum temperature of the day; 𝑇𝑚𝑎𝑥 (K) is the adjusted maximum temperature of the day; and 𝑇𝑚𝑎𝑥 (K) is the maximum temperature of the day. ### 1.1.4.2. Other non-conservative parameters

AquaCrop takes into account a wide range of planting methods, i.e. direct sowing (e.g. for wheat or tomato), transplantation (e.g. for tomato) or regrowth (e.g. for potato). The fertilizing power of CO$_2$ on crops is taken into account in AquaCrop through the sink strength parameters, [SOURCE: Page_209]

201 [SOURCE: Page_209]

which are the upper boundary of the intervals proposed in Table 4 of Vanuytrecht et al. (2011). The maximum effecting rooting depth, maximum canopy cover and germination rates are the same as in the original crop file of AquaCrop. It is important to note that if the root length of the crops exceeds the depth of the soil that is parametrized, AquaCrop adapts by extending the depth of the last soil horizon. The sowing rate of the crop is assumed to be the same over the time span of the study. It can be sourced from Agri-footprint 5, which includes the global average of sowing rates from 2009 to 2013 (Van Paassen et al., 2019). The weight of crop seeds can for example be sourced from governmental institutes (Government of Western Australia, 2017) or from books (Steduto et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_210]

---

### 1.1.5. Initial conditions

Two types of initial conditions need to be defined in order to run the simulations: the Initial Soil Water Content (SWC$_0$) and the sowing date. For SWC$_0$, it was initialized 3 months before the beginning of the first growing season, with a water content at 50% of the soil field capacity (Müller et al., 2019). Sowing dates must be defined for each growing season and crop at a specific location, in order to account for the adaptation of farmers towards the climate context (Bird et al., 2016). Therefore, the methodology of Waha et al. (2012) was used for determining the sowing dates of crops. For locations where sowing happens during the main wet season, the season was considered the same for each year of the simulation. This is due to the methodology of Waha et al. (2012) not considering farmers experience of past sowing dates, contrarily to when the sowing takes place at a specific temperature. Therefore, main wet seasons were calculated for each years of the simulation and the most occurring main wet season was selected for sowing. [SOURCE: Page_210]

LCI for agricultural production [SOURCE: Page_210]

### 1.2.1. Water balance

For the water balance, the amount of water withdrawal by the IBWT is the amount of irrigation water. The share of water withdrawal that is released into the environment is the amount of deep percolation water. Indeed, the AquaCrop model assumes that runoff is only caused by precipitation since the irrigator ought to have total control over the irrigation (Raes, 2017). However, AquaCrop cannot differentiate the share of deep percolation due to irrigation or due to rainfall (Payen, 2015). Therefore, Eq. S.44 of is used for calculating deep percolation linked to irrigation, which thus allows for the water consumption over an irrigated perimeter to be computed. 𝐷𝑃𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑒𝑛 [m^3.ha-1.yr-1] is the amount of deep percolation unrelated to human activities, calculated as in Payen (2015). [SOURCE: Page_210]

202 [SOURCE: Page_210]

𝑅𝑎𝑖𝑛 − 𝑅𝑂 𝐷𝑃𝑖𝑟𝑟 = 𝐷𝑃𝑡𝑜𝑡 − 𝐷𝑃𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑒𝑛 = 𝐷𝑃𝑡𝑜𝑡 ∗ (1 −                   )               (𝑆. 44) 𝑅𝑎𝑖𝑛 − 𝑅𝑂 + 𝐼𝑟𝑟 [SOURCE: Page_211]

Where 𝐷𝑃𝑖𝑟𝑟 [m^3.ha-1.yr-1] is the annual amount of deep percolation due to irrigation over the agricultural perimeter; 𝐷𝑃𝑡𝑜𝑡 [m^3.ha-1.yr-1] is the annual amount of deep percolation due to irrigation and to rainfall over the agricultural perimeter; 𝑅𝑎𝑖𝑛 [m^3.ha-1.yr-1] is the annual amount of rainfall on the agricultural perimeter; 𝑅𝑂 [m^3.ha-1.yr-1] is the annual amount water runoff due to irrigation, which is considered null in AquaCrop (Raes, 2017); and 𝐼𝑟𝑟 [m^3.ha-1.yr-1] is the annual amount of irrigation water supplied to the agricultural perimeter. 𝑅𝑎𝑖𝑛, 𝐼𝑟𝑟, 𝐷𝑃𝑡𝑜𝑡 and 𝑅𝑂 are parameters that are sourced from the output file of AquaCrop. [SOURCE: Page_211]

### 1.2.2.1. Fertilizers inputs

First, nutrient uptake by the crops and their straws is estimated. It is assumed that the fertilizers are supplied to reach the maximum annual yield achievable by the crops depending on the climate and soil of the studied location, which is calculated with AquaCrop outputs in Eq. S.45 – S.46. [SOURCE: Page_211]

𝐵𝑖𝑜𝑀𝑎𝑠𝑠 𝑦𝑖𝑒𝑙𝑑𝑚𝑎𝑥 =             ∗ 𝐻𝐼𝑜 ∗ 1000                           (𝑆. 45) 𝐵𝑟𝑒𝑙𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑣𝑒 [SOURCE: Page_211]

𝐵𝑖𝑜𝑀𝑎𝑠𝑠 𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑤𝑚𝑎𝑥 =             ∗ (1 − 𝐻𝐼𝑜) ∗ 1000                       (𝑆. 46) 𝐵𝑟𝑒𝑙𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑣𝑒 [SOURCE: Page_211]

With 𝑦𝑖𝑒𝑙𝑑𝑚𝑎𝑥 [kg.ha-1.yr-1], the maximal amount of edible yield; 𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑤𝑚𝑎𝑥 [kg.ha-1.yr-1], the maximal amount of straw or haulm that can be produced; 𝐵𝑖𝑜𝑀𝑎𝑠𝑠 [t.ha-1.yr-1], the amount of above-ground biomass of the crop produced; 𝐵𝑟𝑒𝑙𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑣𝑒 [-], the ratio of the actual amount of above-ground biomass of the crop with the ideal amount of above ground-biomass produced by the crops if there is no stresses (i.e. no temperature, water, soil fertility, salinity and weed stresses); and 𝐻𝐼𝑜 [-] is the reference harvest index of the crop. [SOURCE: Page_211]

Chemical compositions of agricultural products can be found on Feedipedia (INRAE et al., 2012), an open access information system on nearly 1400 worldwide livestock feeds. The nitrogen (N), phosphorus (P) and potassium (K) compositions of the crops as well as their straws or haulms are assumed to represent their nutrients uptake (Nemecek et al., 2019). The N inputs to the agricultural perimeter correspond to the total uptake of the total cultivated biomass, even if a part of them (e.g. straws or haulms) is left in the field because the nitrogen in the biomass is not readily available for crops. On the contrary, P and K inputs correspond only to the biomass of the products that leave the agricultural perimeter. It is assumed that the [SOURCE: Page_211]

203 [SOURCE: Page_211]

composition of crops and their straws or haulms stay the same during the timespan of the study and that all of the straws and haulms are taken out of the agricultural perimeter. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Once the NPK quantities have been estimated, a fixed fertilizer mix is defined for each country. The amounts of mineral and organic fertilizers are calculated based on the same assumptions as in Nemecek et al. (2019). First, 80% of the amount of N supplied to the agricultural field is provided by mineral fertilizer and the remaining by organic fertilizers. Then the remaining part of P and K, after subtracting the amount of these nutrients contained in the organic fertilizers, is assumed to be supplied by mineral fertilizers. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Both organic and mineral fertilizers mixes were based on data from the more recent 5-year period available in the databases recommended by Nemecek et al. (2019), i.e. 2015-2019. For organic fertilizers, mixes of the mass fraction of the different organic fertilizers used in a country were constructed based on the mean national consumption in FAOSTAT (FAO, 2019). Mineral fertilizers mixes are based on the mean national consumption of N, P and K supplied by mineral fertilizers from the International Fertilizer Association (International Fertilizer Association, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_212]

---

### 1.2.2.2. Composition of organic fertilizers

In the national mixes of organic fertilizers, the fertilizers are 50% solid and 50% liquid in the case of cattle and pigs, and 100% solid for all the other animals (Nemecek et al., 2019). The total amount of N, P, K per mass of organic fertilizers that can be supplied to the agricultural perimeter is estimated with table 140 of the Agribalyse methodology (Koch and Salou, 2020). Contrarily to WFLDB methodology, the form of organic fertilizer provided by laying hens is assumed 100% solid, because composition data for laying hen slurry was not available in table 140 of Koch and Salou (2020). When a livestock species specified in FAO (2019), is not available in the organic fertilizers composition table of Koch and Salou (2020), an available process is associated depending on the form of the organic fertilizer and the type of animal, as shown in Table S. 10. [SOURCE: Page_212]

### 1.2.3. Models of emissions of the agricultural inputs

Table S. 11 presents the models chosen for this methodology that allow the spatiotemporal calculations of emissions to the air, soil, surface water and groundwater linked to mineral and organic fertilizers, seeds, fungicides, soil amendments as well as crop production. They are calculated as a function of the agricultural inputs, of the management of the agricultural plots (e.g. type of plow), and of the direct environment of the crop (i.e. climate and soil). [SOURCE: Page_213]

Table S. 11 Models used in this methodology for the calculations of emissions linked to agricultural inputs. [SOURCE: Page_213]

### 1.2.3.1. Amount of erosion

The Universal Soil Loss Equation (USLE) model in Eq.S.47 (Wischmeier and Smith, 1978) is one of the most frequently used models to calculate the annual amount of erosion over the agricultural territory. This model is also applicable on a large scale (Panagos et al., 2015), hence of interest for the Territorial-LCA framework. [SOURCE: Page_215]

𝑆𝑒𝑟 = 1000 ∗ 𝑅 ∗ 𝐾 ∗ 𝐿𝑆 ∗ 𝑐1 ∗ 𝑐2 ∗ 𝑃                                  (𝑆. 47) [SOURCE: Page_215]

With 𝑆𝑒𝑟 [kg.ha-1.yr-1] the amount of erosion; 𝑅 [MJ.mm.ha-1.h-1.yr-1] the erosivity factor; 𝐾 [t.h.MJ-1.mm-1] the erodibility factor; 𝐿𝑆 [-] the slope factor; 𝑐1 [-] the crop factor; 𝑐2 [-] the tillage factor; and 𝑃 [-] the practice factor. [SOURCE: Page_215]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5239198

- Summary: Section focusing on 1.2.3.1.1. erosivity factor : r

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 1.2.3.1.1. Erosivity factor : R

Equations in Table 2 of the LANCA method (Bos et al., 2016) are used to calculate the erosivity factor 𝑅, which represents the amount of rainfall kinetic energy potentially transferred to the ground. The equations in this method allow for the calculation of 𝑅 based on the climate zone of the study according to Köppen & Geiger (Kottek et al., 2006). Depending on the zone, it will be necessary to use the total annual precipitation, the height above sea level or the simple index of daily precipitation intensity. The latter is calculated with the following equation (CEN, 2021) : [SOURCE: Page_215]

𝑃𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑖𝑝𝑖𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 𝑆=                                                      (𝑆. 48) 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟𝑤𝑒𝑡 𝑑𝑎𝑦 [SOURCE: Page_215]

With 𝑃𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑖𝑝𝑖𝑡𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 [mm], the total amount of yearly precipitation; and 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟𝑤𝑒𝑡 𝑑𝑎𝑦 [-], the total amount of rainy days. [SOURCE: Page_215]

### 1.2.3.1.2. Erodibility factor : K

𝐾 can be either sourced from the erodibility factors for Europe in Table 5 of Panagos et al. (2014) or from the order of soil taxonomy in the USDA Soil Taxonomy System for locations outside Europe in Table 7-2 of Faist Emmenegger et al. (2009). For the latter, the Harmonized World Soil Database (HWSD) (Nachtergaele et al., 2009) can be used. However, the type of soil in the HWSD is in the FAO soil classification system and needs to be converted in the USDA Soil Taxonomy System, using for example the Encyclopedia Britannica (The Editors of Encyclopaedia Britannica, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_216]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5239198

- Summary: Section focusing on 1.2.3.1.3. slope factor : ls

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 1.2.3.1.3. Slope factor : LS

LS is calculated with equation 4 of Wischmeier and Smith (1978) : [SOURCE: Page_216]

𝛬 𝑚 𝐿𝑆 = (        ) ∗ (65.41 ∗ sin^2 𝜃 + 4.56 ∗ sin 𝜃 + 0.065)         (𝑆. 49) 72.6 [SOURCE: Page_216]

With 𝛬 (ft) the length of the slope taken as 30 m (Koch and Salou, 2020) ; 𝜃 is the angle of the slope taken as 2 % (Koch and Salou, 2020) ; and 𝑚 a parameter of Wischmeier and Smith (1978) that depend on the angle of the slope, and which is taken as 0.3. [SOURCE: Page_216]

### 1.2.3.1.4. Crop and tillage factor: c1 and c2

𝑐1 can be sourced from Table 1 of Panagos et al. (2015) for a variety of crops (i.e. common wheat and spelt, durum wheat, rye, barley, grain maize – corn, rice, dried pulses (legumes) and protein crop, potatoes, sugar beet, oilseeds, rape and turnip rape, sunflower seed, linseed, soya, cotton seed, tobacco and fallow lands). 𝑐2 can be sourced from Table 7-4 of Faist Emmenegger et al. (2009). [SOURCE: Page_216]

### 1.2.3.1.5. Practice factor P

𝑃 is sourced from Table 7-5 of Faist Emmenegger et al. (2009). In this methodology, 𝑃 takes the value for contour farming (i.e. 0.5) as in Agribalyse (Koch and Salou, 2020). [SOURCE: Page_216]

### 1.2.3.2.1. NH3 from mineral fertilizers

The following equation from Nemecek et al. (2019) is used to calculate the emissions linked to mineral fertilizers. [SOURCE: Page_216]

𝑀 17 𝑁𝐻3 𝑚𝑖𝑛 =    ∗ ∑ (𝐸𝐹𝑎 ∗ 𝑝 + 𝐸𝐹𝑏 ∗ (1 − 𝑝)) ∗ 𝑁𝑚𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑙             (𝑆. 50) 14 𝑚=1 [SOURCE: Page_216]

208 [SOURCE: Page_216]

Where 𝑁𝐻3 𝑚𝑖𝑛 [kg NH$_3$.yr-1.ha-1] is the amount of ammonia emitted due to mineral fertilizers; 𝐸𝐹𝑎 [kg N-NH$_3$.kg N-1] the emission factor on soils with pH ≤ 7 from table 3.2 in EMEP/EEA (2016); 𝑝 [-] the fraction of soils with pH ≤ 7 found in the HWSD (Nachtergaele et al., 2009); 𝐸𝐹𝑏 [kg N-NH$_3$.kg N-1] the emission factor on soils with pH > 7 from table 3.2 in EMEP/EEA (2016); 𝑁𝑚𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑙 [kg.yr-1.ha-1] the amount of nitrogen supplied by the mineral fertilizers. [SOURCE: Page_217]

The pH of the soil was evaluated with the mean of the soil horizons pH of the HWSD (Nachtergaele et al., 2009). 𝐸𝐹𝑎 and 𝐸𝐹𝑏 depend on the following temperature thresholds Dong et al. (2006): [SOURCE: Page_217]

-     Cool (T ≤ 14 °C) -     Temperate (15°C < T ≤ 25 °C) -     Warm (T > 25 °C) [SOURCE: Page_217]

### 1.2.3.2.2. NH3 from organic fertilizers

The NH$_3$ emissions from organic fertilizers are calculated with the following equation (Koch and Salou, 2020): [SOURCE: Page_217]

17 𝑁𝐻3 𝑜𝑟𝑔 =      ∗ 𝑁𝑜𝑟𝑔 ∗ 𝑇𝐴𝑁 ∗ 𝐸𝐹                              (𝑆. 51) 14 [SOURCE: Page_217]

Where 𝑁𝐻3 𝑜𝑟𝑔 [kg NH$_3$.yr-1.ha-1] is the amount of ammonia emitted due to organic fertilizers; 𝑁𝑜𝑟𝑔 [t.yr-1.ha-1] is the mass of organic fertilizers ; 𝑇𝐴𝑁 [kg N-NH$_4$+.t-1] the Total amount of Ammoniacal Nitrogen (TAN) in the organic fertilizers of table 140 of Koch and Salou (2020); 𝐸𝐹 [kg N-NH$_3$.(kg N-NH$_4$+)-1] is the emission factor of ammonia from organic fertilizers sourced from table 3-8 of EMEP/EEA (2009). [SOURCE: Page_217]

Available processes in EMEP/EEA (2009) were associated to the livestock species of the FAO (2019). [SOURCE: Page_217]

### 1.2.3.3. Emissions of NOx

The Eq. S.52 is used to evaluate the NOx emissions linked to organic and mineral fertilizers. NOx emissions from fertilizers are low compared to other agricultural activities such as mechanical work (Koch and Salou, 2020). Therefore, the hypothesis that all NOx are NO$_2$ is made. [SOURCE: Page_218]

46 𝑁𝑂𝑥 =      ∗ 𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 ∗ 𝐸𝐹                                    (𝑆. 52) 14 [SOURCE: Page_218]

Where 𝑁𝑂𝑥 [kg NO$_2$.yr-1.ha-1] is the amount of emitted NOx; 𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 [kg N.yr-1.ha-1] in the fertilizers without the quantity of nitrogen evaporated in the form of NH$_3$; and 𝐸𝐹 [kg N-NO$_2$.kg N-1] the emission factor of NO$_2$ calculated in the WFLDB (Nemecek et al., 2019) based on emission factors of NO in EMEP/EEA (2016). [SOURCE: Page_218]

### 1.2.3.4. Leaching of NO3-

The amount of NO$_3$- leaching to groundwater is calculated with the SQCB-NO$_3$ model (Faist Emmenegger et al., 2009) : [SOURCE: Page_218]

62             𝑃 𝑁𝑂3− =        ∗ (21.37 +     ∗ [0.0037 ∗ 𝑆 + 0.0000601 ∗ 𝑁𝑠𝑜𝑚 − 0.00362 ∗ 𝑈])              (𝑆. 53) 14            𝑐∗𝐿 [SOURCE: Page_218]

Where 𝑁𝑂3− [kg NO$_3$-.yr-1.ha-1] is the amount of nitrate leaching to groundwater; 𝑃 (mm.yr-1) is the sum of yearly precipitations and yearly water for irrigation; 𝑐 is the mean clay content of the soil horizons [%], which is calculated with data from the HWSD (Nachtergaele et al., 2009); 𝐿 [m] is the rooting depth of the crop, which is sourced from the AquaCrop original crop file (Vanuytrecht et al., 2014a); 𝑆 [kg N.yr-1.ha-1] the amount of nitrogen supplied through fertilizer without the quantity of nitrogen evaporated in the form of NH$_3$ and NOx; 𝑁𝑠𝑜𝑚 (kg N.ha-1) the [SOURCE: Page_218]

210 [SOURCE: Page_218]

### 1.2.3.9. Heavy metals emission

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

### 1.2.3.9.1. Allocation factor of heavy metals emissions

Only a part of the heavy metals emitted by the studied territory should be allocated to the farmer because the farmer is only responsible for the emissions linked to agricultural inputs and not for the deposition from the air. Indeed, the latter is due to other economic activities. Therefore, the following equation from the SALCA-HEAVY METAL model is used to calculate the allocation factor for the emission of a heavy metal (i.e. Cadmium, Copper, Zinc, Lead, Nickel, Chromium, Mercury) (Freiermuth, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_221]

𝑀𝑎𝑔𝑟𝑜𝑖 𝐴𝑖 =                                                      (𝑆. 62) 𝑀𝑎𝑔𝑟𝑜𝑖 + 𝑀𝑑𝑒𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_221]

Where 𝑖 [-] is the indice for the type of heavy metal; 𝐴 [-] is the allocation factor for the emission of the heavy metal; 𝑀𝑑𝑒𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the total input of heavy metal from atmospheric deposition sourced from Table 75 of Koch and Salou (2020); 𝑀𝑎𝑔𝑟𝑜 [mg.ha-1.year- 1 ] is the total input of heavy metal from agricultural production (i.e from mineral and organic fertilizers, seeds as well as pesticides). For mineral fertilizers it is sourced from Table 27 of Nemecek et al. (2019). For organic fertilizers it is sourced from Table 72 of Koch and Salou (2020). For seeds, it is sourced from Table 26 of Nemecek et al. (2019). For zinc or copper based pesticides, it is sourced from Table 73 of Koch and Salou (2020). [SOURCE: Page_221]

### 1.2.3.9.2. Heavy metals emission leaching to groundwater

The following equation from the SALCA-HEAVY METAL model is used to calculate the leaching of a heavy metal (Freiermuth, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_221]

𝐻𝑀𝑙𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ𝑖 = 𝑚𝑙𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ𝑖 ∗ 𝐴𝑖                                 (𝑆. 63) [SOURCE: Page_221]

Where 𝐴𝑖 is calculated with Eq. S.62; 𝐻𝑀𝑙𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the amount of leaching heavy metal; and 𝑚𝑙𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the average amount of heavy metal emission by the soil, sourced from Table 76 of Koch and Salou (2020). [SOURCE: Page_221]

### 1.2.3.9.3. Heavy metals emission running off to river

The following equation from the SALCA-HEAVY METAL model is used to calculate the runoff of a heavy metal (Freiermuth, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_221]

𝐻𝑀𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑖 = 𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑖 ∗ 𝑆𝑒𝑟 ∗ 𝑎 ∗ 𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 ∗ 𝐴𝑖                     (𝑆. 64) [SOURCE: Page_221]

Where 𝐻𝑀𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the amount of heavy metal that runs off; 𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑖 [mg.(mg of soil)-1], the average amount of heavy metal in the soil, which depend on the type of crops and [SOURCE: Page_221]

213 [SOURCE: Page_221]

is sourced from Table 24 of Nemecek et al. (2019); 𝑎 [-] is an accumulation factor, taken as 1.86 (Nemecek et al., 2019); and 𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 [-] is an erosion factor considering the distance to river or lakes, taken as 0.2 (Nemecek et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_222]

### 1.2.3.9.4. Heavy metals emission in agricultural soils

The following equation from the SALCA-HEAVY METAL model is used to calculate emission of a heavy metal in the agricultural soil (Freiermuth, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_222]

𝐻𝑀𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙𝑖 = (∑ 𝑖𝑛𝑝𝑢𝑡𝑠𝑖 − ∑ 𝑜𝑢𝑡𝑝𝑢𝑡𝑠𝑖 ) ∗ 𝐴𝑖                                  (𝑆. 65) [SOURCE: Page_222]

Where 𝐻𝑀𝑠𝑜𝑖𝑙 [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the amount of heavy metal emitted to the agricultural soil; ∑ 𝑖𝑛𝑝𝑢𝑡𝑠 [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the total input of heavy metal on the agricultural perimeter (i.e. fertilizer, seeds, pesticides and air deposition); ∑ 𝑜𝑢𝑡𝑝𝑢𝑡𝑠 [mg.ha-1.year-1] is the total output of heavy metal of the agricultural perimeter (i.e. plant material uptake, runoff, and leaching). The uptake of the plant material is sourced from Table 26 of Nemecek et al. (2019). [SOURCE: Page_222]
