### 1.2.3.10. CO2 emission linked to urea, limestone and dolomite

The emissions of CO$_2$ linked to the use of urea, limestone and dolomite are evaluated with the following equation (IPCC, 2006a). [SOURCE: Page_222]

𝐶𝑂2𝑖 = 𝐸𝐹𝑖 ∗ 𝑀𝑖                                           (𝑆. 66) [SOURCE: Page_222]

Where 𝑖 [-] is the type of input (i.e urea, limestone or dolomite); 𝐶𝑂2 [kg CO$_2$.ha-1.yr-1] is the quantity 𝐸𝐹 [kg CO$_2$.(kg input)-1] is the emission factor of CO$_2$ from the type of input, either 1.57 for Urea-N, 0.44 for limestone and 0.48 for dolomite (Nemecek et al., 2019); and 𝑀 [kg input.ha- 1 .yr-1] is the quantity of input. [SOURCE: Page_222]

### 2. Illustrative case study

Locations of the case study [SOURCE: Page_222]

These locations were also chosen because they are renowned agricultural areas (U.S. Department of Agriculture - National Agricultural Statistics Service, 2019) and because they are located in a 100 km radius of a river or a reservoir, the maximum length of the IBWT in our case. The six contrasted locations are listed in Table S. 13. [SOURCE: Page_222]

Table S. 13 Six contrasted locations on which the prospective and regionalized methodology for creating LCI of agricultural perimeters is tested [SOURCE: Page_222]

Figure S. 4 and Figure S. 5 represent the annual mean temperature and the annual amount of precipitation of the six contrasted locations respectively. To facilitate the reading of these graphs, they are smoothed with an eleven years moving average, i.e. the mean is performed over the time period (year − 5) to (year + 5). [SOURCE: Page_223]

For the annual mean temperature, all of the locations will see an increase. For the annual amount of precipitation it is more contrasted, i.e. it can increase (e.g. China), stay more or less stable (e.g. Australia) or decrease (e.g. France). [SOURCE: Page_223]

Only dominant soil data of the HWSD (Nachtergaele et al., 2009) were considered, except in the case of France and Australia. For the former, the dominant soil data, which occupies 70% of the Soil Mapping Unit (SMU), were only available for a single horizon of 30 cm depth. Therefore, the associated soil data, which occupies 30% of the SMU, were selected. For the second, there were two dominant soils both occupying 30% of the SMU. Therefore, we mixed the top soil of the first dominant soil with the subsoil of the second dominant. [SOURCE: Page_224]

Agricultural management data [SOURCE: Page_225]

Irrigation with sprinkler was chosen for maize and wheat. For maize, irrigation is not allowed during the flowering of the crop because of the risk of fusarium disease (BRL, 2019). Therefore, irrigation is permitted from the sowing until the start of the flowering stage because the leaf expansive growth of maize is very sensitive to water stress (Steduto et al., 2012). Irrigation is also allowed during grain filling, i.e. from the end of the flowering stage until the harvest, because it is profitable (BRL, 2019). For wheat, inconsistent results were achieved by studies trying to understand what development stages are the most sensitive to water stresses (Steduto et al., 2012). However, as yield is related to the number of grain per unit of area, i.e. allowed by adequate vegetative development, and to the mass of the grains, i.e. allowed by adequate grain filling (Steduto et al., 2012), the same irrigation period as for maize was chosen. [SOURCE: Page_225]

LCA databases [SOURCE: Page_227]

Table S. 20 presents the databases, i.e. Ecoinvent, allocation – cut-off by classification version 3.7.1 (Wernet et al., 2016) and WFLDB version 3.5 (Nemecek et al., 2019), used for the construction of the prospective and regionalized LCI of this case study. This choice of database limits inconsistency because WFLDB and Ecoinvent use the same background processes. [SOURCE: Page_227]

Table S. 20 Databases used for the construction of the LCI [SOURCE: Page_227]

Part of the LCI                                       Database used for the processes Irrigation structure                                  Ecoinvent version 3.7.1 Electricity mix                                       Ecoinvent version 3.7.1 Seeds                                                 WFLDB version 3.5 Pesticides and emissions from pesticides              WFLDB version 3.5 Mechanical works                                      WFLDB version 3.5 Mineral fertilizers                                   WFLDB version 3.5 and Ecoinvent version 3.7.1 Organic fertilizers                                   Ecoinvent version 3.7.1 Soil amendments                                       Ecoinvent version 3.7.1 For the irrigation structure, the inventory is provided by Rogy et al. (2022). For the electricity mixes, they were selected at a regional scale when available (i.e. for China, India and the USA) and at the national scale for the remaining locations. For seeds, pesticides and their emissions, as well as the different mechanical works, they are assumed to be the same in each location. [SOURCE: Page_227]

Therefore, their inventory data are extracted from same country in the WFLDB, i.e. Maize grain, irrigated, at farm (WFLDB)/US U and Wheat grain, irrigated, at farm (WFLDB)/US U. For mineral fertilizers, either the WFLDB or Ecoinvent were used depending on the spatial resolution adequacy of the process selected. [SOURCE: Page_227]

### 1. Water balance model of the AR

Figure S. 19 presents the water balance model of the AR. [SOURCE: Page_244]

Figure S. 19 Operation of the Agricultural Reservoir [SOURCE: Page_244]

The water balance of Eq. S.67 is performed daily. [SOURCE: Page_244]

𝑑𝑉 = 𝑃(𝑡) + 𝐹(𝑡) − 𝐸(𝑡) − 𝐼(𝑡) − 𝐸𝑥𝑐(𝑡)                        (𝑆. 67) 𝑑𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_244]

With 𝑡 [day] the timestep; 𝑉 [m^3] the amount of water in the AR; 𝑃 [m^3.day-1] the amount of precipitations; 𝐹 [m^3.day-1] the amount of water filling the AR; 𝐸 [m^3.day-1] the amount of water evaporated from AR surface; 𝐼 [m^3.day-1] the amount of water used for irrigation; 𝐸𝑥𝑐 [m^3.day- 1 ] the excess water in the reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_244]

Eq. S.68. is solved numerically with Euler’s method: [SOURCE: Page_244]

𝑉(𝑡 + 1) = 𝑉(𝑡) + ∆𝑡 ∗ (𝑃(𝑡) + 𝐹(𝑡) − 𝐸(𝑡) − 𝐼(𝑡) − 𝐸𝑥𝑐(𝑡))              (𝑆. 68) [SOURCE: Page_244]

Eq.S.69 is solved daily, hence ∆𝑡 = 1 day. [SOURCE: Page_244]

𝑉(𝑡 + 1) = 𝑉(𝑡) + 𝑃(𝑡) + 𝐹(𝑡) − 𝐸(𝑡) − 𝐼(𝑡) − 𝐸𝑥𝑐(𝑡)                 (𝑆. 69) [SOURCE: Page_244]

Calculation of F(t) [SOURCE: Page_244]

𝐹(𝑡) is calculated based on the water available for human activities in the watershed between October and May, while taking into account the emptiness of the AR, i.e. 𝐸𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠 [m^3.day- 1 ] and the maximum filling rate of the AR, 𝐹𝑚𝑎𝑥 [m^3.day-1]. [SOURCE: Page_244]

236 [SOURCE: Page_244]

First, the amount of water available for human activities, i.e. 𝐴 [m^3.day-1], is calculated with Eq.S.70. If there is no water available, then the reservoir is not filled. [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝐴(𝑡) = 𝑅𝑂(𝑡) − 𝐸𝐹𝑅(𝑡)                               (𝑆. 70) [SOURCE: Page_245]

With 𝑅𝑂 [m^3.day-1], the runoff at the outlet of the catchment; 𝐸𝐹𝑅 [m^3.day-1] is the Environmental Flow Requirements from (Pastor et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_245]

Second, the maximum amount of waterflow available filling the AR, i.e. 𝑚𝑎𝑥𝐹 [m^3.day-1], is calculated with the following equations. [SOURCE: Page_245]

If: [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝐴(𝑡) < 0                                    (𝑆. 71) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Then: [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝑚𝑎𝑥𝐹 (𝑡) = 0                                 (𝑆. 72) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Elseif: 𝐴(𝑡) ≥ 𝐹𝑚𝑎𝑥                                  (𝑆. 73) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Then: 𝑚𝑎𝑥𝐹 (𝑡) = 𝐹𝑚𝑎𝑥                                 (𝑆. 74) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Else: [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝑚𝑎𝑥𝐹 (𝑡) = 𝐴(𝑡)                                (𝑆. 75) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Then the filling of the AR can be calculated: [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝐹(𝑡) = 𝑚𝑎𝑥𝐹 (𝑡)                                 (𝑆. 76) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Calculation of Exc(t) [SOURCE: Page_245]

The amount of emptiness in the AR is estimated with Eq.S.77 with the maximum amount of water that can be stored in the AR, i.e. 𝑀𝑎𝑥𝑉 [m^3]. [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝐸𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠(𝑡) = 𝑀𝑎𝑥𝑉 + 𝐼(𝑡) + 𝐸(𝑡) − 𝑉(𝑡) − 𝑃(𝑡) − 𝐹(𝑡)                (𝑆. 77) [SOURCE: Page_245]

If: 𝐸𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠 ≤ 0                                 (𝑆. 78) [SOURCE: Page_245]

Then: [SOURCE: Page_245]

𝐸𝑥𝑐(𝑡) = 𝐸𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑛𝑒𝑠𝑠                                (𝑆. 79) [SOURCE: Page_245]

237 [SOURCE: Page_245]

Else: [SOURCE: Page_246]

𝐸𝑥𝑐(𝑡) = 0                                      (𝑆. 80) [SOURCE: Page_246]

Calculation of P(t) [SOURCE: Page_246]

The amount of precipitation that is filling the Agricultural Reservoir are the precipitations that falls directly on it. [SOURCE: Page_246]

Calculation of E(t) [SOURCE: Page_246]

𝐸(𝑡) is calculated based on 𝐸𝑇0 [m^3.day-1], the reference evapotranspiration and 𝜂 a coefficient equal to 0.6 (Lardy et al., 2016). 𝐸(𝑡) only take place when there is water in the AR. [SOURCE: Page_246]

𝐸(𝑡) = 𝐸𝑇0(𝑡) ∗ 𝜂                                   (𝑆. 81) [SOURCE: Page_246]

Calculation of I(t) [SOURCE: Page_246]

Irrigation is triggered by AquaCrop at the maximum evapotranspiration level of the crop, when all the Readily Available Water (RAW) in the soil is depleted. It is also triggered when there is not enough water in the soil for germination. If the demand of crop for irrigation water can not be fully satisfied because there is not enough water in the AR, then the irrigation do not take place. [SOURCE: Page_246]

The response to mixing factors follow the 2nd order model of Eq. S.82 (Czitrom, 1989). Eq. S.82 does not have an intercept because the sum of each share of crop occupying the agricultural perimeter is 1. The response to the process factor follows the first order model of Eq. S.83 (Lawson, 2015). Both Eq. S.82 and S.83 are combined in Eq. S.84 to define the 3rd order model of Eq. S.85 representing the response to the combined mixing and process factors (Lawson, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_263]

4            3      4 [SOURCE: Page_263]

𝑓(𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ) = ∑ 𝑏𝑖 𝑥𝑖 + ∑ ∑ 𝑏𝑖𝑗 𝑥𝑖 𝑥𝑗                     (𝑆. 82) 𝑖=1            𝑖=1 𝑗=𝑖+1 [SOURCE: Page_263]

With 𝑓, the response of eco-efficiencies to mixing factors; 𝑏, the coefficients of the model; 𝑥, the mixture factors. [SOURCE: Page_263]

𝑔(𝑧) = 𝑎0 + 𝑎1 ∗ 𝑧1                                (𝑆. 83) [SOURCE: Page_263]

With 𝑔(𝑧), the response to the process factor; 𝑎, the coefficients of the model; 𝑧1 , is the process factor. [SOURCE: Page_263]

The response to both process and mixing factors is ℎ: [SOURCE: Page_263]

ℎ(𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 , 𝑧1 ) = 𝑓(𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3 , 𝑥4 ) ∗ 𝑔(𝑧1 )        (𝑆. 84) [SOURCE: Page_263]

Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_263]
